Diffuse Interface Energies with Microscopic Heterogeneities I: Homogenization
Peter S. Morfe and Christian Wagner

Abstract
. We analyze Allen-Cahn functionals with stationary ergodic coefficients in the regime where the length scale δ \delta of the heterogeneities is much smaller (microscopic) than the interface width ϵ \epsilon (mesoscopic). In the main result of this paper, we prove that if the ratio δ ​ ϵ − 1 \delta\epsilon^{-1} decays fast enough compared to ϵ \epsilon , then homogenization effects dominate, and the Γ \Gamma -limit of the energy is the same as if the coefficients had been replaced by their homogenized values. As a byproduct of the proof, this implies that homogenization holds in the periodic setting whenever δ ​ ϵ − 1 \delta\epsilon^{-1} vanishes with ϵ \epsilon , no matter how slowly. In a companion paper, we prove this is sharp: if δ ​ ϵ − 1 \delta\epsilon^{-1} decays too slowly, then improbable or atypical local configurations of the medium begin to play a role, and the Γ \Gamma -limit may be smaller than the one predicted by homogenization theory. We refer to this as the rare events regime, and we prove that it can occur in both random and almost periodic media.



1.
Introduction

In this paper, we analyze the effect of microscopic heterogeneities on the macroscopic behavior of (mesoscopic) diffuse interfaces. Specifically, we consider the
\Gamma
-limit of the following Allen-Cahn-type energy functional with diffuse interface width
\epsilon
and heterogeneity length scale
\delta
in the regime
\delta\ll\epsilon
:

(1)

\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}(u;U)=\int_{U}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}a(\delta^{-1}x)\nabla u\cdot\nabla u+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\theta(\delta^{-1}x)W(u)\right)\,dx.

Here
a
is a uniformly elliptic matrix field and
\theta
, a positive function, both of which are taken to be samples of stationary, ergodic random fields. The nonlinearity
W
is a double-well potential with minima at
1
and
-1
, the prototypical choice being
W(u)=(1-u^{2})^{2}
.

Energies of the form (
1
) appear in materials science, where they provide a phenomenological, mesoscopic-scale description of phase transitions. In our context, since
W
has two minima, the material is composed of two phases. The so-called phase field
u
is a function taking values in some compact interval, say,
[-1,1]
, defined in such a way that the set
\{u\approx 1\}
corresponds to the bulk of the first phase,
\{u\approx-1\}
corresponds to the bulk of the second phase, and the remainder of space is understood to be the transition region. For more information on diffuse interface modeling, we refer the interested reader to the books
[
32
,
37
,
52
]
and the article
[
44
]
.

When the underlying material is spatially homogeneous, the coefficients
a
and
\theta
are constant, and then classical results of Modica and Mortola
[
46
,
47
]
imply that the energy
\Gamma
-converges in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
, in such a way that the limiting phase field
u
takes values in
\{-1,1\}
and the limiting energy is proportional to the surface area of the interface
\partial\{u=1\}
. If
a
and
\theta
are stationary ergodic fields and the length scales
\delta
and
\epsilon
are commensurate in the sense that
\delta\epsilon^{-1}\sim c
for some positive
c>0
, it is known that
\Gamma
-convergence still holds, the only difference being that the limiting surface energy is anisotropic; this was proved by Ansini, Braides, and Chiadò-Piat
[
2
]
and Cristoferi, Fonseca, Hagerty, and Popovici
[
19
]
in periodic media and the first author
[
49
]
and Marziani
[
45
]
in the stationary ergodic setting. In this work, we tackle the case when
\epsilon^{-1}\delta\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

Since
\delta\ll\epsilon
, it is natural to expect that the homogenization (
\delta\to 0
) limit takes precedence over the sharp interface (
\epsilon\to 0
) limit. This leads to the guess that the limiting sharp interface energy functional ought to have the form

(2)

\bar{\mathscr{E}}(u;U)=\int_{\partial\{u=1\}\cap U}\bar{\sigma}(\nu_{\{u=1\}})\,d\mathcal{H}^{d-1},\quad{\rm where}\quad\bar{\sigma}(\nu)^{2}=\sigma_{W}^{2}\bar{\theta}\nu\cdot\bar{a}\nu.

Above
\bar{a}
is the homogenized matrix associated with the gradient term in (
1
),
\bar{\theta}
is the expected value of
\theta
, and
\sigma_{W}
is a constant determined by
W
(see formula (
21
) below).

Indeed, we prove in the appendix that, in general, the homogenized energy
\bar{\mathscr{E}}
always serves as an upper bound:

(3)

\displaystyle\Gamma\text{-}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;U)\leq\bar{\mathscr{E}}(u;U);

see Appendix
A
for the proof. In the main result of the paper, we prove that, as long as the ratio
\epsilon^{-1}\delta
vanishes fast enough, the energy
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}

\Gamma
-converges to
\bar{\mathscr{E}}
as expected. This generalizes previous results of Ansini, Braides, and Chiadò-Piat
[
2
]
, Hagerty
[
39
]
, and Cristoferi, Fonseca, and Ganedi
[
18
]
in the periodic setting. We refer to this as the
homogenization regime
.

At the same time, if the ratio
\epsilon^{-1}\delta
vanishes more slowly, it is possible that the
\Gamma
-limit of
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}
is strictly smaller than
\bar{\mathscr{E}}
. We refer to this as the
rare events regime
, and it is the subject of the companion paper
[
48
]
, which treats both random and almost periodic counterexamples. We use the term “rare events” because, in this regime, improbable or atypical configurations of the medium become relevant. This reflects the competition between averaging and energy minimization inherent in the problem: the former drives the energy towards the typical behavior of the coefficients
a
and
\theta
, while the latter seeks to exploit favorable deviations from the mean.

1.1.
Assumptions

We assume that there is a complete probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
supporting a measurable action
\{\tau_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that, for any
(x,\omega)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\Omega
,

a(x,\omega)=A(\tau_{x}\omega),\quad\theta(x,\omega)=\Theta(\tau_{x}\omega).

Here
A
is a symmetric matrix-valued random variable and
\Theta
, a positive random variable.

Concerning
A
, we assume uniform ellipticity, that is, there are constants
\lambda,\Lambda>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\{\lambda\text{Id}\leq A\leq\Lambda\text{Id}\}=1.

Similarly, we assume that
\Theta
is almost surely bounded above and below by positive constants
\theta_{*},\theta^{*}>0
:

\mathbb{P}\{\theta_{*}\leq\Theta\leq\theta^{*}\}=1.

The action
\{\tau_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is assumed to be a bonafide group action, that is,

\tau_{0}=\text{Id},\quad\tau_{x+y}=\tau_{x}\circ\tau_{y}\quad\text{for each}\quad x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We also assume that the map
(\omega,x)\mapsto\tau_{x}\omega
is measurable with respect to the product
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, where
\mathscr{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is the Borel
\sigma
-algebra of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Finally, the action is assumed to be both stationary and ergodic. Thus, for any event
E\in\mathcal{F}
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\mathbb{P}(\tau_{x}^{-1}(E))=\mathbb{P}(E),

and if
E\in\mathcal{F}
satisfies the following invariance assumption

\tau_{x}^{-1}(E)=E\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

then
\mathbb{P}(E)\in\{0,1\}
.

Remark 1
.

As is well-known, the above assumptions are general enough to include the case of periodic and almost periodic media, see, for instance, the discussion in
[
51
]
. (However, care needs to be taken when interpreting probabilistic statements.)

Note that by Fubini’s Theorem and stationarity, the assumptions on
A
and
\Theta
above imply that, on an event of probability one,

\displaystyle\lambda\text{Id}\leq a(y)\leq\Lambda\text{Id}\quad\text{and}\quad\theta_{*}\leq\theta(y)\leq\theta^{*}\quad\text{for a.e.}\,\,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

We will restrict to this event in what follows.

Finally, here are the assumptions on the potential
W:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
. First, we assume that
W
is continuous and additionally

\displaystyle W\,\,\text{is}\,\,C^{2}\,\,\text{in a neighborhood of}\,\,[-1,1],

\displaystyle\{u\in\mathbb{R}\,\mid\,W(u)=0\}=\{-1,1\}.

Following
[
2
]
, we assume the following growth condition on W at infinity

(4)

\displaystyle\liminf_{|u|\to\infty}|u|^{-p}W(u)>0\quad{\rm for~some~}p\geq 2,

This technical assumption ensures that only functions taking values in the interval
[-1,1]
contribute to the
\Gamma
-limit.

In addition, we make a weak nondegeneracy assumption at the minima
-1
and
1
. Namely, we assume there is a
\kappa\in\mathbb{N}
such that
W
is differentiable to order
2\kappa
at
-1
and
1
and, furthermore,

(5)

\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}W^{(2k)}(-1)=W^{(2k)}(1)=0\quad\text{for each}\quad k\in\{0,1,\dots,\kappa-1\},\\
\min\{W^{(2\kappa)}(-1),W^{(2\kappa)}(1)\}>0.\end{array}\right.

This assumption is typically made in the PDE literature with
\kappa=1
. Of course, all these assumptions are satisfied by the standard choice
W(u)=(1-u^{2})^{2}
.

Remark 2
.

In several places in this paper, we will significantly weaken the assumptions on
W
above. In Theorem
2
, proved in Appendix
A
below, we only need to assume that
W
is a nonnegative continuous function such that
\{u\in\mathbb{R}\,\mid\,W(u)=0\}=\{-1,1\}
. Similarly, in Theorem
4
, which is proved in Appendix
B
, we operate under this same assumption and, in addition, require that the (super)quadratic growth assumption (
4
) holds.

Throughout the paper,
\delta
is microscopic, while
\epsilon
is mesoscopic, meaning that

\displaystyle\frac{\delta(\epsilon)}{\epsilon}\rightarrow 0\quad{\rm as}\quad\epsilon\rightarrow 0.

This is a standing assumption; we will usually not restate it later.

\frac{\delta}{\epsilon}
hom. regime
rare events regime
\sigma_{W}\sqrt{\bar{\theta}\bar{a}}

\sigma_{W}\sqrt{\theta_{*}\lambda}

Figure 1.
A depiction of the transition between the homogenization and rare events regimes: Our main theorem shows that if
\delta\epsilon^{-1}
vanishes rapidly enough, then the relevant effective interfacial energy density is
\sigma_{W}\sqrt{\bar{\theta}\bar{a}}
. The examples in
[
48
]
show that if
\delta\epsilon^{-1}
vanishes slowly enough, the minimum values
\lambda
and
\theta_{*}
may instead determine the macroscopic energy. As suggested in the figure, we expect that intermediate values between
\sqrt{\bar{\theta}\bar{a}}
and
\sqrt{\theta_{*}\lambda}
are generically also relevant. In the case of the 1D random checkerboard (with
a\equiv 1
), it is possible to make this diagram precise, see
[
48
, Theorem 1 & Figure 1]
.

1.2.
Homogenization Regime

Before stating the main result, recall the notion of gradient correctors
\phi
and flux correctors
\sigma
from the homogenization theory of elliptic operators in divergence form. For any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\phi_{\xi}
and
\sigma_{\xi}
are random fields with stationary, mean-zero gradients such that

(6)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot(a(x)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{\xi}))=0,\quad-\Delta\sigma_{\xi}=\nabla\times q_{\xi}(x)\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d},

where
q_{\xi}\coloneqq a(x)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{\xi})
and
(\nabla\times q)_{jk}\coloneqq\partial_{j}q_{k}-\partial_{k}q_{j}
. Here
\phi_{\xi}
is a scalar field and
\sigma_{\xi}
, a matrix field, both of which are unique up to additive constants. Following the convention of Gloria, Neukamm, and Otto
[
38
]
, we write
\phi
and
\sigma
for the vector field and
3
-tensor given by
\phi=(\phi_{e_{1}},\dots,\phi_{e_{d}})
and
\sigma=(\sigma_{e_{1}},\dots,\sigma_{e_{d}})
, where
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
is the standard orthonormal basis.

In recent work in the field, the quantification of the sublinear growth of
\phi
and
\sigma
plays a recurring role, particularly as it pertains to estimating the rate of homogenization. Toward that end, we define the stationary field
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
by

(7)

{\rm Sub}_{x}(r)\coloneqq\sup_{R\geq r}\frac{1}{R}\left(\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}|(\phi,\sigma)-\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}(\phi,\sigma)|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

This field is closely related to the so-called minimal radius of
[
38
]
, which is a key ingredient in the large-scale regularity theory developed therein. Specifically, if we define the minimal radius
r_{*}(\nu)
for a given
\nu>0
by

(8)

\displaystyle r_{*}(\nu)=\inf\Big\{~r>0~\Big|~\frac{1}{R}\Big(\fint_{Q_{R}}\Big|(\phi,\sigma)-\fint_{Q_{R}}(\phi,\sigma)\Big|^{2}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq\nu\quad\text{for each}\,\,R\geq r~\Big\},

then

\displaystyle r_{*}(\nu)>r\quad\text{if and only if}\,\,{\rm Sub}_{0}(r)>\nu.

We will show below that the rate of sublinear growth of the correctors also plays an important role in the present work.

Next, we track the oscillations of
\theta
measured in the
H^{-1}
norm, more precisely,

(9)

{\rm Osc}_{x}(r)\coloneqq\sup_{R\geq r}\frac{1}{R}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{R}(x))},

where we recall that
\bar{\theta}=\mathbb{E}[\theta(0)]
. Here we use a suitably scaled
H^{-1}
norm; see (
17
) below for the definition.

In the statement of our results, it will be convenient to note that there is a metric
d_{\Gamma}
on a suitable space of lower semicontinuous functionals in
L^{1}
such that, for any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any sequences
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
, we have that

\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}}(\cdot\,;U)\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\bar{\mathscr{E}}(\cdot\,;U)\quad\text{if and only if}\quad d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}}(\cdot\,;U),\bar{\mathscr{E}}(\cdot\,;U))\to 0,

see Appendix
B
for a review of the relevant details.

Theorem 1
.

Assume that
a
,
\theta
, and
W
satisfy the assumptions of Section
1.1
. If the microscopic length scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
is chosen in such a way that, for any
\nu>0

(10)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{d}}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}=0,

(11)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{1}{\epsilon^{d}}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Osc}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}=0,

then
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\bar{\mathscr{E}}
in probability. More precisely, for any
\nu>0
and any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}\{d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(\cdot\,;U),\bar{\mathscr{E}}(\cdot\,;U))>\nu\}=0.

In Appendix
B
, we reduce the proof of the above theorem to proving convergence of the energy in an auxiliary family of cell problems, c.f. Theorem
4
. This is the same reduction previously employed in
[
2
,
19
,
49
,
45
]
, which will be explained in greater detail in Section
2
. After some preparation, most notably Section
3
, we will prove the convergence of the energy in these cell problems in Section
4.3
, c.f. Theorem
3
.

Estimates on the probability
\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(R)>\nu\}
can be found, for instance, in the previously mentioned work
[
38
]
and the recent book
[
4
]
of Armstrong and Kuusi under the assumption that the law of
a
satisfies certain mixing conditions. These can be used to derive concrete sufficient conditions ensuring that assumption (
10
) holds, see Section
4.6
for the case of the random checkerboard. We expect that similar arguments can be used to quantify
\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Osc}_{0}(R)>\nu\}
(see also Section
4.7
).

In general, even in the setting of qualitative homogenization (i.e., without imposing mixing conditions), the next result shows that the previous theorem is never vacuous: there is always a choice of scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
satisfying (
10
) and (
11
).

Proposition 1
.

Given any stationary ergodic medium
(a,\theta)
as in Section
1.1
, there is a choice of scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta_{*}(\epsilon)
such that if
\delta(\epsilon)\leq\delta_{*}(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon
close to zero, then the scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
satisfies both (
10
) and (
11
).

The proof of Proposition
1
is located in Section
4.5
.

It is possible to upgrade from convergence in probability to almost-sure convergence provided one works with sequences and demands slightly faster decay. This is the subject of the next corollary.

Corollary 1
.

Let
a
and
\theta
be as in Theorem
1
. If
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is any sequence converging to zero and if
(\delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence such that
\frac{\delta_{j}}{\epsilon_{j}}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
and, for any
\nu>0

(12)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{j}^{d}}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{j}}{\delta_{j}}\right)>\nu\right\}<\infty,

(13)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{\epsilon_{j}^{d}}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Osc}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{j}}{\delta_{j}}\right)>\nu\right\}<\infty,

then
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}}\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\bar{\mathscr{E}}
almost surely. More precisely, on an event of probability one, we have that, for any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}}(\cdot\,;U),\bar{\mathscr{E}}(\cdot\,;U))=0.

The corollary will essentially follow from the proof of Theorem
1
. We comment on the details in Section
4.4
.

Our results apply in the full generality of stationary, ergodic media without the need for quantitative assumptions. In particular, they apply to periodic and almost periodic media. Of course, in the periodic case, the probabilities above are more-or-less trivial, hence we obtain an unconditional homogenization result for periodic media. We make this precise next in Corollary
2
.

Actually, we can go slightly beyond periodicity. Recall that, for
p\geq 1
, the Besicovitch space
B^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
of almost periodic functions is the closed span of the (generalized) trigonometric polynomials with respect to the norm

\displaystyle\|f\|_{B^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\left(\lim_{R\to\infty}\fint_{Q_{R}}|f(x)|^{p}\,dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},&\text{if}\,\,p<\infty,\\
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})},&\text{if}\,\,p=\infty.\end{array}\right.

Of particular interest for us will be the case when
p=\infty
. It is well-known that if
f\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then cubical averages of
f
converge to the mean uniformly with respect to the center point, that is, there is a real number
\bar{f}
such that

(14)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup\left\{\left|\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}f(y)\,dy-\bar{f}\right|\,\,\middle|\,\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}=0.

This is not necessarily true for functions in
B^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with
p<\infty
(c.f.
[
48
, Remark 5]
), hence this property distinguishes
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as a space of functions that are, in this sense at least, more like periodic functions. Note that such functions are also sometimes simply called
uniformly almost periodic
.

For
B^{\infty}
coefficients, it is not hard to see that the uniformity property (
14
) extends to
{\rm Osc}(\cdot)
, and it is also possible to prove it for a suitable localized version of
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
; see Section
5
for more details and Remark
9
for a discussion of
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
specifically. We thus obtain unconditional homogenization results in both the periodic and
B^{\infty}
settings.

Corollary 2
.

Let
a
and
\theta
be as in Theorem
1
and assume that
a
and
\theta
are each either
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic or in the Besicovitch space
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. If
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
is any function such that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\delta(\epsilon)}{\epsilon}=0,

then
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\bar{\mathscr{E}}
as
\epsilon\to 0
.

The proof of this corollary is given in Section
5.3
.

1.3.
Outlook: Rare Events Regime

To reiterate, some restriction on the microscale
\delta
is necessary in Theorem
1
. As the ratio
\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)
increases, improbable or atypical local configurations become more and more relevant. This is the main content of the companion paper
[
48
]
, which in this section we put into the context of the above results.

In fact, in the case of the random checkerboard in dimension
d=1
, the assumptions of Theorem
1
are sharp. By the random checkerboard, we mean the medium
(a,\theta)
defined by

(15)

(a(x),\theta(x))=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}(A_{z},\Theta_{z})\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{1}(z)}(x),

where
Q_{1}(z)
denotes the cube with unit sidelength centered at
z
and
\{(A_{z},\Theta_{z})\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}}
are i.i.d. random vectors such that

(16)

\mathbb{P}\{\lambda\text{Id}\leq A_{z}\leq\Lambda\text{Id}\quad\text{and}\quad\theta_{*}\leq\Theta_{z}\leq\theta^{*}\quad\text{for each}\,\,z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\}=1.

Recall that, for such i.i.d. fields, large deviations theory says that atypical events at scale
R\gg 1
occur with probabilities that scale like
\exp(-CR^{d})
.

Indeed, building on earlier work of Armstrong and Smart
[
6
]
, the work of Gloria, Neukamm, and Otto
[
38
]
implies just such an estimate for the sublinear growth of the correctors
(\phi,\sigma)
of the random checkerboard: namely, for any
\nu>0
small enough,

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(R\right)>\nu\right\}\sim_{\nu}R^{d}.

(Above
\sim_{\nu}
means the ratio of the left- and right-hand sides is bounded above and below by positive constants depending only on
\nu
.) We refer the reader also to
[
4
, Chapter 6]
for another proof of the lower bound. Using standard large deviations estimates, one can similarly quantify the oscillations of
\theta
in this setting: for any
\nu>0
small enough,

\displaystyle-\log\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Osc}_{0}\left(R\right)>\nu\right\}\sim_{\nu}R^{d}.

For more details, the reader is referred to Sections
4.6
and
4.7
.

In view of the previous two estimates, Theorem
1
implies the following sufficient condition for convergence of
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}
to
\overline{\mathscr{E}}
:

\lim_{\epsilon\rightarrow 0}\frac{\delta(\epsilon)|\log\epsilon|^{1/d}}{\epsilon}=0.

In the companion paper
[
48
]
, this condition is proved to also be necessary when
d=1
and the matrix
a
is constant: if the limit above equals some nonzero constant
\kappa
, then
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}
will converge to a limiting energy
\check{\mathscr{E}}_{\kappa}
with surface tension
\check{\sigma}_{\kappa}
depending nontrivially on
\kappa
. This shows that, in this very particular case, the additional assumptions (
10
) and (
11
) in Theorem
1
are both necessary and sufficient. This is in sharp contrast to the periodic case, see Corollary
2
.

More generally, in
[
48
]
, both random and almost periodic counterexamples are described in arbitrary dimensions
d\geq 1
in which
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}
does not converge to
\bar{\mathscr{E}}
.

1.4.
Outline of the Paper

The outline of the proof of Theorem
1
is presented in Section
2
. As explained there, the bulk of the work involves proving a
\Gamma\text{-}\liminf
-style inequality, which is our Theorem
3
. The core deterministic arguments involved in Theorem
3
are treated in Section
3
. These are combined with averaging arguments in Section
4
to complete the proof of the theorem, the main step of the proof of which appears in Section
4.3
.

Section
5
discusses the periodic and (uniformly) almost periodic settings. The proof of unconditional homogenization for such media (Corollary
2
) appears at the end of this section.

There are three appendices. Appendix
A
establishes what we call the homogenization upper bound, which asserts that
\Gamma
-limit of
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}
is always no larger than the homogenized surface energy
\bar{\mathscr{E}}
(see (
3
)). Appendix
B
develops a notion we call
\Gamma
-convergence in probability. Using this notion, we prove, following
[
2
]
and
[
49
]
, that if the energies of the planar cell problems converge in probability to the homogenized surface tension
\bar{\sigma}
, then
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}

\Gamma
-converges in probability to the homogenized surface energy
\bar{\mathscr{E}}
.

1.5.
Related Literature

Here we cite the most relevant works related to homogenization of Allen-Cahn-type energies with heterogeneous coefficients. At the end, we also very briefly discuss some related models as well as the relevant background from the homogenization theory for divergence-form elliptic equations.

Some of the works that we cite consider vector-valued phase fields
u
, unlike the present work, which only considers the scalar case. For brevity, we will not emphasize this point here. Suffice it to say, though, that we do use techniques (particularly the De Giorgi-Nash-Moser estimate) that are not available in the vectorial setting.

1.5.1.
\Gamma
-Convergence of Allen-Cahn-type Energies: Deterministic Heterogeneities

The
\Gamma
-limit of the (constant-coefficient) Allen-Cahn functional was first proved by Modica and Mortola
[
46
,
47
]
. Expository accounts of this and related results can be found in the lecture notes of Alberti
[
1
]
and the book of Braides
[
13
]
.

Extensions to periodic media were first treated by Ansini, Braides, and Chiadò-Piat
[
2
]
, who considered energies with gradient terms more general than ours, but without an oscillatory contribution from the potential
W
. They were able to prove
\Gamma
-convergence and characterize the
\Gamma
-limit in the regimes
\epsilon\sim\delta
,
\epsilon\ll\delta
, and
\delta\ll\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}
. In the latter case, they also showed that the surface tension
\bar{\sigma}
coincides with what one obtains by first sending
\delta\to 0
and then
\epsilon\to 0
.
1
1

1

Notice that in the companion paper
[
48
]
we show that, in the random setting, the condition
\delta\ll\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}
given in
[
2
]
is no longer a sufficient condition for the homogenized surface tension
\bar{\sigma}
to appear in the limit.
In all three cases, they first studied the convergence of the normalized energy in a a family of planar cell problems (see Section
2
below) and then combined more abstract compactness arguments with integral representation results to argue that this characterizes the
\Gamma
-limit.

Shortly thereafter, Dirr, Lucia, and Novaga
[
26
,
27
]
considered the problem when
a\equiv\text{Id}
and the standard potential
\epsilon^{-1}W(u)
is replaced by a singular perturbation of the form
\epsilon^{-1}W(u)+\epsilon^{-\alpha}g(\epsilon^{-\alpha}x)u
for some
\alpha\in(0,1]
and a suitable periodic function
g
. In case
\alpha\in(0,1)
, this is a two-scale problem with, in our notation,
\delta\gg\epsilon
, and, indeed, the results in
[
26
]
establish that the
\Gamma
-limit coincides with what one might expect by first sending
\epsilon\to 0
and then
\delta\to 0
. When
\alpha=0
, the scale of the oscillations of
g
coincides with the diffuse interface width. In
[
27
]
, the authors proved
\Gamma
-convergence in the case
\alpha=0
assuming that
g=\Delta v
for a suitably small
v
(in the
W^{1,\infty}
norm). In contrast with the present contribution, in both these works, the constant functions
1
and
-1
are no longer global minima of the functional. Instead, the assumptions of
[
26
,
27
]
imply that there are two “pure phases,” that is, nonconstant, periodic global minimizers close to
1
and
-1
, the oscillations of which need to be considered in the proofs. This is a complication that is not encountered in the present work or
[
2
]
.

In a similar vein, Fonseca and coauthors recently revisited the
\Gamma
-convergence problem in the setting in which only the potential term oscillates. In contrast to
[
26
,
27
]
, they considered general potentials of the form
\epsilon^{-1}W(\delta^{-1}x,u)
. The work of Cristoferi, Fonseca, Hagerty, and Popovici
[
19
,
20
]
treated the case
\epsilon\sim\delta
in the setting where
\{W(y,\cdot)=0\}=\{-1,1\}
so
1
and
-1
are once again global minimizers. They followed a strategy of proof that is similar to the one in
[
2
]
.

Since then, Cristoferi, Fonseca, and Ganedi
[
18
]
considered the case when
\delta\ll\epsilon
in periodic media, again under the assumption
\{W(y,\cdot)=0\}=\{-1,1\}
. They proved, as in our Corollary
2
, that the
\Gamma
-limit is determined by the mean of the potential
W(y,u)
in the
y
variable. (This improved earlier work of Hagerty
[
39
]
, who had imposed the additional restriction
\delta\ll\epsilon^{\frac{3}{2}}
as in
[
2
]
.) The strategy in
[
18
]
is philosophically similar to ours, but quite different mathematically. At a high level, both our work and theirs is based on the idea that one should locally replace a phase field
u
by a another function
\tilde{u}
that incorporates the behavior of the medium at or above scale
\delta
. In
[
18
]
, the argument proceeds by dividing up the domain into cubes of size
\delta
and then using a slicing argument to compare
u
to a candidate for a certain cell problem that approximates
\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}W(y,u)\,dy
. By contrast, we decompose the domain into cubes of size
\epsilon
. In each cube, we compare a minimizer
u
of
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}
to a minimizer
\bar{u}
of the homogenized energy with the same boundary values in that cube, obtaining a bound on the difference of the energies using homogenization error estimates and elliptic regularity results.

We also mention another work of Cristoferi, Fonseca, and Ganedi
[
17
]
, wherein they considered the case when
\delta\gg\epsilon
and the potential
W(y,u)
has space-dependent wells (i.e., the zero set
\{W(y,\cdot)=0\}
varies with
y
). They study the volume scaling of the energy, hence a functional of the form

\displaystyle\mathscr{G}_{\epsilon,\delta}(u;U)=\int_{U}\left(\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{2}|\nabla u|^{2}+W(\delta^{-1}x,u)\right)\,dx.

(We refer to this as the volume scaling since the macroscopic energy in the box
Q_{r}
scales like
r^{d}
rather than
r^{d-1}
.)
They showed that, under certain assumptions, if
\{W(y,\cdot)=0\}=\{a(y),b(y)\}
for two periodic functions
a
and
b
, then
\mathscr{G}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}

\Gamma
-converges to a functional
G^{0}
of the form
G^{0}(u;U)=\int_{U}W^{\text{hom}}(u)\,dx
(Theorem 4.3), the minimizers of which are, in a sense that can be made precise, mixtures of
a
and
b
(Corollary 4.4). Taking this a step further, they also fully characterized the first-order (in
\delta^{-1}\epsilon
) correction to
\mathscr{G}_{\epsilon,\delta}
for a natural class of minimizers (Theorem 5.8).

Finally, since this work first appeared as a preprint, Cristoferi and Pignatelli
[
21
]
extended the result of
[
18
]
to situations involving multiple (microscopic) scales.

1.5.2.
\Gamma
-Convergence of Allen-Cahn-type Energies: Random Heterogeneities

The

first random results of which we are aware were contributed by Dirr and Orlandi
[
28
]
. They considered a perturbation of the standard, constant-coefficient Allen-Cahn functional, in which the potential
\epsilon^{-1}W(u)
is replaced by
\epsilon^{-1}W(u)+\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)g(\epsilon^{-1}x)u
, where
g
is a random checkerboard and
\delta(\epsilon)^{-1}=\zeta|\log(\epsilon)|
for some
\zeta>0
. To simplify the problem, they studied the functional restricted to the torus
\mathbb{T}^{d}
in dimensions
d\geq 3
. They proved the existence of two random functions
u^{+}_{\epsilon}
and
u^{-}_{\epsilon}
, which minimize the energy in
H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
and converge to the unperturbed minima
1
and
-1
, respectively, as
\epsilon\to 0
(Theorem 2.1). They also showed that, after shifting by the energy of
u^{+}_{\epsilon}
, the energy
\Gamma
-converges to the perimeter, as if
g
were identically zero (Theorem 2.3).

More recently, the first author
[
49
]
considered functionals similar to the ones studied here, albeit in the regime
\epsilon\sim\delta
and with stationary ergodic oscillations only in the gradient term. (In fact, only
\delta=\epsilon
is considered there, but the arguments readily generalize to the case when
\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)\to c
for some
c>0
.) That work, which followed the strategy of
[
2
]
, highlighted the fact that it is useful to consider the energy of the planar cell problem as an almost-monotone function in the normal variable and a subadditive process in the transversal variables. That point-of-view is also useful in the present context, as it forms the basis for our proof of the (unconditional) homogenization upper bound (see Appendix
A
below).

The results of
[
2
,
19
,
49
]
were generalized by Marziani
[
45
]
, who considered the general stationary ergodic setting with oscillations both in the gradient and well terms, still only in the regime
\epsilon\sim\delta
and with the assumption that
1
and
-1
are global minima of the functional. The strategy of proof is the same as in
[
2
]
. In contrast with
[
49
]
, in
[
45
]
, the convergence of the planar cell problem is proved by adapting the arguments of Cagnetti, Dal Maso, Scardia, and Zeppieri
[
15
]
, who had earlier considered surface energy functionals with stationary ergodic coefficients.

We also mention the recent work of Dos Santos, Rodiac, and Sandier
[
29
]
, who considered specific classes of Ginzburg-Landau- and Allen-Cahn-type functionals with stationary ergodic (or periodic) coefficients. In the Allen-Cahn case, they restricted to
a\equiv\text{Id}
and considered potentials of the form
W(\delta^{-1}x,u)=(u^{2}-a(\delta^{-1}x))^{2}
, where
a
is stationary ergodic (or periodic). Using the special form of
W
, they were able to characterize the limiting behavior of volume-constrained minimizers in a bounded domain while assuming only that
\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
(Theorem 7.1).

1.5.3.
Related Models

There is a growing literature on
\Gamma
-convergence results for heterogeneous energy functionals incorporating surface effects. Without delving deeply into this body of work, we mention the recent contributions of Bach, Esposito, Marziani, and Zeppieri
[
7
,
8
]
, who studied variants of the Ambrosio-Tortorelli functional with heterogeneous coefficients. As in the literature on Allen-Cahn-type functionals reviewed above, in their work, the heterogeneity length scale
\delta(\epsilon)
is coupled to the singular perturbation scale
\epsilon
, and they were able to characterize the
\Gamma
-limit in the periodic setting in the three regimes
\delta\gg\epsilon
,
\delta\sim\epsilon
, and
\delta\ll\epsilon
, the last case being the most difficult (and involving some extra assumptions). When
\delta\sim\epsilon
, stochastic homogenization was proved by Bach, Marziani, and Zeppieri in
[
9
]
, but, as far as we know, for this class of functionals, the
\delta\ll\epsilon
regime in the random case remains a challenging open problem.

1.5.4.
Stochastic Homogenization of Divergence-Form Elliptic Equations

This paper takes advantage of recent developments in the theory of stochastic homogenization of divergence-form elliptic operators. We refer the reader to the article of Josien and Otto
[
41
]
and the books of Armstrong, Kuusi, and Mourrat
[
3
]
and Armstrong and Kuusi
[
4
]
both for historical background and the state-of-the-art.

Perhaps the most notable place where we benefit from recent insights is in the utilization of the quantities
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
and
{\rm Osc}(\cdot)
defined in (
7
) and (
9
) above. The quantity
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
is closely related to what has been dubbed the minimal radius by Gloria, Neukamm, and Otto
[
38
]
, and, as is apparent in the proofs below, it turns out to be a very convenient tool for measuring how close the gradient energy term is to its homogenized form. Our use of the augmented corrector
(\phi,\sigma)
and
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
is inspired by the work of Otto and collaborators, particularly
[
38
]
and
[
41
]
.

1.6.
Notation and Terminology

Throughout the paper, we denote by
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
the standard orthonormal basis of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We write
Q_{r}
for the cube

Q_{r}=\left(-\frac{r}{2},\frac{r}{2}\right)^{d}=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,|y\cdot e_{i}|<\frac{r}{2}\,\,\text{for each}\,\,i\in\{1,\dots,d\}\right\}.

The translated copy of
Q_{r}
centered at
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is denoted by
Q_{r}(x)
.

Throughout the paper, given two families of real numbers
(A_{\eta})_{\eta}
and
(B_{\eta})_{\eta}
depending on some parameter
\eta
, we write

A_{\eta}\lesssim B_{\eta}

if there is a constant
C>0
such that
A_{\eta}\leq CB_{\eta}
for all values of
\eta
. If the constant
C
is determined by some other parameters, say,
\mu
and
\varrho
, then we indicate this by writing
A_{\eta}\lesssim_{\mu,\varrho}B_{\eta}
. Our usage of the symbol
\gtrsim
is entirely analogous. The symbol
\sim
means that both
\lesssim
and
\gtrsim
hold.

We use a particular normalization of the
H^{-1}
norm, which for
f\in L^{2}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is given by

(17)

\|f\|_{H^{-1}(U)}=\sup\left\{\fint_{U}fv\,\mid\,v\in H^{1}_{0}(U),\,\,\fint_{U}|\nabla v|^{2}\leq 1\right\}.

Occasionally, we abbreviate

(18)

\displaystyle m(\mathscr{F},U,g)\coloneqq\min\{\mathscr{F}(v,U)~|~v\in g+H_{0}^{1}(U)\}.

Finally, we say that a function
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a
modulus of continuity
if
\omega
is nondecreasing, continuous at zero, and
\omega(0)=0
.

2.
Proof of Theorem
1

In this section, we outline the proof of our main theorem on the homogenization regime, Theorem
1
. We use the same reduction as in
[
2
,
49
,
19
,
45
]
, exploiting the fact that
\Gamma
-convergence is equivalent to the convergence of the (suitably scaled) energy in a certain family of planar cell problems. Precisely, we fix a function
q:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfying

(19)

\displaystyle\sup_{s\in\mathbb{R}}|q^{\prime}(s)|+\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}q^{\prime}(s)^{2}+W(q(s))\,ds<\infty,

(20)

\displaystyle-1\leq q(s)\leq 1,\quad q(s)\rightarrow 1\quad\text{as}~s\rightarrow\infty,\quad{\rm and}\quad q(s)\rightarrow-1\quad\text{as}~s\rightarrow-\infty.

Notice that the function
q(\gamma^{-1}\cdot)
converges to
\boldsymbol{1}_{[0,\infty)}-\boldsymbol{1}_{(-\infty,0)}
in
L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R})
as
\gamma\to 0
. In order to prove
\Gamma
-convergence, up to rotation and translation, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\frac{1}{\varrho^{d-1}}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;Q_{\varrho})\,\mid\,u(y)=q(\epsilon^{-1}y)\,\,\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{\varrho}\right\}=\bar{\sigma}(e_{1}).

Here
\bar{\sigma}:S^{d-1}\to(0,\infty)
is the homogenized surface tension defined by the relation

\bar{\sigma}(e)^{2}=\sigma_{W}^{2}\bar{\theta}e\cdot\bar{a}e.

Above
\bar{a}
is the homogenized matrix associated with
a
, the definition of which is recalled in Section
4.1
;
\bar{\theta}=\mathbb{E}[\theta(0)]
is the mean of
\theta
; and the constant
\sigma_{W}
is the surface tension of the constant-coefficient Allen-Cahn functional in case
a\equiv\text{Id}
and
\theta\equiv 1
. It is well-known that
\sigma_{W}
is characterized by the variational formula

(21)

\sigma_{W}=\min\left\{\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\left(\frac{1}{2}u^{\prime}(s)^{2}+W(u(s))\right)\,ds\,\mid\,\lim_{s\to\pm\infty}u(s)=\pm 1\right\}.

For a proof of this variational principle, we refer to the notes of Alberti
[
1
]
.

In order to prove convergence, we begin by observing that the homogenized norm
\bar{\sigma}
of (
2
) is always an upper bound. Note that in contrast to Theorem
3
below, the next result does not assume any smoothness of
W
.

Theorem 2
.

Assume that the medium
(a,\theta)
satisfies the assumptions of Section
1.1
and the potential
W:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
is a nonnegative continuous function such that
W(u)=0
if and only if
u\in\{-1,1\}
. If
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
is any scaling such that
\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, then, for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\varrho>0
, with probability one,

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\frac{1}{\varrho^{d-1}}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;Q_{\varrho}(x_{0}))\,\mid\,u-q(\epsilon^{-1}(\cdot-x_{0})\cdot e_{1})\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{\varrho}(x_{0})\right\}\leq\bar{\sigma}(e_{1}).

Since this is a basic observation, applicable in both the homogenization and rare events regimes, the proof is relegated to Appendix
A
.

Next, we prove that
\bar{\sigma}
is also a lower bound provided the two assumptions (
10
) and (
11
) both hold.

Theorem 3
.

Assume that the medium
(a,\theta)
and the potential
W
satisfy the assumptions of Section
1.1
. If the scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
is chosen so that (
10
) and (
11
) both hold, then, for any
x_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\varrho>0
,

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\frac{1}{\varrho^{d-1}}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;Q_{\varrho}(x_{0}))\,\mid\,u-q(\epsilon^{-1}(\cdot-x_{0})\cdot e_{1})\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{\varrho}(x_{0}))\right\}

in probability.

The proof of Theorem
3
is the subject of the next two sections, Sections
3
and
4
. As we prove in the companion paper
[
48
]
, there are counterexamples showing that the theorem fails to hold without (
10
) or (
11
).

For concreteness, we have fixed the direction of the transition in the results above to equal the first coordinate vector
e_{1}
. In fact, these results remain true if we replace
e_{1}
by any unit vector, as, indeed, our assumptions are rotationally invariant.

Proposition 2
.

If the medium
(a,\theta)
satisfies the assumptions of Section
1.1
, then, for any orthogonal transformation
\mathcal{O}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the rotated coefficients
(a^{\mathcal{O}},\theta^{\mathcal{O}})
given by

a^{\mathcal{O}}(x)=a(\mathcal{O}(x)),\quad\theta^{\mathcal{O}}(x)=\theta(\mathcal{O}(x)),

satisfy the same assumptions with the group action
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
replaced by the action
(\tau^{\mathcal{O}}_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
given by
\tau^{\mathcal{O}}_{x}=\tau_{\mathcal{O}(x)}
. Furthermore, we have

(i)

(a,\theta)
satisfies the assumptions (
10
) and (
11
) if and only if
(a^{\mathcal{O}},\theta^{\mathcal{O}})
does.

(ii)

\bar{a}^{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{O}^{-1}\bar{a}\mathcal{O}
and
\bar{\theta}^{\mathcal{O}}=\bar{\theta}
.

In view of the proposition, we have proved that, in any given direction
e
, the energy of the planar cell problem converges to
\bar{\sigma}(e)
in probability as the size of the cell goes to infinity. The next result asserts that this implies
\Gamma
-convergence in probability. Toward that end, it is again convenient to work with orthogonal transformations. Given an orthogonal transformation
\mathcal{O}\in O(d)
, define the rotated cube
Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r}
by

Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r}=\mathcal{O}(Q_{r}).

Similarly, let
Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r}(x)=x+Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r}
denote the cube centered at
x
with respect to these rotated axes. Since the map
\mathcal{O}\mapsto\mathcal{O}(e_{1})
maps surjectively onto
S^{d-1}
, in this way we cover all possible directions.

Theorem 4
.

Assume that the medium
(a,\theta)
satisfies the assumptions of Section
1.1
and, in addition, that
W
is a nonnegative continuous function such that
\{u\in\mathbb{R}\,\mid\,W(u)=0\}=\{-1,1\}
and satisfying the (super)quadratic growth assumption (
4
).

Let
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
be a positively one-homogeneous convex function. Suppose that the scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
is chosen in such a way that, for any
(x,\varrho,\mathcal{O})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)\times O(d)
, we have that

(22)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\frac{\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x))}{\varrho^{d-1}}\,\mid\,u-q(\epsilon^{-1}(\cdot-x)\cdot\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))\in H^{1}_{0}(Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x))\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad=\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1})).

in probability. Then, for any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\nu>0
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}\left\{d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(\cdot\,;U),\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}(\cdot\,;U))>\nu\right\}=0,

where
\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}
is the anisotropic surface energy determined by
\sigma
defined in (
128
).

Since this result is very similar to related results in
[
49
]
and
[
45
]
, its proof is deferred to Appendix
B
.

Finally, combining these intermediate results, we arrive at the proof of our main theorem on the homogenization regime.

Proof of Theorem
1
.

In view of Proposition
2
, the limit (
22
) holds for any
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)\times O(d)
if and only if it holds for any
(x,\varrho)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)
with
\mathcal{O}=\text{Id}
held fixed. Taken together, Theorems
2
and
3
imply that this is indeed the case. Therefore, Theorem
4
implies that, for any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}\left\{d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(\cdot;U),\bar{\mathscr{E}}(\cdot;U))>\nu\right\}=0,

which is the desired conclusion.∎

2.1.
Proof of Corollary
1

As in the introduction, under slightly stronger assumptions, convergence in probability can be upgraded to almost-sure convergence. Again, as is explained in the appendix, this reduces to proving almost-sure convergence of the energy in the planar cell problems. The next corollary covers the corresponding improvement of Theorem
3
:

Corollary 3
.

Assume that the medium
(a,\theta)
and the potential
W
satisfy the assumptions of Section
1.1
. If
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a sequence of positive numbers converging to zero and
(\delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is such that
\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\delta_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
and conditions (
12
) and (
13
) both hold, then, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\varrho>0
,

\displaystyle\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})\leq\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\frac{1}{\varrho^{d-1}}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}}(u;Q_{\varrho}(x))\,\mid\,u-q(\epsilon_{j}^{-1}(\cdot-x)\cdot e_{1})\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{\varrho}(x))\right\}

with probability one.

For definiteness, the next proof explains how to deduce almost-sure
\Gamma
-convergence from the previous corollary:

Proof of Corollary
1
.

Fix sequences
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
,
\epsilon_{j}^{-1}\delta_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
, and for which the assumptions (
12
) and (
13
) both hold. Combining the results of Corollary
3
and Theorem
2
, we conclude that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\varrho>0
,

\lim_{j\to\infty}\min\left\{\frac{1}{\varrho^{d-1}}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}}(u;Q_{\varrho}(x))\,\mid\,u-q(\epsilon_{j}^{-1}(\cdot-x)\cdot e_{1})\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{\varrho}(x))\right\}=\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})

with probability one. Furthermore, by rotational invariance (Proposition
2
), this remains true if the direction
e_{1}
and the cube
Q_{\varrho}(x)
are rotated. Therefore, by Proposition
13
in the appendix (which is the almost-sure version of Theorem
4
),

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}}(\cdot;U),\bar{\mathscr{E}}(\cdot;U))=0\quad\text{with probability one.}

∎

3.
Relative Error Estimates

This section and the next treat the proof of Theorem
3
. As in the previous section, we begin by fixing a one-dimensional planar boundary condition
q:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, which is only assumed to satisfy (
19
) and (
20
). Writing
e_{1}=(1,0,,\dots,0)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
for the first standard basis vector, recall that our interest is in the analysis of the following limit:

(23)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\frac{1}{\varrho^{d-1}}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;Q_{\varrho}(x))\,\mid\,u-q(\epsilon^{-1}(\cdot-x)\cdot e_{1})\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{\varrho}(x))\right\}.

The prefactor of
\varrho^{d-1}
is natural since heuristically, we expect that the minimizer transitions between the boundary in a neighborhood of a
(d-1)
-dimensional surface. Indeed, using the boundary datum as a competitor, we readily obtain an
\epsilon
-independent upper bound of order
\varrho^{d-1}
.

Our analysis begins by rewriting (
23
) via a mesoscopic rescaling. In particular, blowing up space by a factor
\epsilon^{-1}
, we define

(24)

R\coloneqq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\quad{\rm and}\quad\gamma(R)\coloneqq\frac{\delta(\epsilon)}{\epsilon}

so that we are now interested in the limit as
R\to\infty
and the quantity in (
23
) becomes

(25)

\min\left\{\frac{1}{(\varrho R)^{d-1}}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)}(u;Q_{\varrho R}(Rx))\,\mid\,u-q((\cdot-Rx)\cdot e_{1})\in H_{0}^{1}(Q_{\varrho R}(Rx))\right\}.

Here
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}
is the functional

(26)

\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;U)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}a(\gamma^{-1}x)\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx+\int_{U}\theta(\gamma^{-1}x)W(u)\,dx=\mathscr{F}_{1,\gamma}(u;U)

for any open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

The analysis of (
25
) proceeds in two steps. We begin by defining a homogenized functional
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{F}}(u;U)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}\bar{a}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx+\int_{U}\bar{\theta}W(u)\,dx

Well-known homogenization results imply that
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(\cdot;U)\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\cdot;U)
for any fixed bounded open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
as
\gamma\to 0
.

In the first step, contained in the rest of the present section, we establish a deterministic estimate of the difference
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}-\overline{\mathscr{F}}
measured using the correctors associated with the operator
-\nabla\cdot(a(x)\nabla)
and the
H^{-1}
norm of
\theta(\gamma^{-1}\cdot)-\bar{\theta}
. It bears emphasizing that this part of the proof does not involve any probabilistic arguments. For this reason, a set of distilled, deterministic assumptions on the medium
(a,\theta)
is imposed in Section
3.1
that replace those in Section
1.1
in this section only. Later, in Section
4
, we will prove that the probabilistic assumptions of Section
1.1
imply that those of Section
3.1
hold with probability one.

In the second step, carried out in Section
4
, we use the statistical or self-averaging properties of the medium to show that the error becomes negligible provided
R
does not grow too fast. At a purely qualitative level, the soft criteria (
10
) and (
11
) are sufficient for this purpose.

Remark 3
.

Throughout this section, to lighten the notation, we abuse notation by identifying
q
with its extension
x\mapsto q(x\cdot e_{1})
to
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

3.1.
(Deterministic) Assumptions

As mentioned above, throughout this section, the arguments are entirely deterministic. No properties of the underlying probability space are used, and, in particular, probabilistic (or ergodic theoretic) arguments can be postponed until the next section. Toward that end, it is convenient to make precise exactly the properties of the medium
(a,\theta)
that are used here.

Remark 4
.

In what follows, we allow ourselves the flexibility to choose correctors depending on the domain, that is, we work with correctors
(\phi_{Q},\sigma_{Q})
that vary with the cube
Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This is convenient in the almost-periodic setting for technical reasons, see the discussion in Section
5.2
, particularly Remark
9
.

We assume that, in addition to the medium
(a,\theta)
and effective coefficients
\bar{a}
and
\bar{\theta}
, for each axis-aligned cube
Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there are functions

\displaystyle\phi_{Q,\xi}:Q\to\mathbb{R},\quad\sigma_{Q,\xi}:Q\to\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}

and a constant matrix
\bar{a}(Q)\in\text{Sym}(d)
such that the following conditions hold:

3.1.1.
Bounds on
(a,\theta,\bar{a},\bar{a}(Q),\bar{\theta})

We assume the following pointwise bounds on
a
and
\theta

(27)

\lambda\text{Id}\leq a(y)\leq\Lambda\text{Id},\quad\theta_{*}\leq\theta(y)\leq\theta^{*}\quad\text{for each}\quad y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

together with the identical bounds on the constants
\bar{a}
,
\bar{a}(Q)
, and
\bar{\theta}

(28)

\lambda\text{Id}\leq\bar{a},\bar{a}(Q)\leq\Lambda\text{Id},\quad\theta_{*}\leq\bar{\theta}\leq\theta^{*}.

3.1.2.
Helmholtz-type Decomposition

We assume
\phi_{Q,\xi}\in H^{1}(Q)
;
\sigma_{Q,\xi}\in L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d\times d})
is a skew-symmetric matrix field with
\nabla\cdot\sigma_{Q,\xi}\in L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
; and they combine to provide a Helmholtz-type decomposition of the vector field
a(y)\xi
:

(29)

\displaystyle a(y)\xi=\bar{a}(Q)\xi-a(y)\nabla\phi_{Q,\xi}(y)+(\nabla\cdot\sigma_{Q,\xi})(y)\quad\text{for a.e.}\quad y\in Q.

Above the divergence
\nabla\cdot\sigma
of a matrix field
\sigma
is given by
(\nabla\cdot\sigma)_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial\sigma_{ji}}{\partial x_{j}}
.

Additionally, we assume that the map
\xi\mapsto(\phi_{Q,\xi},\sigma_{Q,\xi})
is linear in
\xi
, that is, for any
\xi_{1},\xi_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\alpha\in\mathbb{R}
,

\displaystyle(\phi_{Q,\alpha\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}},\sigma_{Q,\alpha\xi_{1}+\xi_{2}})=(\alpha\phi_{Q,\xi_{1}}+\phi_{Q,\xi_{2}},\alpha\sigma_{Q,\xi_{1}}+\sigma_{Q,\xi_{2}})\quad\text{a.e. in}\,\,Q.

3.2.
{\rm Sub}(Q)
and
{\rm Osc}(Q)

As in the introduction, we concatenate the correctors by introducing the vector-valued
\phi_{Q}\coloneqq(\phi_{Q,e_{1}},\dots,\phi_{Q,e_{d}})
and tensor-valued
\sigma_{Q}\coloneqq(\sigma_{Q,e_{1}},\dots,\sigma_{Q,e_{d}})
. We then define
{\rm{Sub}}(Q)
, and
{\rm{Osc}}(Q)
by

\displaystyle{\rm{Sub}}(Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|Q|^{\frac{1}{d}}}\left(\fint_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}(y),\sigma_{Q}(y))-\fint_{Q}(\phi_{Q}(y^{\prime}),\sigma_{Q}(y^{\prime}))\,dy^{\prime}|^{2}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

\displaystyle{\rm Osc}(Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|Q|^{\frac{1}{d}}}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)},\phantom{\int}

where the
H^{-1}
norm is normalized as in (
17
).

Notice that the assumption (
29
) is invariant under subtracting the mean from
\phi_{Q}
and
\sigma_{Q}
. This motivates subtracting the mean in the definition of
{\rm Sub}(Q)
.

3.3.
Invariance under Rescaling

It is important to note that the assumptions of Section
3.1
are invariant under rescaling. In particular, for any
\gamma>0
, if we define the rescaled fields
a^{\gamma}
and
\theta^{\gamma}
by

(30)

\displaystyle a^{\gamma}(x)=a(\gamma^{-1}x),\quad\theta^{\gamma}(x)=\theta(\gamma^{-1}x),

then the correctors should also be rescaled by defining
\phi^{\gamma}_{Q}
and
\sigma^{\gamma}_{Q}
by

(31)

\displaystyle\quad\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}(x)=\gamma\phi_{\gamma^{-1}Q}(\gamma^{-1}x),\quad\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma}(x)=\gamma\sigma_{\gamma^{-1}Q}(\gamma^{-1}x).

An immediate computation then shows that, for each
i\in\{1,2,\dots,d\}
,

(32)

\displaystyle a^{\gamma}(y)e_{i}=\bar{a}(Q)e_{i}-a^{\gamma}(y)\nabla\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}(y)+(\nabla\cdot\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})(y)\quad\text{for a.e.}\quad y\in Q.

3.4.
Regularized Functional

In the first step of the proof, we utilize a regularized functional
\mathscr{F}^{\rm reg}_{\gamma}
obtained from
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}
by replacing
W
with a suitably regularized version
W_{\rm reg}
. We must emphasize that this is possible because the minimizers in the planar cell problems take values in
[-1,1]
. This is made precise in the next lemma.

Lemma 1
.

Given
R>0
, if
u\in H^{1}(Q_{R})
is such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})=\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(v;Q_{R})\,\mid\,v(y)=q(y\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{R}\right\},

then
-1\leq u\leq 1
in
Q_{R}
.

Proof.

By the assumptions of Section
1.1
, the potential
W
is strictly positive outside of the set
[-1,1]
. Thus,
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(\max\{\min\{u,1\},-1\},Q_{R})\leq\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})
, with strict inequality if
|u|>1
on a set of positive measure. At the same time, by assumption (
20
), the boundary datum
q
takes values in
[-1,1]
, so
\max\{\min\{u,1\},-1\}\in q+H^{1}_{0}(Q_{R})
. Therefore, by minimality,
u=\max\{\min\{u,1\},-1\}
a.e. ∎

Due to the fact that
u
takes values in
[-1,1]
, we are free to change the definition of
W
outside of that interval without changing the energy. In this way, it will be convenient to work with a modification of
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}
that restricts to a strictly convex functional at sufficiently small length scales — or, put differently, the gradient term dominates the potential term at these scales. A convenient way to do this is to replace
W
by a regularized potential
W_{\text{reg}}
such that

\displaystyle W_{\text{reg}}=W\,\,\text{in a neighborhood of}\,\,[-1,1],

(33)

\displaystyle[W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime}]_{C^{0,1}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty,\quad\liminf_{|u|\to\infty}W_{\text{reg}}(u)=\infty,

\displaystyle W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime}(u)=W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime}(2)\quad\text{if}\quad u\geq 2,\quad W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime}(u)=W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime}(-2)\quad\text{if}\quad u\leq-2.

We define the regularized energy
\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}
by using
W_{\text{reg}}
as the potential

\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}(u;U)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u+\int_{U}\theta^{\gamma}W_{\text{reg}}(u).

Define
\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\text{reg}}
similarly. This is a convenient modification for two reasons. On the one hand, the modified functional coincides with the original one when restricted to functions between
-1
and
1
, that is,

\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\text{reg}}(u;U)=\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;U)\quad\text{if}\quad-1\leq u\leq 1\quad\text{in}\,\,U.

By Lemma
1
, this applies, in particular, to minimizers in our planar cell problem. On the other hand, in the homogenization arguments given below, certain error estimates involving the potential
W
trivialize when it is replaced by
W_{\text{reg}}
.

The existence of a
W^{\text{reg}}
as above follows readily from the assumptions in Section
1.1
. See Figure
2
for a depiction of the construction.

u

W_{\rm reg}(u)

-1

+1

Figure 2.
A possible regularization
W_{\rm reg}
of the classical quartic well
W(u)=(1-u^{2})^{2}
(dotted) by a piecewise linear extension (line).

Concerning the strict convexity of
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\text{reg}}
and
\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\text{reg}}
at small scales, we use the following proposition, which is relatively well-known.

Proposition 3
.

There is an
r_{c}>0
depending only on
\lambda
,
\theta^{*}
and
W_{\text{reg}}
such that if
Q
is a cube of side length
r\leq r_{c}
and
u\in H^{1}(Q)
, then

\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}(v;Q)\geq\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\langle D\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}(u;Q),v-u\rangle+\frac{\lambda}{4}\int_{Q}|\nabla v-\nabla u|^{2}\,dx

for any
v\in u+H_{0}^{1}(Q)
. The same conclusion holds true for
\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm reg}
.

Proof.

For simplicity let us write
\mathscr{F}=\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}(\cdot,Q)
. It is well known that under the condition (
33
),
\mathscr{F}
is twice Fréchet differentiable with

(34)

\displaystyle\langle D\mathscr{F}(u),h\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla h+\int_{Q}\theta^{\gamma}W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime}(u)h,

\displaystyle D^{2}\mathscr{F}(u)(h,k)

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}a^{\gamma}\nabla h\cdot\nabla k+\int_{Q}\theta^{\gamma}W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime\prime}(u)hk,

where
h,k\in H^{1}_{0}(Q)
. Hence

\displaystyle D^{2}\mathscr{F}(u)(h,h)

\displaystyle\geq\lambda\int_{Q}|\nabla h|^{2}-\theta^{*}\sup W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime\prime}\int_{Q}|h|^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\lambda-r^{2}\,C_{\text{Poincaré}}\,\theta^{*}\,\sup W_{\text{reg}}^{\prime\prime}\right)\int_{Q}|\nabla h|^{2},

where
r
is the side length of
Q
. Choosing
r\leq r_{c}
sufficiently small yields

D^{2}\mathscr{F}(u)(h,h)\geq\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{Q}|\nabla h|^{2},

which implies the desired estimate.
∎

As hinted already above, the regularized functional
\mathscr{F}^{\rm reg}_{\gamma}
will be useful when we compare the minimizer
u
of (
37
) to its two-scale expansion; see the proof of Proposition
6
below. For now, the next result already hints at the utility of our regularization.

Proposition 4
.

If
Q
is a cube of side length
r_{c}
and
g\in H^{1}(Q)
satisfies
-1\leq g\leq 1
in
Q
, then there are unique
u,\bar{u}\in H^{1}(Q)
such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(v;Q)\,\mid\,v\in g+H^{1}_{0}(Q)\right\},

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\gamma}(\bar{u};Q)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{\gamma}(v;Q)\,\mid\,v\in g+H_{0}^{1}(Q)\right\}.

Furthermore,
-1\leq u,\bar{u}\leq 1
in
Q
and

\displaystyle\frac{\max\{\lambda,\theta_{*}\}}{\min\{\Lambda,\theta^{*}\}}\leq\frac{\mathscr{F}(u;Q)}{\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)}\leq\frac{\max\{\Lambda,\theta^{*}\}}{\min\{\lambda,\theta_{*}\}}.

Proof.

Any two minimizers
u
and
\bar{u}
solve the Euler-Lagrange equation

(35)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot a^{\gamma}\nabla u+\theta^{\gamma}W^{\prime}(u)=0=-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}\nabla\bar{u}+\bar{\theta}W^{\prime}(\bar{u})\quad{\rm in}~Q;\quad u=\bar{u}=g\quad{\rm on~}Q.

Since
-1\leq g\leq 1
, the proof of Lemma
1
implies that
-1\leq u,\bar{u}\leq 1
. Hence, by (
33
), we may replace
W
by
W_{\rm reg}
in (
35
). Therefore
u
,
\bar{u}
are also minimizers of the strictly convex energies
\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}
and
\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\text{reg}}
, and are therefore unique.

Concerning the estimate on the ratio of the energies, this follows from the fact that
\lambda\text{Id}\leq a,\bar{a}\leq\Lambda\text{Id}
and
\theta_{*}\leq\theta,\bar{\theta}\leq\theta^{*}
by the assumptions of Section
3.1
.
∎

\varrho R

r_{c}\sim 1

\gamma

Figure 3.
Cube decomposition (
36
) and a schematic picture of the three length scales
\varrho R\gg 1
,
r_{c}\sim 1
, and
\gamma\ll 1

3.5.
Cube Decomposition

The length scale
r_{c}
of the previous result suggests a cube decomposition as depicted in Figure
3
. It involves three parameters, corresponding to the macroscopic, mesoscopic, and microscopic scales. There is the cube size
\varrho R\gg 1
; the mesh size
r_{c}
, which is fixed from now on, so that it is of order one; and the length scale
\gamma\ll 1
of the underlying medium. Our strategy to establish the closeness of
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}
and
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
involves homogenization error estimates employed on each subcube. The main difficulty comes from the surface scaling
(\varrho R)^{d-1}
of the energy in (
25
). Since we use a cover consisting of approximately
r_{c}^{-d}(\varrho R)^{d}
cubes, it is crucial that these estimates are relative to the energy in the cube under consideration. Otherwise, their sum leads to the wrong scaling.

Before giving the precise statements, let us fix some notation regarding the decomposition. Given the cube
Q_{R}
with
R=(2K+1)r_{c}
for some
K\in\mathbb{N}
, we observe that we can write
Q_{R}
as a union of subcubes of side length
r_{c}
:

(36)

Q_{R}=\bigcup_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z)

The estimate below, comprising the first step of the proof of Theorem
3
, shows that the macroscopic error is controlled by the maximum of the mesoscopic averaging errors in each subcube. As suggested already above, this is a relative estimate: the error equals a small multiple of the total energy. In particular, it scales like
R^{d-1}
.

Theorem 5
.

There is a modulus of continuity
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
with the following property: given any
K\in\mathbb{N}
, if
R=(2K+1)r_{c}
and if
u\in H^{1}(Q_{R})
is such that

(37)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})=\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(v;Q_{R})\,\mid\,v-q\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{R})\right\},\quad u-q\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{R}),

then there is a
\tilde{u}\in q+H^{1}_{0}(Q_{R})
such that

\displaystyle\frac{|\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})-\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\tilde{u};Q_{R})|}{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})}

\displaystyle\leq\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}\omega\big({\rm Sub}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))+{\rm Osc}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))+|\bar{a}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))-\bar{a}|\big),

where
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
is defined by (
7
) and
{\rm Osc}(\cdot)
by (
9
).

Above we abuse the notation for
q
, following Remark
3
.

In the theorem above, it really is necessary to consider the maximum of the mesoscopic homogenization errors
{\rm Sub}_{r_{c}z}\left(\gamma^{-1}r_{c}\right)
and
{\rm Osc}_{r_{c}z}\left(\gamma^{-1}r_{c}\right)
— the counterexamples of the companion paper
[
48
]
show that homogenization may fail if the medium is far from its average even in a finite string of mesoscopic cubes. Of course, in order to control the suprema above in full generality, it is necessary to ensure that the macroscopic length
R=(2K+1)r_{c}
does not grow too fast relative to the inverse of the microscopic parameter
\gamma
. This is the reason why homogenization may fail if
\delta\epsilon^{-1}
decays too slowly with
\epsilon
.

With Theorem
5
in hand, all that is left in the proof of homogenization is to establish that if
K
does not grow too fast relative to
\gamma^{-1}
, then

(38)

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}{\rm Osc}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))+{\rm Sub}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))+|\bar{a}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z)-\bar{a}|\to 0

in probability. The details will be made precise in Section
4
.

3.6.
Properties of Minimizers on Subcubes

In the proof of Theorem
5
, we will need some elementary properties of and estimates on the minimizer
u
of (
37
) restricted to the subcubes in the decomposition (
36
). For the reader’s convenience, we collect them in this subsection.

First, our proof relies on the following PDE lemma, which shows that
u
is uniformly (in the parameter
\gamma
) Hölder continuous at mesoscopic scales.

Lemma 2
.

Suppose
u\in H^{1}(Q_{R})
with
-1\leq u\leq 1
solves the equation
-\nabla\cdot a^{\gamma}\nabla u+\theta^{\gamma}W^{\prime}(u)=0
in
Q_{R}
;
u(x)=q(x\cdot e_{1})
on
\partial Q_{R}
. Then there exist constants
0<\alpha<1
and
C_{\text{Hölder}}>0
, which depend only on
q
,
\lambda
,
\Lambda
,
\theta_{*}
,
\theta^{*}
,
W
and the dimension
d
, such that

[u]_{C^{0,\alpha}(Q)}\leq C_{\text{Hölder}}

for every cube
Q\subset Q_{R}(x)
of side length
r_{c}
.

The lemma is an application of the classical De Giorgi-Nash-Moser theorem. Its proof is given in Section
3.10
.

In our proof of Theorem
5
, we will use the lemma above together with local and global energy bounds that we state precisely for the reader’s convenience in the next proposition.

Proposition 5
.

Let
u
be a minimizer of the variational problem (
37
). Then
u
satisfies the bound

\quad\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\theta_{*}\int_{Q_{R}}W(u)\lesssim R^{d-1}.

Furthermore, on every cube
Q
of side length
r_{c}

(39)

-1\leq u\leq 1\quad\text{in}\,\,Q,\quad[u]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\overline{Q})}\leq C_{\text{Hölder}},\quad\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2}+\int_{Q}W(u)\leq C_{\text{Energy}},

where the constants
C_{\text{Hölder}}
and
C_{\text{Energy}}
only depend
q
,
\lambda
,
\Lambda
,
\theta_{*}
,
\theta^{*}
,
W
and
r_{c}
.

Proof.

Considering the function
v(x)=q(x\cdot e_{1})
as a competitor, one deduces that
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})\leq R^{d-1}\left(\frac{\Lambda}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|q^{\prime}|^{2}+\theta^{*}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}W^{\prime}(q)\right)
, the constant in the parentheses being finite by assumption (
19
).

The
L^{\infty}
and Hölder bounds are direct consequences of Lemmas
1
and
2
, and the local energy bound follows from an application of Caccioppoli’s inequailty, see, e.g., (
78
) and (
79
) below.
∎

In addition to the basic estimates of the previous proposition, we need some higher integrability of
\nabla u
which is provided by the next lemma.

Remark 5
.

To simplify the notation in our estimates, we adapt the convention that for any cube
Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote by
\varrho Q
the cube that has the same center as
Q
but with the radius multipled by
\varrho
.

Lemma 3

(Meyer’s estimate)
.

Let
u\in H^{1}(Q_{R})
be a minimizer of (
37
) and denote by
Q
a cube of side length
r_{c}
. There exists some
p=p(d)>2
such that:

(1)

If
2Q\subset Q_{R}
, then

\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\left(\int_{2Q}|\nabla u|^{2}+\int_{2Q}W(u)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

(2)

If
Q\subset Q_{R}
and
\partial Q\cap\partial Q_{R}\neq\emptyset
, then

\displaystyle\left(\int_{Q\cap Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{2Q\cap Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}+\int_{2Q\cap Q_{R}}W(u)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{2Q\cap Q_{R}}|\nabla q|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

Once again, in the previous lemma, we abuse the notation for
q
as in Remark
3
.

Of importance for us are only estimates in terms of energetic quantities on the right hand side. On a technical level this boils down to replacing
W^{\prime}(u)
terms (that come from standard linear PDE estimates) by
W(u)
. For the classical double-well potential
W(u)=(1-u^{2})^{2}
one expects

\left(\int|W^{\prime}(u)|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\approx\left(\int|W(u)|^{\frac{p}{2}}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

away from the transition of
u
. This step is made rigorous in the proof of Lemma
3
in Section
3.10
by using the so-called
clearing-out property
, which is well known in the literature, cf. Theorem 6 in
[
12
]
, and is adapted to our setting in the next lemma.

Lemma 4

(Clearing-out property)
.

Let
Q
be a cube of side length
r_{c}
. For every
\varepsilon>0
there exists a
\delta>0
such that, for every
u\in H^{1}(Q)
that satisfies (
39
), the following implication holds:

\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2}+\int_{Q}W(u)<\delta\quad\Rightarrow\quad\min\{\sup_{Q}|u-1|,\sup_{Q}|u+1|\}<\varepsilon.

Moreover, the same conclusion holds true for minimizers
\bar{u}
of the energy functional
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\cdot;Q)
on
u+H^{1}_{0}(Q)
.

The clearing out property will turn out to be helpful due to the following observation. By (
5
) both
W
and
W^{\prime}
look like a polynomial near the minima of
W
. Indeed, let us focus on the minimum at
u=1
, where

\displaystyle W(u)=W^{(2\kappa)}(1)(u-1)^{2\kappa}+r(u)(u-1)^{2\kappa}\quad{\rm with}\quad\lim_{u\rightarrow 1}r(u)=0,

so that

(40)

\displaystyle\frac{W(u)}{W^{(2\kappa)}(1)|u-1|^{2\kappa}}\sim 1,\quad\frac{W^{\prime}(u)}{W^{(2\kappa)}(1)|u-1|^{2\kappa-1}}\sim 1\quad{\rm near}\quad u=1.

In the small energy regime, the clearing-out lemma shows that we can appeal to (
40
).

Lastly, when comparing
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}
to
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
using homogenization techniques, we will also need some estimates for the solution of the homogeneous problem on each subcube.

Lemma 5

(Meyer’s estimate)
.

Let
Q
be a cube of side length
r_{c}
and suppose
\bar{u}\in H^{1}(Q)
minimizes
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\cdot;Q)
on
u+H^{1}_{0}(Q)
, where the boundary datum
u
satisfies (
39
). Then

\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}+\int_{Q}W(\bar{u})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for some
p=p(d)>2
.

Let us remark that the above estimate is the same statement as the Meyer’s estimate in Lemma
3
for the heterogeneous medium. In fact, the same proof applies. We comment on the details in Section
3.10
.

Finally, we will need interior Schauder estimates for minimizers
\bar{u}
of the homogenized energy
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
. Once again, the interest here is our bounds are controlled by the energy itself.

Lemma 6

(Interior Schauder estimates)
.

Let
Q
be a cube of side length
r_{c}
and suppose
\bar{u}\in H^{1}(Q)
minimizes
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\cdot;Q)
on
u+H^{1}_{0}(Q)
, where the boundary datum
u
satisfies (
39
). Then

\sup_{\varrho Q}|\nabla^{2}\bar{u}|+|\nabla\bar{u}|\lesssim_{\varrho}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}+\int_{Q}W(\bar{u})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

for all
0<\varrho<1
.

As remarked earlier, it is crucial to have an estimate with energetic quantities on the right hand side. The r.h.s. of the classical Schauder estimate for the Euler-Lagrange equation depends on the norm
\|W^{\prime}(u)\|_{C^{0,\alpha}}
. Via the De Giorgi-Nash-Moser estimate this can be replaced by
2
2

2

Here
L^{\frac{d}{2}+}
is supposed to denote
L^{\frac{d}{2}+\varepsilon}
for some arbitrary small
\varepsilon>0
.

\|W^{\prime}(u)\|_{L^{\frac{d}{2}+}}
. For the classical quartic well this expression scales like a
L^{\frac{d}{2}+}
-norm while
(\int_{Q}W(u))^{\frac{1}{2}}
scales like a
L^{2}
-norm near the constant states
\pm 1
. This mismatch is resolved below by using a variant of Moser iteration.

3.7.
Relative Error Estimates on Subcubes

In this section, we sketch the main ingredient for the proof of Theorem
5
, a slightly quantified homogenization result on the subcubes of the cube decomposition (
36
).

In the rest of this section we fix three constants: the length scale
r_{c}
coming from Proposition
3
and the energetic and Hölder bounds
C_{\text{Energy}}
and
C_{\text{H\"{o}lder}}
from Lemma
5
. Since these constants are fixed once and for all, in what follows, we suppress the dependence of further constants upon them.

Our goal for the remainder of this section is to prove the following qualitative homogenization result that will be used to derive Theorem
5
.

Proposition 6
.

Let
Q
be a cube of side length
r_{c}
and suppose
u\in H^{1}(Q)
is a minimizer of
\mathscr{\mathscr{F}}_{\gamma}
subject to its own boundary conditions, which satisfies (
39
) and
u\in W^{1,p}(Q)
for some
p>2
. Denote by
\bar{u}\in H^{1}(Q)
a minimizer of
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\cdot,Q)
on
u+H^{1}_{0}(Q)
. Then for every
\alpha>0
there exists a
\delta>0
such that if

|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|+\left(\fint_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})-\fint_{Q}(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}<\delta,

then

\displaystyle|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)|\leq\alpha\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\right).

Before we sketch the proof of Proposition (
6
), let us recall from Lemma
3
that there is no loss in assuming
u\in W^{1,p}(Q)
as long as
p
is sufficiently close to two.

The proof of Proposition
6
is based on PDE estimates for the cut-off two-scale expansion that are summarized in the next lemma.

Lemma 7
.

Let
u
and
\bar{u}
be as in Proposition
6
. Furthermore let
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
be a smooth cut-off function with support
\supp{\eta}
compactly contained in
Q
,
0\leq\eta\leq 1
and fix some exponent
p>2
sufficiently close to two. For every
\alpha>0
there exists a
\delta>0
such that if
\fint_{Q}(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})=0
and

|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|+\left(\fint_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}\leq\delta,

then

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\nabla(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|^{2}\phantom{\fint}

\displaystyle+C_{\eta}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)\left(\fint_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+C\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\right)\left(\int_{Q}(1-\eta)^{\frac{2p}{p-2}}\right)^{\frac{p-2}{p}}

where
C
, resp.
C_{\eta}
, denotes a constant, which is independent of, resp. depends on,
\eta
.

A proof is given further down in this section. The estimate from Lemma
7
is upgraded to a statement about energies by a convexity argument. Here is where it is convenient to work with the regularized functionals, since the two-scale expansion of
\bar{u}
that appeared in Lemma
7
does not necessarily have values in between
-1
and
1
. Indeed, notice that Proposition
3
implies that

(41)

|\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}(u;Q)-\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}(u^{*};Q)|\leq\langle D\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}(u;Q)-D\mathscr{F}^{\text{reg}}_{\gamma}(u^{*};Q),u-u^{*}\rangle

whenever
u
and
u^{*}
share the same boundary conditions and
u
is a critical point. Before we make this precise, let us continue to explain the proof strategy.

Lemma
7
is reminiscent of
[
41
, Proposition 2.1, (18)]
. As therein its proof is based on PDE estimates for the Euler-Lagrange equation that we collect in Lemmas
5
and
6
. To employ them it is worth noting that the right hand sides are of the same order as the one in Proposition
6
.

Homogenization will enter the proof of Lemma
7
in a rather soft way via the following lemma, which is inspired by
[
41
, Proposition 2.1, (17)]
.

Lemma 8
.

Let
u
and
\bar{u}
be as in Proposition
6
. For every
\alpha>0
, there exists a
\delta>0
such that if

(42)

\displaystyle|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|+\left(\fint_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})-\fint_{Q}(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\theta\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}\leq\delta,

then

(43)

\int_{Q}|u-\bar{u}|^{2}\leq\alpha\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q).

The above lemma may be seen as a weak form of Lemma
7
; rather then comparing
u
and (the two-scale expansion of)
\bar{u}
in the strong
H^{1}
-norm, we begin with the weaker
L^{2}
-norm. Its proof, as presented in Section
3.9
, is based on a compactness argument. At this point we rely on the local energy bound (
39
).

We will continue by first proving Theorem
5
based on the homogenization result in Proposition
6
, see Section
3.8
. Next, we prove the homogenization statements above, see Section
3.9
. Finally, we collect the proofs of our PDE estimates in Section
3.10
.

3.8.
Proof of Theorem
5

Taking for granted Proposition
6
and Lemma
3
for now, here is the proof of the relative energy error estimate, Theorem
5
.

Proof of Theorem
5
.

The theorem follows directly from Proposition
6
applied to the subcubes in (
36
), after accounting for the rescaling. Toward that end, recall the definitions of the rescaled medium
(a_{Q}^{\gamma},\theta_{Q}^{\gamma})
, see (
30
), and correctors
(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})
, see (
31
). Observe that, given a cube
Q=Q_{r_{c}}(z)
of radius
r_{c}
centered at some point
z
, we can write

\displaystyle\left(\fint_{Q_{r_{c}}(z)}|(\phi_{Q_{r_{c}}(z)}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q_{r_{c}}(z)}^{\gamma})-\fint_{Q_{r_{c}}(z)}(\phi_{Q_{r_{c}}(z)}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q_{r_{c}}(z)}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad=\gamma\left(\fint_{Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(\gamma^{-1}z)}|(\phi,\sigma)_{Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(\gamma^{-1}z)}-\fint_{Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(\gamma^{-1}z)}(\phi,\sigma)_{Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(\gamma^{-1}z)}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq r_{c}{\rm Sub}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))

and, similarly, by (
17
),

\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{r_{c}}(z))}=\gamma\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}\gamma^{-1}z))}\leq r_{c}{\rm Osc}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z)).

Let
u\in H^{1}(Q_{R})
be a minimizer of (
37
). Given a subcube
Q=Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z)
for some
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]
, let
\bar{u}_{Q}
be the minimizer of
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\cdot;Q)
in
u+H^{1}_{0}(Q)
. We define
\tilde{u}\in H^{1}(Q_{R})
by gluing together these functions, that is,

\displaystyle\tilde{u}=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]}\bar{u}_{Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z)}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z)}.

Now, by Proposition
6
, there is a modulus of continuity
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that, for any
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}
,

\displaystyle|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\tilde{u};Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z))-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z))|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\omega\!\left({\rm Sub}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))+{\rm Osc}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))+|\bar{a}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))-\bar{a}|\right)

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\times\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z))+\left(\int_{Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z)}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\right).

Let
\mathcal{E}_{K}(\gamma)
be the maximum error obtained in this approximation:

\mathcal{E}_{K}(\gamma)=\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}\omega\left({\rm Sub}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))+{\rm Osc}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))+|\bar{a}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma^{-1}}(r_{c}z))-\bar{a}|\right).

After summing over cubes, the previous estimate becomes
