\displaystyle|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\tilde{u};Q_{R})-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\mathcal{E}_{K}(\gamma)\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})+\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}\left(\int_{Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z)}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}\right).

Invoking Lemma
3
, the additional gradient term can be bounded via

\displaystyle\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}

\displaystyle\left(\int_{Q_{r_{c}}(r_{c}z)}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{2r_{c}}(r_{c}z)\cap Q_{R})+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\\
|z|_{\infty}=K\end{subarray}}\left(\int_{Q_{2r_{c}}(r_{c}z)\cap Q_{R}}|\nabla q|^{p}\right)^{\frac{2}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{S})+R^{d-1}\sup|q^{\prime}|^{2}.

In view of the fact that
\theta_{*}\leq\theta
and
\lambda\text{Id}\leq a
, we have a lower bound

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})

\displaystyle\geq\min\left\{\frac{\lambda}{2}\int_{Q_{R}}|\nabla v|^{2}+\theta_{*}\int_{Q_{R}}W(v)\,\mid\,v(x)=q(x)\,\,\text{for each}\,\,x\in\partial Q_{R}\right\}

\displaystyle\gtrsim R^{d-1},

where in the last line we used
\Gamma
-convergence of the constant-coefficient Allen-Cahn functional to obtain the lower bound (cf.
[
2
, Theorem 3.7]
). Concatenating the previous estimates, we observe that the additional gradient error terms can be absorbed into
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u,Q_{R})
, leading to

\displaystyle|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\tilde{u};Q_{R})-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})|\lesssim\mathcal{E}_{K}(\gamma)\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})

Up to multiplying
\omega
by a constant, this is precisely the desired conclusion.∎

3.9.
Proof of the Homogenization Statements.

We are now going to prove Proposition
6
. We start with the weak approximation statement in Lemma
8
and show the upgrade to the
H^{1}
-statement later on. While the argument is inspired by Proposition 2.1 in
[
41
]
for the linear problem, the argument here is slightly more elaborate since the PDE is nonlinear.

Proof of Lemma
8
.

The following proof is completely deterministic in the sense that we use only the assumptions of Section
3.1
, most importantly, the decomposition (
29
), and the growth condition (
42
) as an input to obtain the error estimate (
43
). In particular, as throughout this section, no probabilistic arguments are involved here.

Step 1 (Contradictive assumption).
We prove the error estimate (
43
) by contradiction. To this end, let us notice that by translation and dilation invariance of the statement, we may restrict our contradictive proof to a fixed cube
Q
. Therefore, it is enough to suppose that
\{(a_{k},\theta_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are sequences of coefficients satisfying the assumptions of Section
3.1
. Let
\{(\bar{a}_{k}(Q),\bar{a}_{k},\bar{\theta}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
and
\{(\phi_{Q,k},\sigma_{Q,k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
be the associated constants and correctors. As in the assumptions of the present lemma, suppose that
\{(u_{k},\bar{u}_{k})\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
are functions in
H^{1}(Q)
such that
u_{k}
satisfies (
39
) and minimizes
\mathscr{F}_{k}(v;Q)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q}a_{k}\nabla v\cdot\nabla v+\int_{Q}\theta_{k}W(v)
on
u_{k}+H^{1}_{0}(Q)
(i.e., subject to its own boundary conditions), and
\bar{u}_{k}
minimizes
\overline{\mathscr{F}}_{k}(v;Q)\coloneqq\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q}\bar{a}_{k}\nabla v\cdot\nabla v+\int_{Q}\bar{\theta}_{k}W(v)
on
u_{k}+H^{1}_{0}(Q)
. In particular,
(u_{k},\bar{u}_{k})
solves

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot a_{k}\nabla u_{k}+\theta_{k}W^{\prime}(u_{k})

\displaystyle=0=-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}_{k}\nabla\bar{u}_{k}+\bar{\theta}_{k}W^{\prime}(\bar{u}_{k})

\displaystyle\quad{\rm in}~Q

\displaystyle u_{k}

\displaystyle=\bar{u}_{k}

\displaystyle\quad{\rm on}~\partial Q,

To see that the error estimate (
43
) must hold, it suffices to establish that if

(44)

\displaystyle|\bar{a}_{k}(Q)-\bar{a}_{k}|+\left(\fint_{Q}|(\phi_{Q,k},\sigma_{Q,k})-\fint_{Q}(\phi_{Q,k},\sigma_{Q,k})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\theta_{k}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}\rightarrow 0

as
k\rightarrow\infty
, then

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)^{-1}\int_{Q}|u_{k}-\bar{u}_{k}|^{2}=0.

Toward that end, we argue by contradiction: Specifically, we fix an
\alpha^{*}>0
and assume that (after passing to another subsequence)

(45)

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|u_{k}-\bar{u}_{k}|^{2}\geq\alpha^{*}\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)\quad\text{for each}\quad k\in\mathbb{N}.

Below we show that this leads to a contradiction. Before we do this, let us collect some properties of
(u_{k},\bar{u}_{k})
that follow from the above assumptions.

First, by (
39
) and Proposition
4
, we have

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{Q}(u_{k}^{2}+\bar{u}_{k}^{2}+|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}+|\nabla\bar{u}_{k}|^{2})<\infty.

Thus, up to passing to a subsequence, we can assume that there are functions
u,\bar{u}\in H^{1}(Q)
such that
\bar{u}-u\in H^{1}_{0}(Q)
and

\nabla u_{k}\rightharpoonup\nabla u,\quad\nabla\bar{u}_{k}\rightharpoonup\nabla\bar{u},\quad u_{k}\rightarrow u,\quad\bar{u}_{k}\rightarrow\bar{u}\quad\text{in}~L^{2}(Q)

as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Due to the uniform boundedness assumption on the constants
(\bar{a}_{k},\bar{\theta}_{k})
from Section
3.1
, we may select a further subsequence to ensure that

\bar{a}_{k}\rightarrow\bar{a}\quad{\rm and}\quad\bar{\theta}_{k}\rightarrow\bar{\theta}

as
k\rightarrow\infty
for some constant matrix
\bar{a}
and positive number
\bar{\theta}
. The last two assertions are enough to show that both
u
and
\bar{u}
solve

(46)

-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}\nabla\bar{v}+\bar{\theta}W^{\prime}(\bar{v})=0\quad\text{in}~Q

with boundary conditions
\bar{v}=u=\bar{u}
on
\partial Q
; see the next paragraph for the details.

Here comes the argument for (
46
). For
\bar{u}
, the statement almost immediately follows from the above convergence statements. As in
[
41
]
, one can show that

\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q,k},\sigma_{Q,k})-\fint_{Q}(\phi_{Q,k},\sigma_{Q,k})|^{2}=0~~\Rightarrow~~a_{k}\nabla u_{k}-\bar{a}_{k}(Q)\nabla u_{k}\rightharpoonup 0\quad\text{in}~L^{2}(Q).

as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Since we arranged everything such that
\bar{a}_{k}\rightarrow\bar{a}
, we know by assumption (
44
) that also
\bar{a}_{k}(Q)\rightarrow\bar{a}
. This implies

(47)

a_{k}\nabla u_{k}=\bar{a}_{k}(Q)\nabla u_{k}+(a_{k}-\bar{a}_{k}(Q))\nabla u_{k}\rightharpoonup\bar{a}\nabla u\quad\text{in}~L^{2}(Q).

Furthermore, we may write the well terms as

\theta_{k}W^{\prime}(u_{k})-\bar{\theta}W^{\prime}(u)=(\theta_{k}-\bar{\theta})W^{\prime}(u)+\theta_{k}(W^{\prime}(u_{k})-W^{\prime}(u)).

Since
W^{\prime}(u)\in H^{1}(Q)
and
\theta_{*}\leq\theta_{k}\leq\theta^{*}
, this implies

(48)

\theta_{k}W^{\prime}(u_{k})-\bar{\theta}W^{\prime}(u)\rightharpoonup 0\quad{\rm in}~L^{2}(Q).

Together (
47
) and (
48
) imply that
u
satisfies (
46
) distributionally.

In the remainder of the proof, we let
\delta^{*}>0
be a small positive constant to be determined below and consider separately two cases: (i)
\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)\geq\delta^{*}
for each
k
and (ii)
\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)\leq\delta^{*}
for each
k
. (Up to passing to yet another subsequence, these two cases are exhaustive.) We refer to these two cases as the
large
and
small energy regimes
, respectively.

Step 2 (Large energy regime).
Assume that
\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)\geq\delta^{*}
for each
k
. Since
-1\leq u,\bar{u}\leq 1
on
Q
, which is a cube of side length
r_{c}
, Proposition
4
implies that equation (
46
) has at most one solution. Therefore,

0=\int_{Q}|u-\bar{u}|^{2}=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}|u_{k}-\bar{u}_{k}|^{2}\geq\alpha^{*}\inf_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)\geq\alpha^{*}\delta^{*}>0,

which yields the desired contradiction in the large energy regime.

Step 3 (Small energy regime).
It only remains to consider the case when we have
\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)\leq\delta^{*}
for each
k
. Here we use the clearing-out lemma to deduce that
u_{k}
and
\bar{u}_{k}
are uniformly close to
1
or
-1
, and then the regularity assumptions on
W
allow us to pass to a linearized equation.

For technical reasons, we need to separately consider the cases when
\kappa=1
(
W
is approximately quadratic near its minima) and
\kappa>1
(
W
is superquadratic near its minima).

Step 3.1 (Linearization for locally quadratic wells).
Assume that the parameter
\kappa
of Section
1.1
is equal to one. Since
\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)\leq\delta^{*}
for each
k
, we apply the clearing-out lemma (Lemma
4
) to deduce that
u_{k}
is close to either of the minima of
W
, w.l.o.g.

(49)

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\|u_{k}-1\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq\varepsilon^{*},

where
\varepsilon^{*}\rightarrow 0
as
\delta^{*}\rightarrow 0
. In view of Proposition
4
, the energy of the functions
(\bar{u}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is also uniformly small, hence, up to decreasing
\delta^{*}
by a constant factor, we also have
\|\bar{u}_{k}-1\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq\varepsilon^{*}
for each
k
. (Recall that
u_{k}
and
\bar{u}_{k}
share the same boundary conditions. Hence
\bar{u}_{k}
has to be close to the same minimum of
W
as
u_{k}
, see (
49
).) We claim that this leads to a contradiction.

To see this, we begin by defining the functions
\{R_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
by

R_{k}\coloneqq\frac{W^{\prime}(u_{k})-W^{\prime}(1)-W^{\prime\prime}(1)(u_{k}-1)}{u_{k}-1}=\frac{W^{\prime}(u_{k})-W^{\prime\prime}(1)(u_{k}-1)}{u_{k}-1}

and
\{\bar{R}_{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
analogously, so that

(50)

\|R_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}+\|\bar{R}_{k}\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}\leq\omega(\varepsilon^{*}),

where
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
is a modulus of continuity determined by
W
. Now let
w_{k}=\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-u_{k})
and
\bar{w}_{k}=\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-\bar{u}_{k})
, with the same normalization on both terms. Note that

-\nabla\cdot a_{k}\nabla w_{k}+\theta_{k}W^{\prime\prime}(1)w_{k}=f_{k},\quad-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}_{k}\nabla\bar{w}_{k}+\bar{\theta}_{k}W^{\prime\prime}(1)\bar{w}_{k}=\bar{f}_{k}\quad\text{in}~Q

where
f_{k}=-\theta_{k}R_{k}w_{k}
,
\bar{f}_{k}=-\bar{\theta}_{k}\bar{R}_{k}\bar{w}_{k}
. Moreover, the lower bound (
45
) becomes

(51)

\int_{Q}|w_{k}-\bar{w}_{k}|^{2}\geq\alpha^{*}>0.

Since in this step of the proof we restrict ourselves to
\kappa=1
in (
5
), it is straight forward to see by using (
40
) and (
49
) that, for sufficiently small
\varepsilon^{*}
,
W
looks like a quadratic so that from assumption (
39
) on
u_{k}
and the minimiality of
\bar{u}_{k}
, cf. Proposition
4
, we obtain

(52)

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{Q}(|\nabla w_{k}|^{2}+|\nabla\bar{w}_{k}|^{2}+w_{k}^{2}+\bar{w}_{k}^{2})\lesssim 1<\infty.

Hence we may as well assume that there are functions
w,\bar{w}\in H^{1}(Q)
such that

\nabla w_{k}\rightharpoonup\nabla w,\quad\nabla\bar{w}_{k}\rightharpoonup\nabla\bar{w},\quad w_{k}\rightarrow w,\quad\bar{w}_{k}\rightarrow\bar{w}\quad\text{in}~L^{2}(Q)

as
k\rightarrow\infty
. Furthermore, due to (
50
), we can similarly assume there are
f,\bar{f}\in L^{2}(Q)
such that

f_{k}\rightharpoonup f,\quad\bar{f}_{k}\rightharpoonup\bar{f}\quad\text{in}~L^{2}(Q).

Combining these convergence statements with the arguments from Step 1, we obtain the limiting equation

-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}\nabla(w-\bar{w})+\bar{\theta}W^{\prime\prime}(1)(w-\bar{w})=\bar{f}-f\quad\text{in}~Q.

Furthermore, by (
50
), (
52
), and weak convergence,

\int_{Q}(|f|^{2}+|\bar{f}|^{2})\leq\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}(|f_{k}|^{2}+|\bar{f}_{k}|^{2})\lesssim\omega(\varepsilon^{*})^{2}.

Since the constant
\kappa
in Section
1.1
is equal to one by assumption, we know that
W^{\prime\prime}(1)>0
. Thus, if we test the equation above with
w-\bar{w}
and invoke (
49
), we find

\lambda\int_{Q}|\nabla w-\nabla\bar{w}|^{2}+\frac{W^{\prime\prime}(1)}{2}\int_{Q}|w-\bar{w}|^{2}\lesssim\omega(\varepsilon^{*})^{2},

a contradiction to (
51
) as soon as
\delta^{*}
(hence also
\varepsilon^{*}
) is sufficiently small.

Step 3.2 (Linearization for locally superquadratic wells.)
We now sketch the necessary modifications for the last argument from (
50
) onwards, for the case
\kappa>1
in (
5
). As in the previous step, we define
(R_{k})
so that the following identity holds

R_{k}(u_{k}-1)=W^{\prime}(u_{k})-W^{\prime\prime}(1)(u_{k}-1)=W^{\prime}(u_{k}).

By assumption (
5
),

|R_{k}|\lesssim(u_{k}-1)^{2\kappa-2}.

Defining
w_{k}=\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-u_{k})
and
\bar{w}_{k}=\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)^{-\frac{1}{2}}(1-\bar{u}_{k})
as above, they become almost
a_{k}
-harmonic, resp.
\bar{a}_{k}
-harmonic, in the sense that

-\nabla\cdot a_{k}\nabla w_{k}=-\theta_{k}R_{k}w_{k},\quad-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}_{k}\nabla\bar{w}_{k}=-\bar{\theta}_{k}\bar{R}_{k}\bar{w}_{k}\quad\text{in}~Q.

Let us now write
f_{k}=-\theta_{k}R_{k}w_{k}
, resp.
\bar{f}_{k}=-\bar{\theta}_{k}\bar{R}_{k}\bar{w}_{k}
. In view of (
40
), as soon as
\varepsilon^{*}
is small enough,

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|u_{k}-1|^{2\kappa-2}w_{k}^{2}\lesssim\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)^{-1}\int_{Q}W(u_{k})\leq 1,

(53)

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\nabla w_{k}|^{2}\lesssim\mathscr{F}_{k}(u_{k};Q)^{-1}\int_{Q}|\nabla u_{k}|^{2}\leq 1.

Using the first of the above equations, we can estimate

\int_{Q}|f_{k}|^{2}\lesssim\int_{Q}|u_{k}-1|^{2(2\kappa-2)}w_{k}^{2}\lesssim\|u_{k}-1\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}^{2\kappa-2}.

The same computation on
\bar{w}_{k}
together with (
49
) yields

(54)

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{Q}(|f_{k}|^{2}+|\bar{f}_{k}|^{2})\lesssim\|u_{k}-1\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}^{2\kappa-2}+\|\bar{u}_{k}-1\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}^{2\kappa-2}\lesssim 1.

Therefore, by compactness, we may assume that

f_{k}\rightharpoonup f,\quad\bar{f}_{k}\rightharpoonup\bar{f}\quad\text{in}~L^{2}(Q)

along a subsequence as
k\rightarrow\infty
.

Replacing
w_{k}
and
\bar{w}_{k}
by
v_{k}\coloneqq w_{k}-\fint_{Q}w_{k}
and
\bar{v}_{k}\coloneqq\bar{w}_{k}-\fint_{Q}w_{k}
(the same average on both terms), the relation (
51
) becomes

(55)

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|v_{k}-\bar{v}_{k}|^{2}\geq\alpha^{*}>0,

and the equations stay the same, i.e.,

-\nabla\cdot a_{k}\nabla v_{k}=f_{k},\quad-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}_{k}\nabla\bar{v}_{k}=\bar{f}_{k}\quad\text{in}~Q.

Note that
v_{k}-\bar{v}_{k}\in H^{1}_{0}(Q)
since we subtracted the same constant.

First, since by definition we have
\int_{Q}v_{k}=0
, the estimate (
53
) and Poincaré together yield

\int_{Q}v_{k}^{2}\lesssim\int_{Q}|\nabla v_{k}|^{2}\lesssim 1.

Next, after subtracting the equations for
w_{k}
and
\bar{w}_{k}
, we have

-\nabla\bar{a}_{k}\nabla(v_{k}-\bar{v}_{k})=f_{k}-\bar{f}_{k}-\nabla\cdot(\bar{a}_{k}-a_{k})\nabla v_{k}\quad\text{in}~Q

so that, by the energy estimate on this equation (recall
v_{k}-\bar{v}_{k}\in H^{1}_{0}(Q)
),

\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{Q}(|\nabla v_{k}|^{2}+|\nabla\bar{v}_{k}|^{2}+v_{k}^{2}+\bar{v}_{k}^{2})\lesssim 1.

From all this, we may conclude that

\nabla v_{k}\rightharpoonup\nabla v,\quad\nabla\bar{v}_{k}\rightharpoonup\nabla\bar{v},\quad v_{k}\rightarrow v,\quad\bar{v}_{k}\rightarrow\bar{v}\quad\text{in}~L^{2}(Q)

along a subsequence as
k\rightarrow\infty
. All this is enough to pass to the limiting equations, i.e.,

-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}\nabla v=f,\quad-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}\nabla\bar{v}=\bar{f}\quad\text{in}~Q,

the same conclusion as we reached in the previous steps of the proof. In particular, we can conclude that

-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}(\nabla v-\nabla\bar{v})=f-\bar{f}\quad\text{in}~Q.

The energy estimate (and Poincaré inequality) for this equation, together with (
54
), yields

\int_{Q}|v-\bar{v}|^{2}\lesssim\int_{Q}(|f|^{2}+|\bar{f}|^{2})\lesssim\liminf_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{Q}(|f_{k}|^{2}+|\bar{f}_{k}|^{2})\lesssim\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\|u_{k}-1\|_{L^{\infty}(Q)}^{2\kappa-2},

which, in view of (
49
), contradicts (
55
).
∎

We now give the proof of Lemma
7
. The strategy is quite standard in the literature, see, for example, Proposition 2.1 in
[
41
]
. Therein a local estimate is proved for the gradient of the two-scale expansion on some small ball relative to the
H^{1}
norm on a larger ball. It turns out that for us it is more natural to introduce the cut-off directly in the two-scale expansion rather than in the estimate; this leaves the boundary datum unchanged.

Proof of Lemma
7
.

We start with some useful identities that culminate in what is known as the intertwining property of the (cut-off) two-scale expansion, cf. (
57
). As for the standard two-scale expansion we compute

\nabla(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}=(e_{i}+\nabla\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u}+\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma} \nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})+(1-\eta)\nabla\bar{u}

and

\displaystyle a^{\gamma} \nabla(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}

\displaystyle=a^{\gamma}(e_{i}+\nabla\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u}+\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}a^{\gamma} \nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})+(1-\eta)a^{\gamma}\nabla\bar{u}.

Invoking the Helmholtz-type decomposition (
32
), we rewrite this in the form

(56)

\displaystyle a^{\gamma} \nabla(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}

\displaystyle=\bar{a}(Q)\nabla\bar{u}+(\nabla\cdot\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u}+\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma} a^{\gamma}\nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})+(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a}(Q))\nabla\bar{u}

\displaystyle=\bar{a}(Q)\nabla\bar{u}+(\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma} a^{\gamma}-\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})\nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})+(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a}(Q))\nabla\bar{u}+\nabla\cdot\eta(\partial_{i}\bar{u})\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}.

Note that the last term in the above computation is divergence free, so that

(57)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot a^{\gamma} \nabla(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}=\nabla\cdot\bar{a}\nabla\bar{u}

\displaystyle+\nabla\cdot(\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma} a^{\gamma}-\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})\nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})+\nabla\cdot(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a}(Q))\nabla\bar{u}+\nabla\cdot(\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a})\nabla\bar{u}.

Hence
u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}
solves the equation

(58)

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot a^{\gamma} \nabla(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})+\theta^{\gamma}W^{\prime}(u)-\bar{\theta}W^{\prime}(\bar{u})

\displaystyle=\nabla\cdot(\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma} a^{\gamma}-\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})\nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})+\nabla\cdot(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a}(Q))\nabla\bar{u}

\displaystyle+\nabla\cdot(\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a})\nabla\bar{u}.

Having the identity
\theta^{\gamma}W^{\prime}(u)-\bar{\theta}W^{\prime}(\bar{u})=(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})W^{\prime}(\bar{u})+\theta^{\gamma}(W^{\prime}(u)-W^{\prime}(\bar{u}))
in mind, one can see that the energy estimate for (
58
) implies

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\nabla(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{Q}(W^{\prime}(u)-W^{\prime}(\bar{u}))^{2}+\int_{Q}(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})W^{\prime}(\bar{u})(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})

\displaystyle+\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}a^{\gamma}-\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})\nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})|^{2}+\int_{Q}|(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a}(Q))\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}

\displaystyle+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|^{2}\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}

so that, by our assumptions in Section
1.1
, we can estimate

(59)

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\nabla(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|W^{\prime\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{\infty}([-1,1])}\int_{Q}(u-\bar{u})^{2}+\int_{Q}(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})W^{\prime}(\bar{u})(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})

\displaystyle+\sup_{Q}|\nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})|^{2}\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\left(\int_{Q}(1-\eta)^{2q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle+|\bar{a}_{Q}-\bar{a}|^{2}\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2},

where
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
.

Let us now handle the second term on the r.h.s.. To this end, we split the integral using the cut-off
\eta
to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{Q}(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})W^{\prime}(\bar{u})(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla(\eta W^{\prime}(\bar{u})(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}))|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\int_{Q}(1-\eta)(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})W^{\prime}(\bar{u})(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla(\eta W^{\prime}(\bar{u})(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}))|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\qquad+\left(\int_{Q}((1-\eta)W^{\prime}(\bar{u}))^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

As before, we may use the Poincaré inequality and Lemma
6
, also in form of (
91
), together with the energetic minimality of
\bar{u}
in form of the estimate in Proposition
4
, to estimate

\displaystyle\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla(\eta W^{\prime}(\bar{u})(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}))|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\lesssim\left(\sup_{Q}|\eta W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|+|\nabla(\eta W^{\prime}(\bar{u})))|\right)\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq C_{\eta}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Furthermore, by Sobolev inequality, used as in (
85
) below, we can estimate

\displaystyle\left(\int_{Q}((1-\eta)W^{\prime}(\bar{u}))^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{Q}(1-\eta)^{2q}\right)^{\frac{1}{2q}}\left(\int_{Q}(W^{\prime}(\bar{u}))^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{2p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{Q}(1-\eta)^{2q}\right)^{\frac{1}{2q}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}+\int_{Q}W(\bar{u})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

for
p
close to one. Inserted in the last three equations into each other implies

(60)

\displaystyle\int_{Q}(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})W^{\prime}(\bar{u})(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})

\displaystyle\lesssim\Big(C_{\eta}\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}+\left(\int(1-\eta)^{2q}\right)^{\frac{1}{2q}}\Big)\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\times\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Using Lemmas
5
,
6
, and
8
together with the energetic minimality of
\bar{u}
in form of the estimate in Proposition
4
, on the equations (
59
) and (
60
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{Q}|\nabla(u-(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq o(1)\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+C_{\eta}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+C\left(\int_{Q}(1-\eta)^{2q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|^{2}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q),

where
o(1)\rightarrow 0
as
\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|^{2}\rightarrow 0
. That yields the claim.
∎

We can connect the lemmas proven above to show Proposition
6
.

Proof of Proposition
6
.

Since the assumptions in Section
3.1
, in particular the Helmholtz decomposition (
29
), are unchanged if we subtract a constant from
(\phi_{Q},\sigma_{Q})
, we assume without loss of generality that
\fint_{Q}(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})=0
. The proof itself will be given in several steps. Throughout them
\eta\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
denotes a smooth cut-off with
\supp{\eta}
compactly contained in
Q
and
0\leq\eta\leq 1
that we will choose in the end. Constants depending on
\eta
are tracked by a subscript.

Before we start the actual proof, the reader may wish to revisit the definitions of
W_{\rm reg}
and
\mathscr{F}^{\rm reg}
in Section
3.4
. It will be useful to keep in mind that these coincide with
W
,
\mathscr{F}
on functions with values between
-1
and
1
, but differ when the two-scale correction is added.

Step 1 (Convexity).
We start the argument by showing that for every
\alpha>0
, there exists a
\delta_{1}=\delta_{1}(\alpha,\eta)>0
such that

\displaystyle|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|+\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}<\delta_{1}

implies

(61)

\displaystyle|\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u};Q)-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}(u;Q)|\phantom{\fint}

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+C_{\eta}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle+C\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)\left(\int_{Q}(1-\eta)^{2q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)|\bar{a}_{Q}-\bar{a}|^{2}.

That is, the convexity, cf. Proposition
3
, enables us to lift the
H^{1}
-bound on the (cut-off) two-scale expansion from Lemma
7
to the energies.

We want to appeal to Proposition
3
. For convenience, we introduce
\delta u=(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}-u
. Using the explicit form for
D\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}
that we derived (
34
), the r.h.s. in (
41
) becomes

\displaystyle\langle D\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u};Q)-D\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}(u;Q),(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}-u\rangle\phantom{\fint}

\displaystyle=\int_{Q}a^{\gamma}\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla\delta u+\int_{Q}\theta^{\gamma}W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\delta u

\displaystyle-\int_{Q}a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\delta u-\int_{Q}\theta^{\gamma}W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}(u)\delta u,

which by Young’s and Poincaré inequality is estimated by (recall also the global Lipschitz bound on
W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}
in (
33
))

\displaystyle\langle D\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u};Q)-D\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}(u;Q),(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}-u\rangle\phantom{\fint}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{Q}|\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u}-u)|^{2}+\int_{Q}|\nabla\delta u|^{2}

\displaystyle+\int_{Q}|W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})-W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}(u)|^{2}+\int|\delta u|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{Q}|\nabla\delta u|^{2}.

This, together with Lemma
7
and estimate (
41
), implies the claimed (
61
).

Step 2 (Boundary layer estimate).
We now argue that the contributions along the boundary layer are negligible. More precisely, we show

(62)

\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm reg}(\bar{u};Q\setminus\varrho Q)\lesssim|Q\setminus\varrho Q|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\right).

The same statement holds true with
\bar{u}
and
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
replaced by
u
and
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}
.

First, observe that

(63)

\int_{Q\setminus\varrho Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\leq|Q\setminus\varrho Q|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{Q\setminus\varrho Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for
p>1
. To show a similar estimate for the well term, we distinguish the cases
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\geq\delta
and
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)<\delta
, where
\delta>0
is chosen such that the clearing-out property, i.e. the conclusion of Lemma
4
, holds.

If
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)<\delta
, we may assume w.l.o.g. that
|\bar{u}-1|\ll 1
, so that by (
40
) we have

W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\sim|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa}\quad\text{in}~Q.

Hence

\int_{Q\setminus\varrho Q}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\lesssim|Q\setminus\varrho Q|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{Q}|(\bar{u}-1)^{\kappa}|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for any
p>1
. If
p
is sufficiently close to one, we can appeal to the Sobolev inequality to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{Q\setminus\varrho Q}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})

\displaystyle\lesssim|Q\setminus\varrho Q|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla(\bar{u}-1)^{\kappa}|^{2}+\int_{Q}|(\bar{u}-1)^{\kappa}|^{2}\right).

Since
|\bar{u}-1|\ll 1
this implies

\displaystyle\int_{Q\setminus\varrho Q}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})

\displaystyle\lesssim|Q\setminus\varrho Q|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}+\int_{Q}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\right).

Note that this estimate also holds true in the regime
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\geq\delta
since
W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})=W(\bar{u})
is bounded. With the gradient estimate (
63
) this combines to (
62
). Again, to reiterate, the same argument would also work with
\bar{u}
replaced by
u
and
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
by
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}
.

Step 3 (Homogenization on the level of energies).
We now argue, that the cut-off two-scale expansion is also a good approximation on the level of energies. To make this precise, we assume from this point on that the cut-off
\eta
is chosen such that
\supp{\eta}\subset\varrho Q
(for
0<\varrho<1
as in Step 2) and
0\leq\eta\leq 1
. For convenience, let us also assume that
\supp{\eta}
is convex. We claim that there exists a
\delta_{2}>0
such that if

\displaystyle\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|<\delta_{2},

then

(64)

\displaystyle|\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u};\varrho Q)-\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm reg}(\bar{u};\varrho Q)|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}+|\bar{a}_{Q}-\bar{a}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{\varrho Q}|1-\eta|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(C_{\varrho}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right),

where
\frac{1}{q}+\frac{1}{p}=1
with
q
sufficiently large. We will show the above estimate for the gradient and well part separately.

Step 3.1 (Gradient term).
Let us begin with the gradient term that splits into

(65)

\displaystyle\int_{\varrho Q}a^{\gamma}\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{j}}^{\gamma}\partial_{j})\bar{u})

\displaystyle=\int_{\varrho Q}a^{\gamma}\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla\bar{u}+\int_{\varrho Q}a\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi_{Q,e_{j}}^{\gamma}\partial_{j}\bar{u})

We also argue for the two terms on the r.h.s. of (
65
) separately.

On the first term on the r.h.s. in (
65
), we decompose the flux
a^{\gamma}\nabla(1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}\partial_{i})\bar{u}
using identity (
56
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\varrho Q}a^{\gamma}\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla\bar{u}

\displaystyle=\int_{\varrho Q}\bar{a}\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla\bar{u}+\int_{\varrho Q}\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}a^{\gamma}\nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})\cdot\nabla\bar{u}+\int_{\varrho Q}(\nabla\cdot\sigma^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{i}})\cdot(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u}\nabla\bar{u})

\displaystyle+\int_{\varrho Q}(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a}(Q))\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla\bar{u}+\int_{\varrho Q}(\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a})\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla\bar{u}.

After integrating-by-parts to take the derivative off of
\sigma_{Q,i}
and invoking Lemma
6
to control the derivatives of
\bar{u}
that appear, this becomes

(66)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}a^{\gamma}\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla\bar{u}-\int_{\varrho Q}\bar{a}\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla\bar{u}\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim C_{\eta}\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)+\left(\int_{\varrho Q}|1-\eta|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q).\phantom{\int}

where
\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1
. Note that all these terms appear in the right-hand side of (
64
).

On the second term in (
65
), we also appeal to (
57
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\varrho Q}a\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})

\displaystyle=-\int_{\varrho Q}\bar{\theta}\,W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}(\bar{u})\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{i}}\partial_{i}\bar{u}

\displaystyle+\int_{\varrho Q}(\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}a^{\gamma}-\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})\nabla(\eta\partial_{i}\bar{u})\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})

\displaystyle+\int_{\varrho Q}(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a}(Q))\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})

\displaystyle+\int_{\varrho Q}(\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a})\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u}).

Note that by Lemma
6
and Caccioppoli’s estimate (applicable since
\supp{\eta}\subset\varrho Q
), applied to the gradient of
\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}+(x-x_{0})\cdot e_{i}
, where
x_{0}
denotes the center of
Q
,

\displaystyle\int_{\varrho Q}|\nabla(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\left(\int_{Q}|\eta\nabla\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}+\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\left(1+\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)

and

\int_{\varrho Q}(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})^{2}\leq C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}.

Combining this with another application of Lemma
6
, the above identity is estimated by

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}a\nabla((1+\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{i}}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}(\bar{u})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma})|^{2}+|\bar{a}_{Q}-\bar{a}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\left|\int_{\varrho Q}(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a})\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})\right|.

On the last term, we need to be more careful. Note that

\nabla(\eta\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})=\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}\nabla(\eta\partial_{j}\bar{u})+\eta(\partial_{j}\bar{u})\nabla\phi^{\gamma}_{Q,e_{j}}

so that Lemma
6
shows

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}(1-\eta)(a^{\gamma}-\bar{a})\nabla\bar{u}\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\left(\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)

\displaystyle+C_{\varrho}\left(\int_{\varrho Q}|\nabla\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\varrho Q}|1-\eta|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q).

We want to remark that the constant in the last line depends only on
\varrho
, not on
\eta
, since we may apply Lemma
6
on
\varrho Q\subset Q
after neglecting the cut-off
\eta
. By another application of Caccioppoli’s estimate, the last three equations combine to

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}a\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}(\bar{u})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{\varrho}\left(1+\left(\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right)\left(\int_{\varrho Q}|1-\eta|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q).

The well term
W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}
is handled via a Sobolev inequality like in (
85
) so that overall we obtain the estimate

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}a\nabla((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}\partial_{i})\bar{u})\cdot\nabla(\eta\phi_{Q,e_{j}}\partial_{j}\bar{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+|\bar{a}_{Q}-\bar{a}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(1+\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{\varrho}\left(1+\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{\varrho Q}|1-\eta|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q).

Assuming
\delta_{2}<1
, these terms are exactly the errors appearing in (
64
). By combining this with (
66
), we finish the estimate for (
65
). This contains all contributions of the gradient part to (
64
).

Step 3.2 (Potential term).
On the well part of (
64
), we appeal to another convexity argument. We start with the observation

|W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u}+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i}\bar{u})-W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})-W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}(\bar{u})\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i}\bar{u}|\leq\frac{1}{2}\sup|W_{\rm reg}^{\prime\prime}|(\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i}\bar{u})^{2}.

It is crucial to work with a second order Taylor estimate here, to obtain an estimate with the right scaling on the r.h.s.. Indeed, by Lemma
6
,

(67)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}\theta^{\gamma}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u}+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i}\bar{u})-\int_{\varrho Q}\theta^{\gamma}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\left(\int_{Q}|W_{\rm reg}^{\prime}(\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\left(\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+C_{\eta}\left(\int_{Q}|\phi_{Q}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)

As above, the
W^{\prime}
term may be handled via a Sobolev inequality, cf. (
85
).

In addition to (
67
), we need to control
(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})
, which we split up in a boundary and interior contribution. Since
\supp\eta\subset\varrho Q\subset Q
, we have

(68)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})\eta W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}\left(\int_{\supp\eta}|W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})|^{2}+\int_{\varrho Q}|\nabla W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since we assume that
\supp{\eta}
is convex, we can apply the Poincaré inequality on
\supp{\eta}
to obtain

\displaystyle\left(\int_{\supp\eta}|W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\int_{\supp\eta}|W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})-\fint_{\supp\eta}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+C_{\eta}\fint_{\supp\eta}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\left(\left(\int_{\supp\eta}|\nabla W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\int_{Q}W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\right).

Since
-1\leq\bar{u}\leq 1
, we may use the previous estimate to post-process (
68
) to

(69)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})\eta W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{\eta}\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}\left(\overline{\mathscr{F}}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{\supp\eta}|\nabla W(\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\right).

We can estimate

\left(\int_{\supp\eta}|\nabla W(\bar{u})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=\left(\int_{\supp\eta}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim(\sup_{\supp\eta}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|)\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

By virtue of (
91
), in the proof of Lemma
6
, we may conclude

(70)

\left|\int_{\varrho Q}(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})\eta W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\right|\leq C_{\eta}\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q),

whenever
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\ll 1
. In the opposite regime
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\gtrsim 1
, we don’t need to use (
91
) to conclude the above inequality since
W^{\prime}_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})
is uniformly bounded.

Lastly, we may argue by Sobolev’s inequality (as done in Step 2) that

(71)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\varrho Q}(\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta})(1-\eta)W_{\rm reg}(\bar{u})\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{\varrho Q}(1-\eta)^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)

for some large
q\gg 1
. Together, (
67
), (
70
), and (
71
) deal with the well part of (
64
).

r_{c}

(1-\varrho)r_{c}

\left(1-\frac{1}{2}\varrho\right)r_{c}

\eta=1
0\leq\eta\leq 1
\eta=0

Figure 4.
Cut-off strategy used in proof of Proposition
6
: The boundary layer of width
1-\varrho
(where
\eta=0
) is energetically negligible while on the set
\{\eta=1\}
the two-scale expansion is a good approximation in energy. The transition layer
\{0<\eta<1\}
, which is squeezed into
\varrho Q\setminus\frac{\varrho}{2}Q
, appears as a technical artifact of the proof and is also shown to be negligible as well.

Step 5 (Conclusion).
We now prove the proposition. Given
\alpha>0
, we show that there exists some
\delta>0
and a constant
C_{\alpha}>0
such that if

\displaystyle|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|+\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}<\delta,

then

\displaystyle|\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm reg}(\bar{u};Q)-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}(u;Q)|

\displaystyle\leq\alpha\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)

\displaystyle+C_{\alpha}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}+|\bar{a}_{Q}-\bar{a}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since
-1\leq u,\bar{u}\leq 1
, we replace
(\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm reg},\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg})
by
(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\mathscr{F}_{\gamma})
on the l.h.s., so that the above claim implies the proposition.

Here comes the argument. First, we choose
\varrho>0
close to one such that the boundary layer is negligible. That is, we appeal to the estimate (
62
) in combination with Meyer’s estimate, cf. Lemma
5
, to get

(72)

\displaystyle\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm reg}(\bar{u};Q\setminus\varrho Q)+\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}(\bar{u};Q\setminus\varrho Q)

\displaystyle\lesssim|Q\setminus\varrho Q|^{1-\frac{1}{p}}\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{4}\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right).

provided
0<\varrho<1
is sufficiently close to one.
Simultaneously, by choosing
\varrho
even closer to one if necessary, we may achieve that for every cut-off
0\leq\eta\leq 1
with
\eta|_{\frac{\varrho}{2}Q}=1
, we have

(73)

C\left(\int_{\varrho Q}(1-\eta)^{2q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq C\left|Q\setminus\frac{\varrho}{2}Q\right|^{\frac{1}{q}}\leq\frac{\alpha}{2},

where
C
and
q
are chosen as in (
61
). Estimate (
73
) will be important later on. For now, we combine (
72
) with the fact that the cut-off two-scale expansion approximates the energy inside
\varrho Q
. This is made precise by (
64
). Therefore, the last estimates and (
72
) and (
64
) combine to

\displaystyle|\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm reg}(\bar{u};Q)-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u};Q)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{4}\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)

\displaystyle+C_{\eta}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+\left(\int_{\varrho Q}|1-\eta|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\left(C_{\varrho}\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\bar{u};Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right).

By choosing the transition layer of
\eta=\eta(\varrho)
sufficiently thin in
\varrho Q\setminus\frac{\varrho}{2}Q
, and appealing to Meyer’s estimate, cf. Lemma
5
, and minimality of
\bar{u}
in form of the estimate provided in Proposition
4
, we obtain

(74)

\displaystyle|\overline{\mathscr{F}}^{\rm reg}(\bar{u};Q)-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u};Q)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)

\displaystyle+C_{\eta}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

It is now left to approximate the energy of
u
by the cut-off two-scale expansion. More precisely, if
\delta
is made sufficiently small, by our choice of
\eta
and (
73
), we may use (
61
) to get

(75)

\displaystyle|\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}((1+\eta\phi_{Q,e_{i}}^{\gamma}\partial_{i})\bar{u};Q)-\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}^{\rm reg}(u;Q)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)+\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\right)

\displaystyle+C_{\eta}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q)\left(\int_{Q}|(\phi_{Q}^{\gamma},\sigma_{Q}^{\gamma})|^{2}+\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}^{2}+|\bar{a}(Q)-\bar{a}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

The estimates (
74
) and (
75
) imply the desired conclusion.
∎

3.10.
Proof of PDE Estimates

In this section we prove the main PDE estimates. We start with the proof of Lemma
2
, which is a consequence of the DeGeorgi-Nash-Moser theorem, cf. Theorem 8.24 in
[
36
]
.

Proof of Lemma
2
.

We partition the cube
Q_{R}(x)
in sub cubes of size
r_{c}
as in (
36
). There is no loss of generality in assuming that
Q
is one of these cubes. We distinguish two cases: (i)
\partial Q
is contained in the interior of
Q_{R}(x)
or (ii)
\partial Q
intersects the boundary of
Q_{R}(x)
.

In case
\partial Q
is contained in the interior of
Q_{R}(x)
, we may directly appeal to Theorem 8.24 in
[
36
]
, that yields

(76)

[u]_{C^{0,\alpha}(Q)}\lesssim_{q}\left(\int_{2Q}|u|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{2Q}|W^{\prime}(u)|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}\quad{\rm for}\quad q>\frac{d}{2}

and some
\alpha
depending only on
r_{c}
, the space dimension
d
, the ellipticity constants
\lambda,\Lambda
, and
\theta^{*}
. The r.h.s. of the this equation is uniformly bounded since
-1\leq u\leq 1
by Lemma
1
.

In case
\partial Q
intersects the boundary of
Q_{R}(x)
, we use the boundary version of the DeGeorgi-Nash-Moser theorem, cf. Theorem 8.29 in
[
36
]
that comes with the estimate

(77)

[u]_{C^{0,\alpha}(Q)}\lesssim_{q}\sup_{2Q\cap Q_{R}(x)}|u|+\left(\int_{2Q}|W^{\prime}(u)|^{q}\right)^{\frac{1}{q}}+\|q\|_{C^{0,\alpha}(\mathbb{R})}\quad{\rm for}\quad q>\frac{d}{2},

where
\alpha
has the same dependencies as above.
∎

Lemma
2
, see also the condition (
39
), enters all proofs only implicitly through the clearing-out lemma, which we prove next.

Proof of Lemma
4
.

Our argument is based on the fact that
u
satisfies (
39
). Upon changing the constants, the same estimate holds true for any minimizer
\bar{u}
of
\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\cdot,Q)
on
u+H^{1}_{0}(Q)
. Indeed, we may appeal to Theorem 8.29 in
[
36
]
to deduce an estimate similar to (
77
) for
\bar{u}
. Hence the following argument applies to both
\bar{u}
and
u
. For simplicity let us restrict to the latter.

Let us fix
\varepsilon>0
and set
A_{\varepsilon}=\{|u-1|\geq\varepsilon~\text{and}~|u+1|\geq\varepsilon\}\subset Q
. Due to the uniform Hölder continuity of the functions under consideration, there exists a radius
r=r(\varepsilon)>0
(that depends on the function only through the upper bound on the Hölder norm) such that

B_{r}+(Q\setminus A_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}})\subset\{|u-1|<\varepsilon~\text{or}~|u+1|<\varepsilon\}\subset Q.

From the continuity of
W
, and the assumption that
W
attains its minimum only at
-1
and
+1
, we know that

|A_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}|\lesssim_{\varepsilon}\int_{Q}W(u)<\delta.

Hence if we choose
\delta\leq\delta_{0}(\varepsilon)
,
A_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}
contains no ball of radius
r
. Phrased differently, that means that every ball of radius
r
intersects
Q\setminus A_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}
, so that

Q\subset B_{r}+\left(Q\setminus A_{\frac{\varepsilon}{2}}\right)\subset\{|u-1|<\varepsilon~\text{or}~|u+1|<\varepsilon\},

which by virtue of the continuity of
u
implies the claim.
∎

Next, we are going to prove Lemma
3
, which corresponds to Meyer’s estimate in our setting. We follow the classical strategy of establishing a reverse Hölder inequality, which enables us to appeal to the Gehring-type lemma of
[
34
, Theorem 3.1]
to conclude.

Proof of Lemma
3
.

For notational simplicity, we will denote by
Q
a cube of some size
r>0
. In the conclusion (Step 3), we specialize to
r=r_{c}
.

Step 1 (Caccioppoli’s estimate).
We will need two slightly different versions of Caccioppoli’s estimate: First, let us consider some cube
Q
of width
r>0
with
2Q\subset Q_{R}
. Then

(78)

\int_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{2Q}(u-c)^{2}+r^{2}\int_{2Q}W^{\prime}(u)^{2}

for every constant
c>0
. Additionally, we need a boundary version of (
78
). To this end, we consider a cube
Q
of width
r>0
such that
2Q
is not fully contained in
Q_{R}
. Then

(79)

\int_{Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{r^{2}}\int_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}(u-q)^{2}+\int_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla q|^{2}+r^{2}\int_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}W^{\prime}(u)^{2}.

Let us recall that
q
denotes the boundary value of
u
, i.e.
u=q
on
\partial Q_{R}
.

The proofs of (
78
) and (
79
) are rather standard. For the readers convenience, we will give an argument for (
79
): Let
\eta\geq 0
be a smooth cutoff that is to be chosen later. Observe that
(u-q)\eta^{2}
vanishes on
\partial Q_{R}
. Therefore we can test the Euler-Lagrange equation for
u
and obtain

(80)

\int_{Q_{R}}a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla(u-q)\eta^{2}=-\int_{Q_{R}}\theta^{\gamma}W^{\prime}(u)(u-q)\eta^{2}.

The right hand side is estimated by

(81)

-\int_{Q_{R}}\theta^{\gamma}W^{\prime}(u)(u-q)\eta^{2}\leq\frac{\theta^{*}}{2r^{2}}\int_{Q_{R}}(u-q)^{2}\eta^{2}+\frac{\theta^{*}r^{2}}{2}\int_{Q_{R}}W^{\prime}(u)^{2}\eta^{2},

while on the left hand side we compute by Cauchy-Schwarz combined with Young’s inequality

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{R}}a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla(u-q)\eta^{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u)\eta^{2}-\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla q)\eta^{2}+2\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla\eta)(u-q)\eta

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u)\eta^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla q\cdot\nabla q)\eta^{2}

\displaystyle-2\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u)^{\frac{1}{2}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}|u-q|\eta

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{4}\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u)\eta^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla q\cdot\nabla q)\eta^{2}-4\int_{Q_{R}}(a^{\gamma}\nabla\eta\cdot\nabla\eta)(u-q)^{2}.

Together with (
80
) and (
81
), the last equation implies

\displaystyle\int_{Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}\eta^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\int_{Q_{R}}\left(\frac{1}{r^{2}}\eta^{2}+|\nabla\eta|^{2}\right)(u-q)^{2}+\int_{Q_{R}}|\nabla q|^{2}\eta^{2}

\displaystyle+r^{2}\int_{Q_{R}}W^{\prime}(u)^{2}\eta^{2}.

Choosing
\eta
with
\eta=1
on
Q
,
\eta=0
outside
2Q
and
|\nabla\eta|\lesssim\frac{1}{r}
yields (
79
).

Step 2 (Reverse Hölder inequality).
We now use (
78
) and (
79
) to show two kinds of reverse Hölder inequalities: On the one hand, given a cube
Q
of width
r>0
such that
2Q\subset Q_{R}
, we claim that

(82)

\left(\fint_{Q}|\nabla u|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left(\fint_{2Q}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+r\left(\fint_{2Q}W^{\prime}(u)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad{\rm where}\quad p=\frac{2d}{d+2}.

On the other hand, if
2Q
is not fully contained in
Q_{R}
, we instead claim that

(83)

\displaystyle\left(\fint_{Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\fint_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}+\left(\fint_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla q|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle+r\left(\fint_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}W^{\prime}(u)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},\quad{\rm where}\quad p=\frac{2d}{d+2}.

As in the last step, we only give an argument for the second claim. The first inequality follows similarly.

Let us recall the following version of the scale-invariant Poincaré-Sobolev inequality: if
p=\frac{2d}{d+2}
and
f\in W^{1,p}(2Q)
is such that
|\{f=0\}|\geq\alpha|2Q|
for some
\alpha\in(0,1)
, then

\left(\int_{2Q}f^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim_{\alpha}\left(\int_{2Q}|\nabla f|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

(See, e.g.,
[
35
, pp. 153]
.) Applying this estimate to the function
f=(u-q)\mathbf{1}_{Q_{R}}
, which satisfies
|\{f=0\}|\geq|(2Q)\setminus Q_{R}|\geq 2^{-1}|Q|
, we find

\left(\int_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}(u-q)^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\left(\int_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla u-\nabla q|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}.

It is now left to observe that by our choice of
p
,

r^{-1}r^{-\frac{d}{2}}=r^{-\frac{2+d}{2}}=r^{-\frac{d}{p}}\quad\text{and}\quad p<2

so that the above inequality and (
79
) combine to yield (
83
).

Step 3 (Conclusion).
To conclude, we now use the version of Gehring’s lemma from
[
34
, Theorem 3.1]
. Again, we only present the argument for the boundary estimate.

First note that (
83
), as stated above, holds for every cube. Indeed, (
82
) implies (
83
) for interior cubes. Therefore, Gehring’s lemma yields the estimate

(84)

\displaystyle\left(\fint_{Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\fint_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\fint_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|\nabla q|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

\displaystyle+\left(\fint_{2Q}\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{R}}|W^{\prime}(u)|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for some
p=p(d)>2
for any cube
Q
of size
r_{c}
. We apply this to a cube that satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
3
. That is,
Q
has size
r_{c}
and is either
2Q\subset Q_{R}
or
Q\subset Q_{R}
with
\partial Q\cap\partial Q_{R}\neq\emptyset
. Since we only work with cubes of size
r_{c}
from now on, we can drop the averages in (
84
).

To conclude the lemma, we need to post-process the last term on the r.h.s. of (
84
). To this end, we distinguish the two cases
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u,2Q\cap Q_{R})\geq\delta
and
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u,2Q\cap Q_{R})<\delta
where
\delta>0
is chosen according to the clearing-out lemma, cf. Lemma
4
.

If
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u,2Q\cap Q_{R})<\delta
, we may assume w.l.o.g. that
|u-1|\ll 1
in
2Q\cap Q_{R}
. In particular, by virtue of the non-degeneracy assumption (
5
) in form of (
40
), we know that

W(u)\sim|u-1|^{2\kappa}\quad\text{and}\quad W^{\prime}(u)\sim|u-1|^{2\kappa-1}\quad{\rm in}~2Q\cap Q_{R}

if
\delta
is chosen small enough. Furthermore, we may assume that
p>2
is close to two. Hence, by Sobolev embedding applied to
(u-1)^{2\kappa-1}
, we get

\left(\int_{2Q\cap Q_{R}}|W^{\prime}(u)|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim_{p}\left(\int_{2Q\cap Q_{R}}|(u-1)^{2\kappa-1}|^{2}+|\nabla(u-1)^{2\kappa-1}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

But since
|u-1|\ll 1
and
2(2\kappa-1)\geq 2\kappa
, we can conclude

(85)

\left(\int_{2Q\cap Q_{R}}|W^{\prime}(u)|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim\left(\int_{2Q\cap Q_{R}}|\nabla u|^{2}+W(u)\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\lesssim\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u,2Q\cap Q_{R})^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Note that if
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u,2Q\cap Q_{R})\geq\delta
, we may appeal to the boundedness of
W^{\prime}(u)
to see that the above estimate is still true. In combination with (
84
), (
85
) yields our claim.
∎

Proof of Lemma
5
.

As announced earlier, the same proof as for Lemma
3
applies: the Caccioppli inequalites (
78
) and (
79
) also hold for
(u,q)
replaced by
(\bar{u},u)
, so that the conclusions from Step 2 and 3 within the proof of Lemma
3
also apply.
∎

Finally, we establish local Schauder estimates for the constant-coefficient equation. Note that the assumption on the boundary conditions is redundant but simplifies the proof. We use it to appeal to the clearing-out property, see Lemma
4
.

Proof of Lemma
6
.

Fix
0<\varrho<\varrho^{\prime}<\varrho^{\prime\prime}<1
. The De Giorgi-Nash-Moser estimate, cf. Theorem 8.24 in
[
36
]
, on
\bar{u}-\fint_{Q}\bar{u}
yields

\displaystyle\sup_{\varrho^{\prime}Q}|\bar{u}-\fint_{Q}\bar{u}|+[\bar{u}]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\varrho^{\prime}Q)}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{\varrho^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime\prime}}\left(\int_{\varrho^{\prime\prime}Q}|\bar{u}-\fint_{Q}\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sup_{\varrho^{\prime\prime}Q}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sup_{\varrho^{\prime\prime}Q}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|

for some
0<\alpha<1
. Furthermore, the classical Schauder estimate, cf. Corollary 6.3 in
[
36
]
, applied to
\bar{u}-\fint_{Q}\bar{u}
reads

\sup_{\varrho Q}(|\nabla\bar{u}|+|\nabla^{2}\bar{u}|)\lesssim_{\varrho,\varrho^{\prime}}\sup_{\varrho^{\prime}Q}(|\bar{u}-\fint_{Q}\bar{u}|+|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|)+[W^{\prime}(\bar{u})]_{C^{0,\alpha}(\varrho^{\prime}Q)}.

Since
W
is
C^{2}
in a neighborhood of
[-1,1]
, the estimates combine to

(86)

\sup_{\varrho Q}(|\nabla\bar{u}|+|\nabla^{2}\bar{u}|)\lesssim_{\varrho,\varrho^{\prime},\varrho^{\prime\prime}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\sup_{\varrho^{\prime\prime}Q}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|.

To conclude Lemma
6
we will perform a bootstrap argument on
W^{\prime}(u)
to obtain the estimate

(87)

\sup_{\varrho^{\prime\prime}Q}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|\lesssim_{\varrho^{\prime\prime}}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}+W(\bar{u})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Evidently, (
86
) and (
87
) imply the claim.

We now set up the iteration that leads to (
87
): Let us first assume that
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(\bar{u},Q)\ll 1
. Hence we can further assume w.l.o.g. that

(88)

W^{\prime}(\bar{u})\sim|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1}\quad{\rm in}~Q,

see (
40
). We select a finite number of radii
\varrho^{\prime\prime}=r_{N+1}<r_{N}<\ldots<r_{1}<r_{0}=1
where
N
denotes the integer satisfying
N\leq\frac{d}{2}<N+1
. Recall the interior Calderón-Zygmund estimate, cf. Theorem 3.7 in
[
53
]
,

\left(\int_{r_{k}Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}\lesssim_{p,k}\left(\int_{r_{k-1}Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}+\bar{u}^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{r_{k-1}Q}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|^{p}\right)^{\frac{1}{p}}

for
2<p<\infty
. Since our equation is in divergence form, we may pass to
u-\fint_{r_{k-1}Q}u
and obtain the estimates

(89)

\left(\int_{r_{k}Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)}}}\lesssim_{p,k}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{r_{k-1}Q}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|^{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)}}}.

Here the sequence of exponents
{p^{*}}^{(k)}
are determined recursively so that the exponent
{p^{*}}^{(k)}
equals the critical Sobolev exponent for
{p^{*}}^{(k-1)}
when
k>0
and the initial value
{p^{*}}^{(0)}=p\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\mathbb{Q}
is chosen such that
\frac{2d}{d+2}<p<2
(the former meaning that
{p^{*}}^{(1)}>2
) and
p(N+1)>d
(which is possible since
d<2(N+1)
). Note that
{p^{*}}^{(k)}=\frac{pd}{d-pk}
.

We observe that since
-1\leq\bar{u}\leq 1
, the Sobolev inequality implies

(90)

\displaystyle\left(\int_{r_{k}Q}(|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1})^{{p^{*}}^{(k)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{{p^{*}}^{(k)}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{k}\left(\int_{r_{k}Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{\max\{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)},2\}}+\int_{r_{k}Q}(|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1})^{\max\{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)},2\}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\max\{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)},2\}}}.

Since
p^{*(k)}>2
for each
k\geq 1
, the equations (
89
) and (
90
) combine with
|W^{\prime}(u)|\sim|u-1|^{2\kappa-1}
, see (
88
), to

\displaystyle\left(\int_{r_{k}Q}(|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1})^{{p^{*}}^{(k)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{{p^{*}}^{(k)}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\lesssim_{k}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{r_{k-1}Q}(|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1})^{\max\{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)},2\}}\right)^{\frac{1}{\max\{{p^{*}}^{(k-1)},2\}}}.

We iterate this equation finitely many times (hence the
k
dependent constant does not blow up) and use the condition
p(N+1)>d
(on the first inequality) and (
89
) (on the second inequality) to obtain

\displaystyle\sup_{r_{N+1}Q}|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{p,N}\left(\int_{r_{N+1}Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{{p^{*}}^{(N)}}+\int_{r_{N+1}Q}(|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1})^{{p^{*}}^{(N)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{{p^{*}}^{(N)}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim_{N}\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{r_{N}Q}(|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1})^{{p^{*}}^{(N)}}\right)^{\frac{1}{{p^{*}}^{(N)}}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{Q}(|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa-1})^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

Since
2(2\kappa-1)\geq 2\kappa
, and
W(u)\sim|u-1|^{2\kappa}
by virtue of (
88
), we conclude

(91)

\displaystyle\sup_{\varrho Q}|W^{\prime}(\bar{u})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}+\left(\int_{Q}|\bar{u}-1|^{2\kappa}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\int_{Q}|\nabla\bar{u}|^{2}+\int_{Q}W(\bar{u})\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}.

As usual, we observe that this estimate is also true if
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u,Q)\geq C
for some uniform constant
C>0
since the left hand side is always bounded.
∎

4.
Averaging

This section completes the proof of Theorem
3
. In this random setting, independent of the cube
Q
, we define

(92)

(\phi_{Q},\sigma_{Q},\bar{a}(Q))=(\phi,\sigma,\bar{a}),

where
(\phi,\sigma)
are the infinite-volume correctors and
\bar{a}
is the homogenized matrix, as in the introduction.

The results of the previous section showed that the energy of a minimizer
u
in the plane-like cell problem (
23
) can be approximated by the homogenized energy of a suitable competitor provided we are able to control the sublinear growth of the correctors
(\phi,\sigma)
associated with the operator
-\nabla\cdot(a\nabla)
and the oscillations of
\theta
. In view of our definition of
(\phi_{Q},\sigma_{Q})
and
\bar{a}(Q)
in this context, this control is phrased in terms of the stationary fields
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
and
{\rm Osc}(\cdot)
defined by

(93)

\displaystyle{\rm Sub}_{x}(r)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\varrho\geq r}\frac{1}{\varrho}\left(\fint_{Q_{\varrho}(x)}|(\phi(y),\sigma(y))-\fint_{Q_{\varrho}(x)}(\phi,\sigma)|^{2}\,dy\right)^{\frac{1}{2}},

(94)

\displaystyle{\rm Osc}_{x}(r)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\varrho\geq r}\varrho^{-1}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{\varrho}(x))}.

In this section, we prove that the needed control over these quantities is indeed furnished by the assumptions (
10
) and (
11
).

In addition to completing the proof of the lower bound in Theorem
1
, we briefly recall the optimal decay rate of
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
and
{\rm Osc}(\cdot)
for the random checkerboard. This last part is expository and intended for readers who may not be experts in quantitative stochastic homogenization.

4.1.
Preliminaries from Homogenization Theory

For the reader’s convenience, we recall the relevant results from homogenization theory that will be needed in what follows. In particular, as we review next, the assumptions of Section
1.1
imply that, on an event of probability one, all of the structural assumptions of Section
3.1
hold. Further, we recall the fact that the stationary fields
{\rm Sub}(R)
and
{\rm Osc}(R)
vanish as
R\to\infty
.

4.1.1.
Helmholtz-type Decomposition.

First, the assumptions of Section
1.1
imply that there is a matrix
\bar{a}
, a random vector field
\phi
, and a random
3
-tensor
\sigma
such that, with probability one, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the function
\phi_{\xi}=\xi_{i}\phi_{i}
and skew-symmetric matrix field
\sigma_{\xi}=\xi_{i}\sigma_{i}
relate to
a(y)\xi
via the formula

(95)

a(y)\xi=\bar{a}\xi-a(y)\nabla\phi_{\xi}(y)+(\nabla\cdot\sigma_{\xi})(y).

Furthermore, the gradient fields
\nabla\phi
and
\nabla\sigma
are stationary with mean zero. For a more precise statement and proof, we refer the reader to the short proof-sketch in
[
41
, Section 1.2]
or, alternatively, the proof of
[
38
, Lemma 1]
.

4.1.2.
Bounds on
\bar{a}
and
\bar{\theta}
.

Concerning the constant
\bar{a}
, recall that, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\displaystyle\xi\cdot\bar{a}\xi

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[a(y)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{\xi}(y))\cdot(\xi+\nabla\phi_{\xi}(y))

\displaystyle=\min_{\nabla\psi}\mathbb{E}[a(y)(\xi+\nabla\psi(y))\cdot(\xi+\nabla\psi(y))],

where the minimum is over stationary gradient fields; see, for instance,
[
40
, Section 7.2]
. Since
\lambda\text{Id}\leq a\leq\Lambda\text{Id}
pointwise by the assumptions of Section
1.1
, and the minimizer is
\nabla\psi\equiv 0
if
a
is replaced by a constant, we deduce that
\lambda\text{Id}\leq\bar{a}\leq\Lambda\text{Id}
.

Recall from the introduction that we define
\bar{\theta}=\mathbb{E}[\theta(y)]
. Since the pointwise bounds
\theta_{*}\leq\theta\leq\theta^{*}
are imposed in Section
1.1
, it follows that
\theta_{*}\leq\bar{\theta}\leq\theta^{*}
.

4.1.3.
Sublinearity and Oscillations

Next, let us recall that, since the action
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is stationary and ergodic, we know that, for any
\nu>0
,

(96)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(r)>\nu\right\}=0,

(97)

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Osc}_{0}(r)>\nu\right\}=0.

The first is classical and follows from the fact that
\nabla\phi
is stationary and mean-zero, see, for instance,
[
38
, Section 3.4]
for the proof. The second follows directly from the fact that, by the ergodic theorem,
\theta(\gamma^{-1}\cdot)\rightharpoonup\bar{\theta}
weakly in
L^{2}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
as
\gamma\to 0
. For the fact that weak convergence follows from the ergodic theorem, see
[
40
, Section 7.1]
. Alternatively, both this and the (strong)
H^{-1}
-convergence are stated and proved in detail in
[
4
, Corollary 1.9]
.

4.2.
Dilatational and Rotational Invariance

In Section
3
, we proved that the energy in the planar cell problems (
23
) converges provided the phase transition occurs across the hyperplane with normal vector
e_{1}
. In fact, the argument applies if
e_{1}
is replaced by any unit vector
e\in S^{d-1}
. To see that, it is necessary to argue that the assumptions (
10
) and (
11
) remain true after rotation, as asserted in Proposition
2
.

It will first be useful to verify that the assumptions are invariant under dilations rather than rotations. That is the aim of the next proposition.

Proposition 7
.

If (
10
) and (
11
) hold, then, for any
\alpha,\nu>0
,

(98)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-d}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\alpha\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}

\displaystyle=0,

(99)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-d}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Osc}_{0}\left(\frac{\alpha\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}

\displaystyle=0.

Proof.

If
\alpha\geq 1
, then this is trivial since, for any function
F
,
\max_{r\geq S}F(r)
decreases as a function of
S
. Thus, fix
\alpha<1
from here on.

Given any
r>0
, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(\alpha r)^{2}}\fint_{Q_{\alpha r}}|(\phi,\sigma)-\fint_{Q_{\alpha r}}(\phi,\sigma)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{(\alpha r)^{2}}\fint_{Q_{\alpha r}}|(\phi,\sigma)-\fint_{Q_{r}}(\phi,\sigma)|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\alpha^{2+d}r^{2}}\fint_{Q_{r}}|(\phi,\sigma)-\fint_{Q_{r}}(\phi,\sigma)|^{2},

which after taking the supremum
r\geq R
shows
{\rm Sub}_{0}(\alpha R)\leq\alpha^{-\frac{d+2}{2}}{\rm Sub}_{0}(R)
. Thus, setting
R=\delta^{-1}\epsilon
, we find

\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\alpha\epsilon}{\delta}\right)>\nu\right\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta}\right)>\alpha^{\frac{d+2}{2}}\nu\right\},

which proves (
98
) follows from (
10
).

A completely analogous argument applies to
{\rm Osc}_{0}
. Indeed, observe that

\|f\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{\alpha r})}\leq\alpha^{-\frac{d}{2}}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{r})}\quad\text{for each}\quad f\in L^{2}_{\rm loc}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

To see this, let us fix
v\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{\alpha r})
, so that the function
\tilde{v}=\alpha^{-\frac{d}{2}}v\boldsymbol{1}_{Q_{\alpha r}}
is in
H^{1}_{0}(Q_{r})
and satisfies

\displaystyle\fint_{Q_{r}}|\nabla\tilde{v}|^{2}=\fint_{Q_{\alpha r}}|\nabla v|^{2}\quad{\rm and}\quad\fint_{Q_{\alpha r}}fv=\alpha^{-\frac{d}{2}}\fint_{Q_{r}}f\tilde{v}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\|f\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{\alpha r})}\leq\alpha^{-\frac{d}{2}}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{r})},\quad{\rm and~therefore}\quad{\rm Osc}_{0}(\alpha R)\leq\alpha^{-\frac{d}{2}}{\rm Osc}_{0}(R).

As in the case of
{\rm Sub}_{0}(\cdot)
, we conclude that (
99
) follows from (
11
).
∎

Finally, we prove rotational invariance. To make the discussion precise, it will be convenient to introduce some notation. Given any orthogonal transformation
\mathcal{O}
and
r>0
, define
Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r}
by

(100)

Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r}=\mathcal{O}(Q_{r}).

We denote by
{\rm Sub}^{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
and
{\rm Osc}^{\mathcal{O}}(\cdot)
the functions defined analogously to
{\rm Sub}(\cdot)
and
{\rm Osc}(\cdot)
, see (
93
) and (
94
), but with rotated cubes in place of the standard ones.

Proposition 8
.

If the assumptions (
10
) and (
11
) hold, then, for any orthogonal transformation
\mathcal{O}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
\alpha>0
,

(101)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-d}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}^{\mathcal{O}}_{0}\left(\frac{\alpha\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}

\displaystyle=0,

(102)

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-d}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Osc}^{\mathcal{O}}_{0}\left(\frac{\alpha\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}

\displaystyle=0.

Proof.

Fix an orthogonal transformation
\mathcal{O}
and let
r>0
. Since
Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r}
contains the origin in its interior, there is a
C>1
, which is independent of
r
, such that
Q_{C^{-1}r}\subseteq Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r}\subseteq Q_{Cr}
. Thus, arguing as in the previous proposition, we deduce
{\rm Sub}_{0}^{\mathcal{O}}(R)\leq C^{\frac{d}{2}+1}{\rm Sub}_{0}(R)
, so that the previous proposition and (
10
) imply (
101
).

Similarly, arguments similar to those in the previous proof show that if
f\in L^{2}(Q_{Cr})
, then

\|f\|_{H^{-1}(Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{r})}\leq C^{\frac{d}{2}}\|f\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{Cr})}\quad{\rm and~thus}\quad{\rm Osc}_{0}^{\mathcal{O}}(r)\leq C^{\frac{d}{2}}{\rm Osc}_{0}(Cr).

As before, (
11
) implies (
102
). ∎

Finally, here is the proof of Proposition
2
for completeness:

Proof of Proposition
2
.

Fix an orthogonal transformation
\mathcal{O}\in O(d)
. It is immediate to check that the rotated coefficients
a^{\mathcal{O}}
and
\theta^{\mathcal{O}}
satisfy the assumptions of Section
1.1
with the action
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
replaced by the rotated action
(\tau^{\mathcal{O}}_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
.

By the previous proposition (Proposition
8
), the rotated medium
(a^{\mathcal{O}},\theta^{\mathcal{O}})
satisfies the assumptions (
10
) and (
11
) if and only if
(a,\theta)
does.

Finally, by manipulating the variational formula in Section
4.1.2
, one readily deduces that
\bar{a}^{\mathcal{O}}=\mathcal{O}^{-1}\bar{a}\mathcal{O}
. Further, we immediately deduce that
\bar{\theta}^{\mathcal{O}}=\mathbb{E}[\theta^{\mathcal{O}}(0)]=\mathbb{E}[\theta(0)]=\bar{\theta}
. ∎

4.3.
Proof of Theorem
3

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem
3
. To get an idea why assumptions (
10
) and (
11
) are the correct ones, recall from (
24
) that, writing
R=\epsilon^{-1}
and
\gamma(R)=\delta(\epsilon)\epsilon^{-1}
for the macroscopic and microscopic length scales after mesoscopic rescaling, we can invoke dilatational invariance (Proposition
7
) to find

(103)

\lim_{R\to\infty}R^{d}\,\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1})>\nu\}=0,\quad\lim_{R\to\infty}R^{d}\,\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Osc}_{0}(r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1})>\nu\}=0.

By ergodicity, if
E_{R},F_{R}\subseteq Q_{R}
are the subsets defined by

E_{R}=\{x\in Q_{R}\,\mid\,{\rm Sub}_{x}(r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1})>\nu\},\quad F_{R}=\{x\in Q_{R}\,\mid\,{\rm Osc}_{x}(r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1})>\nu\},

then

|E_{R}|\approx R^{d}\,\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(\gamma(R)^{-1})>\nu\}\quad\text{and}\quad|F_{R}|\approx R^{d}\,\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Osc}_{0}(\gamma(R)^{-1})>\nu\},

and, thus, (
103
) suggests that
E_{R}
and
F_{R}
are negligible. For all intents and purposes, this puts us in a situation in which we have uniform control of the homogenization error in the entire box
Q_{R}
(cf. (
38
)).

The proof given next closely follows the previous heuristic discussion.

Proof of Theorem
3
.

Suppose that the scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
satisfies (
10
) and (
11
). We want to show that, for any
\varrho>0
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;Q_{\varrho}(x))\,\mid\,u-q(\epsilon^{-1}(\cdot-x)\cdot e_{1})\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{\varrho}(x))\right\}\geq\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})

To be precise, we prove this holds in probability.

Given
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
S>0
, and a functional
\mathscr{F}
, define
m(\mathscr{F},Q_{S}(x),q)
by

(104)

\displaystyle m(\mathscr{F},Q_{S}(x),q)

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\mathscr{F}(u;Q_{S}(x))\,\mid\,u-q((\cdot-x)\cdot e_{1})\in H^{1}_{0}(Q_{S}(x))\right\}.

Changing variables by setting
R=\epsilon^{-1}
and
\gamma(R)=\delta(\epsilon)\epsilon^{-1}
(cf. (
24
)), what we seek to prove becomes

\liminf_{R\to\infty}R^{1-d}\,m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)},Q_{\varrho R}(x),q)\geq\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})\quad\text{in probability.}

Note that, by stationarity (since we are only interested in convergence in probability), it suffices to assume
x=0
.

In order to appeal to Theorem
5
, assume that
R\geq r_{c}\varrho^{-1}
and define
K(R)\in\mathbb{N}
in such a way that

(105)

(2(K(R)-1)+1)r_{c}\leq\varrho R\leq(2K(R)+1)r_{c}.

At this stage, it will be convenient to define radii
S_{+}(R)
and
S_{-}(R)
by
S_{-}(R)=(2(K(R)-1)+1)r_{c}
and
S_{+}(R)=(2K(R)+1)r_{c}
. By the definition of
K(R)
, we have
Q_{S_{-}(R)}\subseteq Q_{\varrho R}\subseteq Q_{S_{+}(R)}
.

By a monotonicity argument similar to
[
49
, Proposition 5]
, for any
\mathscr{F}\in\{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)},\overline{\mathscr{F}}\}
, we have that

(106)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} m(\mathscr{F},Q_{S_{+}(R)},q)-E(R)&\leq m(\mathscr{F},Q_{\varrho R},q)\\
&\leq m(\mathscr{F},Q_{S_{-}(R)},q)+E(R),\end{aligned}

where
E:(0,\infty)\to(0,\infty)
is a deterministic function such that
R^{1-d}E(R)\to 0
as
R\to\infty
.

Next, for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
, let
u_{K}:Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}}\to[-1,1]
be such that

m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)},Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}},q)=\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)}(u_{K};Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}}),

and
u_{K}(y)=q(y\cdot e_{1})
for
y\in\partial Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}}
. Since
u_{K}
is a minimizer, observe that if
v
is the function
v(y)=q(y\cdot e_{1})
, then

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)}(u_{K};Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}}}\left(\frac{1}{2}a(\gamma^{-1}y)Dv(y)\cdot Dv(y)+\theta(\gamma^{-1}y)W(v(y))\right)\,dy

and, thus, by slicing and (
19
),
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)}(u_{K};Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}})\leq C((2K+1)r_{c})^{d-1}
, where the constant
C
depends on
q
,
\Lambda
,
\theta^{*}
, and
W
, but not on
K
.

In what follows, let
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
be the modulus of continuity from Theorem
5
and let
\{X_{\gamma}(z)\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
be the random variables

\displaystyle X_{\gamma}(z)=\omega\left({\rm Sub}_{r_{c}z}\left(\frac{r_{c}}{\gamma}\right)+{\rm Osc}_{r_{c}z}\left(\frac{r_{c}}{\gamma}\right)\right).

Fix
\nu>0
. Using
u_{K}
as the minimizer in Theorem
5
, for any
K\in\mathbb{N}
and
\gamma>0
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}},q)<m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},Q_{(2K+1)r_{c}},q)-\nu((2K+1)r_{c})^{d-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{C\max_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}X_{\gamma}(z)>\nu\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}\cap[-K,K]^{d}}\mathbb{P}\{X_{\gamma}(z)>C^{-1}\nu\}=(2K+1)^{d}\,\mathbb{P}\{X_{\gamma}(0)>C^{-1}\nu\}.

Setting
\gamma=\gamma(R)
and
K=K(R)
with
K(R)
determined by (
105
), we invoke the definitions of
X_{\gamma}
,
S_{+}(R)
,
R
, and
\gamma(R)
to obtain, for
R\geq\varrho^{-1}r_{c}
, the error estimate

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)},Q_{S_{+}(R)},q)<m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},Q_{S_{+}(R)},q)-\nu S_{+}(R)^{d-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\leq(r_{c}^{-1}S_{+}(R))^{d}\,\mathbb{P}\{X_{\gamma(R)}(0)>C^{-1}\nu\}

\displaystyle\quad\leq 3^{d}r_{c}^{-d}\varrho^{d}\cdot R^{d}\,\mathbb{P}\{X_{\gamma(R)}(0)>C^{-1}\nu\}

\displaystyle\quad=3^{d}r_{c}^{-d}\varrho^{d}\cdot\epsilon^{-d}\,\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}r_{c}\right)+{\rm Osc}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}r_{c}\right)>\omega^{-1}\left(C^{-1}\nu\right)\right\},

where
\omega^{-1}
is any fixed left-inverse of
\omega
.
Invoking our assumptions (
10
) and (
11
) on
\delta
in the form of (
103
), we combine the previous string of inequalities with (
106
) to find

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\{R^{1-d}m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)},Q_{\varrho R},q)<R^{1-d}m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},Q_{\varrho R},q)-\nu\}=0.

At the same time, since
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
has constant coefficients, it is well-known (see
[
2
, Theorem 3.7]
) that

\lim_{R\to\infty}R^{1-d}m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},Q_{\varrho R},q)=\bar{\sigma}(e_{1}).

Putting it all together, we conclude that, for any
\nu>0
,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{R^{1-d}m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)},Q_{\varrho R},q)<\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})-\nu\right\}=0.

∎

4.4.
Proof of Corollary
3

As advertised in the introduction, if we work with sequences
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(\delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and ask for slightly better decay of
\delta
relative to
\epsilon
, we can upgrade from convergence in probability to almost-sure convergence. The proof is a more-or-less standard modification of the proof of Theorem
3
involving the Borel-Cantelli Lemma, hence, in the proof that follows, only the relevant changes are described.

Proof of Corollary
3
.

The argument proceeds as in the proof of Theorem
3
. Let
R_{j}=\epsilon_{j}^{-1}
and
\gamma_{j}=\delta_{j}\epsilon_{j}^{-1}
. As before, it is convenient to define
K_{j}\in\mathbb{N}
such that

(2(K_{j}-1)+1)r_{c}\leq\varrho R_{j}\leq(2K_{j}+1)r_{c}.

Let
\nu>0
. The key observation is that, as in the proof of Theorem
3
, we can write

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma_{j}},Q_{(2K_{j}+1)r_{c}}(R_{j}x),q)<m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},Q_{(2K_{j}+1)r_{c}}(R_{j}x),q)-\nu((2K_{j}+1)r_{c})^{d-1}\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq(2K_{j}+1)^{d}\mathbb{P}\{X_{\gamma_{j}}(0)>C^{-1}\nu\}

\displaystyle\qquad\leq 3^{d}r_{c}^{-d}\varrho^{d}\cdot\epsilon_{j}^{-d}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{j}}{\delta_{j}}r_{c}\right)+{\rm Osc}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon_{j}}{\delta_{j}}r_{c}\right)>\omega^{-1}\left(\frac{\nu}{C}\right)\right\}

and, thus, by (
12
),

\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}\left\{\begin{aligned} &m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma_{j}},Q_{(2K_{j}+1)r_{c}}(R_{j}x),q)\\
&\qquad<m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},Q_{(2K_{j}+1)r_{c}}(R_{j}x),q)-\nu((2K_{j}+1)r_{c})^{d-1}\end{aligned}\right\}<\infty.

Therefore, by the Borel-Cantelli Lemma, with probability one,
