\liminf_{j\to\infty}((2K_{j}+1)r_{c})^{1-d}m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma_{j}},Q_{K_{j}r_{c}}(R_{j}x),q)\geq\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})-\nu.

Since
\nu>0
was arbitrary, this concludes the proof.
∎

4.5.
Proof of Proposition
1

The previous results show that homogenization holds conditional on a decay assumption on the microscale
\delta
. We now prove that, as long as the medium
(a,\theta)
is stationary and ergodic, there is always a choice of scales satisfying this assumption.

Proof of Proposition
1
.

Since (
96
) and (
97
) both hold, for any
j\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a scale
R_{j}\geq j
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(R_{j})>2^{-j}\right\}\leq 2^{-j},

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Osc}_{0}(R_{j})>2^{-j}\right\}\leq 2^{-j}.

Define
\delta_{*,{\rm Sub}}
via the following rule:

\delta_{*,{\rm Sub}}(\epsilon)=\epsilon R_{j}^{-1}\quad\text{for each}\quad 2^{-(j+1)/2d}\leq\epsilon<2^{-j/2d}.

Suppose that
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
is any choice of scale such that
\delta(\epsilon)\leq\delta_{*,{\rm Sub}}(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon
close enough to zero. Given any
\nu>0
, if
2^{-(j+1)/2d}\leq\epsilon<2^{-j/2d}<\nu^{\frac{1}{2d}}
, then
\epsilon\delta(\epsilon)^{-1}\geq R_{j}
and, therefore,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{-d}

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}\leq 2^{(j+1)/2}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>2^{-j}\right\}\leq\sqrt{2}\cdot 2^{-j/2}.

This proves that

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-d}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Sub}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}=0.

Via analogous arguments, we define a scale
\delta_{*,{\rm Osc}}
in such a way that if
\delta(\epsilon)\leq\delta_{*,{\rm Osc}}(\epsilon)
for all
\epsilon
in a neighborhood of zero, then

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\epsilon^{-d}\mathbb{P}\left\{{\rm Osc}_{0}\left(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)}\right)>\nu\right\}=0.

We conclude by setting
\delta_{*}(\epsilon)=\min\{\delta_{*,{\rm Sub}}(\epsilon),\delta_{*,{\rm Osc}}(\epsilon)\}
. ∎

4.6.
Quantitative Sublinearity of Correctors in the Random Checkerboard

In this section, we recall the optimal decay rate of the limit (
96
) in the case when
a
is a random checkerboard as in the discussion of Section
1.3
.

Specifically, it follows from the main results of
[
38
]
that, for any
\nu>0
, there is a constant
c(\nu)>0
such that

(107)

\displaystyle-r^{-d}\log\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(r)>\nu\}\geq c(\nu)\quad\text{for any}\,\,r\geq 1.

As sketched in
[
38
, Remark 5]
, provided
a
is in fact nonconstant, it is possible to prove that the matching upper bound also holds, at least for small enough
\nu
. That is, for any
\nu>0
small enough,

(108)

\displaystyle-r^{-d}\log\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(r)>\nu\}\leq c(\nu)^{-1}\quad\text{for any}\,\,r\geq 1.

A proof of this fact for a specific choice of the variables
\{A_{z}\}_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}
can be found in the monograph
[
3
, Section 3.6]
. Below, for the reader’s convenience, we give a short proof that it holds for
any
(nonconstant) uniformly elliptic random checkerboard.

Remark 6
.

The work
[
38
]
considers stationary uniformly elliptic matrix fields, obtaining quantitative results under the assumption that certain functional inequalities hold. This applies, in particular, to the random checkerboard. To be precise, if
a
is the random checkerboard considered here, then, by
[
31
, Proposition 2.3]
and its proof, there is a
\gamma_{*}=\gamma_{*}(d)>0
such that the rescaled field
a^{\gamma_{*}}=a(\gamma_{*}^{-1}\cdot)
satisfies the standard logarithmic Sobolev inequality with respect to the essential oscillation
\partial^{\text{osc}}
, as defined in
[
38
, Definition 1]
. Therefore,
[
38
, Theorem 2]
and its proof apply to the rescaled field. Since rescaling the field has the effect of replacing
R\mapsto{\rm Sub}_{0}(R)
by
R\mapsto{\rm Sub}_{0}(\gamma^{-1}_{*}R)
, it does not effect the qualitative statement (
107
).

Concerning the large deviations lower bound (
107
), this follows from the proof of
[
38
, Theorem 2]
. In particular, the proof in
[
38
]
begins by establishing that there is a deterministic exponent
\mu>0
and a random variable
r_{**}>0
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\exp(C^{-1}r_{**}^{d})]<\infty\quad\text{for some}\,\,C=C(d,\lambda,\Lambda)>0

\displaystyle\text{and}\quad\frac{1}{R}\left(\fint_{B_{R}}\left|(\phi,\sigma)-\fint_{B_{R}}(\phi,\sigma)\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\leq C\left(\frac{r_{**}}{R}\right)^{\mu}\quad\text{for each}\,\,R\geq r_{**},

see
[
38
, Proof of Theorem 2 & Proposition 2]
, which yields a constant of the form
c(\nu)\sim\min\{1,\nu^{\frac{d}{\mu}}\}
in (
107
) after applying Markov’s inequality.

Remark 7
.

The bound (
107
) can also be obtained using the approach exposed in the recent monograph
[
4
, Chapters 4 & 6]
. Like
[
38
]
, the argument uses the notion of a random scale analogous to the minimal radius
r_{*}(\nu)
(see (
8
) above), above which homogenization effects become apparent. (To the best of our knowledge, this idea first appears in
[
6
]
, which introduced some of the techniques used in
[
4
]
.)

We next give a short proof of the large deviations upper bound (
108
).

Proof of the Upper Bound (
108
).

In the proof, it is convenient to take
a
of the form
a(x)=\sum_{z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}A_{z}\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{Q}(z)}(x)
, where
\tilde{Q}(z)=\prod_{j=1}^{d}[z_{j},z_{j}+1)
for
z=(z_{1},\dots,z_{d})
, which is no loss of generality since it is a translation of the field given in (
15
). Since
a
is nonconstant, we can choose two distinct points
A_{\star}\neq A_{\bullet}
in the support of the law of
a(0)
. Let
\|\cdot\|
be some norm on the space of
d\times d
matrices. For a given
\alpha:\{1,\dots,d\}\to\{-1,1\}
, let
H_{\alpha}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
be the orthant consisting of points
x
with
x_{j}=|x_{j}|\alpha_{j}
for each
j
. Define a deterministic matrix field
\underline{a}:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\text{Sym}(d)
by

\displaystyle\underline{a}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}A_{\star},&\text{if}\,\,x\in H_{\alpha}\,\,\text{for some}\,\,\alpha\,\,\text{with}\,\,|\{j\,\mid\,\alpha_{j}=1\}|\,\,\text{even},\\
A_{\bullet},&\text{if}\,\,x\in H_{\alpha}\,\,\text{for some}\,\,\alpha\,\,\text{with}\,\,|\{j\,\mid\,\alpha_{j}=1\}|\,\,\text{odd}.\end{array}\right.

Since for any
\epsilon>0
, the probabilities
\mathbb{P}\{\|A_{z}-A_{\star}\|<\epsilon\}
and
\mathbb{P}\{\|A_{z}-A_{\bullet}\|<\epsilon\}
are positive and independent of
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, and for any
z\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
we have
\tilde{Q}(z)\subseteq H_{\alpha}
for some
\alpha
, there is a constant
c(\epsilon)>0
such that if
E_{\epsilon}(\gamma)
is the event

\displaystyle E_{\epsilon}(\gamma)=\{\|a^{\gamma}(x)-\underline{a}(x)\|<\epsilon\,\,\text{for a.e.}\,\,x\in Q_{1}\},

then

(109)

\displaystyle-\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{d}\log\mathbb{P}(E_{\epsilon}(\gamma))=c(\epsilon).

At the same time, we know that, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, with probability one,

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot a^{\gamma}(x)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{\xi}^{\gamma})=0\quad\text{in}\,\,Q_{1}.

Thus, if we define the space of matrix fields
\mathcal{A}(\epsilon)
and the constant
M(\epsilon,\xi)
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{A}(\epsilon)=\{b:Q_{1}\to\text{Sym}(d)\,\mid\,\|b(x)-\underline{a}(x)\|<\epsilon\,\,\text{for a.e.}\,\,x\in Q_{1}\},

\displaystyle\nu(\epsilon,\xi)=\inf\left\{\big(\int_{Q_{1}}|v|^{2}\,dx\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\mid\,(v,b)\in H^{1}(Q_{1})\times\mathcal{A}(\epsilon),\,\,-\nabla\cdot b(\xi+\nabla v)=0\,\,\text{in}\,\,Q_{1}\right\},

then, by scaling,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(\gamma^{-1})\geq\nu(\epsilon,\xi)\}\geq\mathbb{P}\left\{\left(\int_{Q_{1}}|\phi_{\xi}^{\gamma}-\fint_{Q_{1}}\phi_{\xi}^{\gamma}|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\geq\nu(\epsilon,\xi)\right\}\geq\mathbb{P}(E_{\epsilon}(\gamma)).

In view of (
109
), it only remains to observe that there is a
\xi_{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
such that

(110)

\displaystyle\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\nu(\epsilon,\xi_{*})>0,

so that the set
\{\epsilon>0\,\mid\,\nu(\epsilon,\xi_{*})>0\}
is nonempty and the bound obtained above is thus nontrivial. This last claim we prove by contrapositive.

Indeed, if for some
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
we have that
\nu(\epsilon,\xi)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
, then this implies that the equation
-\nabla\cdot\underline{a}(x)\xi=0
holds in the distributional sense in
Q_{1}
. By definition of
\underline{a}
, this implies that the measure

\displaystyle n_{M}\cdot(A_{\star}-A_{\bullet})\xi\,\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\lfloor_{M}=0\quad\text{in}\,\,Q_{1},

where
M=\{(x_{1},\dots,x_{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,x_{j}=0\,\,\text{for some}\,\,j\}
,
n_{M}
is the normal vector to
M
, and
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}\lfloor_{M}
is the
(d-1)
-dimensional Hausdorff measure on
M
. Since each of the standard coordinate vectors is attained by
n_{M}
in a set of positive
\mathcal{H}^{d-1}
measure, this last identity implies that

\displaystyle(A_{\star}-A_{\bullet})\xi=0.

Thus,
\nu(\epsilon,\xi)
can only vanish in the limit
\epsilon\to 0
if
A_{\star}\xi=A_{\bullet}\xi
. Since
A_{\star}\neq A_{\bullet}
, it is possible to find a
\xi_{*}\neq 0
such that
A_{\star}\xi_{*}\neq A_{\bullet}\xi_{*}
, and (
110
) follows.
∎

In view of (
107
) and (
108
), for the random checkerboard, the assumption (
10
) in our main theorem amounts to requiring that
\epsilon^{-d}\exp(-C(\frac{\epsilon}{\delta(\epsilon)})^{d})
vanishes as
\epsilon\to 0
for any
C>0
, or, equivalently,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\frac{\delta(\epsilon)|\log(\epsilon)|^{1/d}}{\epsilon}=0.

In the next subsection, we will see that this same sufficient condition applies to our other assumption (
11
) when the field
\theta
is a random checkerboard. The main result of the companion paper
[
48
]
shows this is also a necessary condition when
d=1
. We expect that the higher dimensional random checkerboard is fundamentally different; this will be treated in future work.

Remark 8
.

More generally, the results of
[
38
]
and
[
4
]
provide estimates on the decay of
\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Sub}_{0}(R)>\nu\}
provided the law of
a
satisfies certain mixing conditions, which are specified in both cases using functional derivatives analogous to the one from Malliavin calculus. In that context, the estimates from
[
38
,
4
]
can be used to specify sufficient conditions for the assumption (
10
) to hold.

4.7.
Quantitative Averaging of the Random Checkerboard

Let us next consider the case when
\theta
is the random checkerboard as in (
15
). We claim that, analogous to the previous discussion, for any
\nu>0
small enough, there is a constant
C_{\nu}\geq 1
such that

(111)

C_{\nu}^{-1}\leq-r^{-d}\log\mathbb{P}\{{\rm Osc}_{0}(r)>\nu\}\leq C_{\nu}\quad\text{for any}\quad r\geq 1.

Since we are not aware of a reference for this relatively elementary large deviations estimate, we provide a proof below.

In fact, we expect that the i.i.d. assumption used here is stronger than what is needed to obtain the lower bound in (
111
). We comment on this in the proof.

In the proof below, we assume for notational convenience that
\theta_{*}
is the essential infimum of
\theta(x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof of (
111
).

By the definition of
{\rm Osc}_{0}(\cdot)
and a union bound, it suffices to establish that

\displaystyle 0<

\displaystyle-\limsup_{r\to\infty}r^{-d}\log\left(\mathbb{P}\{r^{-1}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{r})}>\nu\}\right),

\displaystyle-\liminf_{r\to\infty}r^{-d}\log\left(\mathbb{P}\{r^{-1}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{r})}>\nu\}\right)<\infty.

Toward this end, it is somewhat convenient to rescale, writing
\theta^{\gamma}(x)=\theta(\gamma^{-1}x)
, in which case the statement becomes

(112)

\displaystyle 0<

\displaystyle-\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{d}\log\left(\mathbb{P}\{\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{1})}>\nu\}\right),

(113)

\displaystyle-\liminf_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{d}\log\left(\mathbb{P}\{\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{1})}>\nu\}\right)<\infty.

To see that (
112
) holds, let
(e_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}
be the trigonometric orthonormal system of eigenfunctions of the Laplacian with Dirichlet boundary conditions, so that
-\Delta e_{n}=\pi^{2}|n|^{2}e_{n}
and
\int_{Q_{1}}e_{n}(x)e_{m}(x)\,dx=\delta_{nm}
, and recall that

(114)

\displaystyle\|\theta^{\gamma}-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{1})}^{2}=\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d}}\frac{1}{\pi^{2}|n|^{2}}\left|\int_{Q_{1}}(\theta^{\gamma}(x)-\bar{\theta})e_{n}(x)\,dx\right|^{2}.

On the one hand, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
,

(115)

\displaystyle\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\,:\,|n|\geq N}\frac{1}{4\pi^{2}|n|^{2}}\left|\int_{Q_{1}}(\theta^{\gamma}(x)-\bar{\theta})e_{n}(x)\,dx\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-2}\int_{Q_{1}}|\theta^{\gamma}(x)-\bar{\theta}|^{2}\,dx

\displaystyle\lesssim N^{-2}(\theta^{*}-\theta_{*})^{2}.

On the other hand, since
\theta
is the random checkerboard, the standard large deviations estimate for sums of bounded, independent random variables implies that, for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
and any
\nu>0
, there is a constant
C_{\nu,n}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|\int_{Q_{1}}(\theta^{\gamma}(x)-\bar{\theta})e_{n}(x)\,dx\right|>\nu\right\}\leq\exp\left(-C_{\nu,n}\gamma^{-d}\right).

see, for instance,
[
25
, Chapter 2]
or
[
54
, Section 2.4, Example 4]
.
This is the only place in the proof of (
112
) where we use the fact that
\theta
is a random checkerboard. We expect this would still hold provided that the law of
\theta
satisfies a logarithmic Sobolev inequality in a suitable sense; see
[
30
, Proposition 1.7]
for related results in this direction.

Taking
N\gtrsim(\theta^{*}-\theta_{*})\nu^{-\frac{1}{2}}
and combining the last estimate with (
115
), we conclude

-\limsup_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{d}\log\left(\mathbb{P}\left\{\|\theta(\gamma^{-1}\cdot)-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{1})}>\nu\right\}\right)\geq\sum_{n\in\mathbb{N}^{d}\,:\,|n|<N}C_{\nu/2,n}>0,

which proves (
112
).

Finally, regarding (
113
), it suffices to notice that since
\theta_{*}
is the essential infimum of
\theta(x)
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the definition of
\theta
implies that, for any
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\theta}-\theta_{*})
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\gamma^{d}\log\mathbb{P}\{\theta^{\gamma}(x)\leq\theta_{*}+\epsilon\,\,\text{for each}\,\,x\in Q_{1}\}=\log\mathbb{P}\{\theta(0)\leq\theta_{*}+\epsilon\}>-\infty.

Let
\mu=\int_{Q_{1}}e_{(1,1,\dots,1)}(x)\,dx
be the total mass of the principal eigenfunction
e_{(1,1,\dots,1)}
. Since the principle eigenfunction is positive, for any
\epsilon\in(0,\bar{\theta}-\theta_{*})
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\{\theta^{\gamma}\leq\theta_{*}+\epsilon\,\,\text{ in}\,\,Q_{1}\}\leq\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|\int_{Q_{1}}(\theta^{\gamma}(x)-\bar{\theta})e_{(1,1,\dots,1)}(x)\,dx\right|\geq\mu(\bar{\theta}-\theta_{*}-\epsilon)\right\}.

In view of (
114
), this implies (
113
) for any
\nu>0
small enough. ∎

5.
Periodic and (Uniformly) Almost Periodic Media

This section discusses the relevant adaptations of the arguments of Section
4
to the case when the coefficients
a
and
\theta
are both either periodic or in the class
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Recall that functions in
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
are sometimes called
uniformly almost periodic.

The main aim of this section is to prove Corollary
2
, which shows that if
a
and
\theta
are periodic or uniformly almost periodic, then homogenization determines the limiting behavior of the energy
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}
, no matter the choice of the scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
(as long as
\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)\rightarrow 0
). In order to do this, we need to show that the quantities
{\rm Osc}(Q_{R}(x))
,
{\rm Sub}(Q_{R}(x))
, and
|\bar{a}(Q_{R}(x))-\bar{a}|
appearing in Theorem
5
converge uniformly to zero as
R\to\infty
in this setting, which is the main focus of Sections
5.1
and
5.2
.

It should be emphasized that there are nonuniformly almost periodic media for which the homogenized functional
\bar{\mathscr{E}}
is not necessarily the
\Gamma
-limit of the energy
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}
for certain choices of the scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
. This is proved in the companion paper
[
48
]
.

5.1.
Periodic Media

Let us recall the relevant facts from periodic homogenization, which imply, in particular, that the assumptions of Section
3.1
all hold. As in the random case, we work with correctors
(\phi_{Q},\sigma_{Q})=(\phi,\sigma)
and matrices
\bar{a}(Q)=\bar{a}
that do not depend on the cube
Q
.

First, if
a
is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic, then, for any
\xi\in S^{d-1}
, recall that it is possible to fix a
\phi_{\xi}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
such that

-\nabla\cdot(a(x)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{\xi}))=0\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{T}^{d},\quad\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\phi_{\xi}=0.

Indeed, the existence and uniqueness of such
\phi_{\xi}
is discussed in detail in the classic books
[
10
]
and
[
40
]
, and
\xi\mapsto\phi_{\xi}
is linear by linearity of the equation. Observe that, for any
x\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}
, in the limit as
R\rightarrow\infty
, by the Poincaré inequality,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R^{2}}\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}\left|\phi_{\xi}-\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}\phi_{\xi}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{R^{2}}\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}\left|\phi_{\xi}\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\sim\frac{1}{R^{2}}\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\phi_{\xi}|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{R^{2}}\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\phi_{\xi}|^{2},

where the implicit constants above do not depend on
x
.
Writing
\phi=(\phi_{e_{1}},\dots,\phi_{e_{d}})
for the vector-valued corrector, this proves

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{R}\left(\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}\left|\phi-\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}\phi\right|^{2}\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}=0.

The flux corrector
\sigma_{\xi}
can be defined as follows. Since the flux
q_{\xi}=a(\xi+\nabla\phi_{\xi})
is an element of
L^{2}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
, for any
i,j\in\{1,2,\dots,d\}
, there is a unique function
\sigma_{\xi ij}\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
satisfying the following two equations:

\displaystyle-\Delta\sigma_{\xi ij}=\partial_{j}q_{\xi i}-\partial_{i}q_{\xi j}\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{T}^{d},\quad\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\sigma_{\xi ij}=0.

By the uniqueness of
\sigma_{\xi ij}
, we learn that
\sigma_{\xi ij}=-\sigma_{\xi ji}
. Hence the matrix
\sigma_{\xi}
is skew-symmetric. By the same argument as for
\phi_{\xi}
, we have that
R^{-1}(\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}|\sigma_{\xi}-\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}\sigma_{\xi}|^{2})^{\frac{1}{2}}\to 0
uniformly with respect to
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Arguing as in
[
41
, Section 2.1]
, one can show that the Helmholtz-type decomposition (
29
) holds with the matrix
\bar{a}
determined by the formula

\displaystyle\bar{a}\xi=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}a(y)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{\xi}(y))\,dy.

Furthermore, a variational argument along the lines of the one in Section
4.1.2
shows that
\lambda\text{Id}\leq\bar{a}\leq\Lambda\text{Id}
(see also
[
10
]
).

The previous two paragraphs prove that if
a
is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic, then it certainly satisfies the assumptions of Section
3.1
and

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\big({\rm Sub}(Q_{R}(x))+|\bar{a}(Q_{R}(x))-\bar{a}|\big)=0.

Similarly, if
\theta
is
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic, we define
\bar{\theta}=\int_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\theta(y)\,dy
, which clearly satisfies
\theta_{*}\leq\bar{\theta}\leq\theta^{*}
. Observe that if we let
\psi\in H^{1}(\mathbb{T}^{d})
denote the solution of the PDE

\displaystyle-\Delta\psi=\theta-\bar{\theta}\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{T}^{d},\quad\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}\psi=0,

then it is straightforward to check that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
R>0
,

(116)

\frac{1}{R^{2}}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{R}(x))}^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{R^{2}}\fint_{Q_{R}(x)}|\nabla\psi|^{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{R^{2}}\fint_{\mathbb{T}^{d}}|\nabla\psi|^{2},

where the implied constants do not depend on
x
.
Therefore,

\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm Osc}(Q_{R}(x))=\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{R}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}({Q_{R}(x)})}=0.

5.2.
Uniformly Almost Periodic Media

In this section, we consider the case when
a
or
\theta
are uniformly almost periodic, or, more precisely, elements of
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We prove that it is possible to construct fields
(\phi_{Q},\sigma_{Q})
and matrices
\bar{a}(Q)
satisfying the assumptions of Section
3.1
in such a way that

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big({\rm Osc}(Q_{R}(x))+{\rm Sub}(Q_{R}(x))+|\bar{a}(Q_{R}(x))-\bar{a}|\Big)=0.

We begin by recalling the definition of the Bohr compactification, which provides a natural ergodic-theoretic setting for studying almost periodic functions. For more information and references, see
[
11
, Section 3]
,
[
50
]
, or
[
43
, Chapter 7, Section 5]
.

Let
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
be the group of all complex (possibly discontinuous) multiplicative characters of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
:

\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}=\left\{\omega:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{C}\,\mid\,\omega(x+y)=\omega(x)\omega(y),\,\,|\omega(x)|=1\,\,\text{for each}\,\,x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}.

\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
is an Abelian group under pointwise multiplication:
(\omega^{\prime}\omega)(x)=\omega^{\prime}(x)\omega(x)
.
As is standard, we equip
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
with the product topology inherited from
\mathbb{C}^{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
. Hence by Tychonoff’s theorem,
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
is a compact topological Abelian group, referred to as the Bohr compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Note that
\mathbb{R}^{d}
itself includes into
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
. Indeed, define the group action
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
such that, for any
\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
and any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the character
\tau_{x}\omega
is given by

(\tau_{x}\omega)(y)=e^{i2\pi x\cdot y}\omega(y).

Clearly, if
\mathbf{1}\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
denotes the constant function
\mathbf{1}\equiv 1
, then
x\mapsto\tau_{x}\mathbf{1}
is an embedding (group monomorphism) of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
into
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
. It is possible to show that the set
\{\tau_{x}\mathbf{1}\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is dense in
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
, hence
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
really is a topological compactification of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Further, and most important for us,
f\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
3
3

3

A similar equivalence holds relating
B^{p}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
to
L^{p}(\Omega_{\text{Bohr}})
in case
p<\infty
, see
[
50
]
.
if and only if there exists an
F\in C(\Omega_{\rm Bohr})
such that

f(x)=F(\tau_{x}\mathbf{1})\quad\text{for each}\quad x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}.

Letting
\mathbb{P}_{\text{Bohr}}
denote the normalized Haar measure on
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
, we have the identity

\mathbb{E}_{\text{Bohr}}[F]=\lim_{R\to\infty}\fint_{B_{R}(x)}f,

where the convergence is uniform with respect to the center point
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. From this, it follows that the action
(\tau_{x})_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
is ergodic on
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
equipped with its Borel
\sigma
-algebra and
\mathbb{P}_{\text{Bohr}}
.

We next establish that if
\theta\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then the quantity
{\rm Osc}(\cdot)
defined in (
9
) vanishes as
R\to\infty
uniformly with respect to the center point
x
.

Proposition 9
.

If
\theta\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, then

\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm Osc}_{x}(R)=\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm Osc}(Q_{R}(x))=0.

The proof of the proposition is a routine extension of the periodic case already covered in the previous subsection, which we include for completeness. Here it is convenient to recall that the characters
\{e_{\lambda}\}_{\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
defined by
e_{\lambda}(x)=\exp\left(i2\pi\lambda\cdot x\right)
densely span the space
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
; see
[
16
, Chapter 3]
or
[
40
, Section 7.4]
.

Proof.

Given a
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
, let
E_{\lambda}:\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}\to\mathbb{C}
be defined by
E_{\lambda}(\omega)=\omega_{\lambda}
. Observe that if we define
e_{\lambda}(x)=E_{\lambda}(\tau_{x}\mathbf{1})
, then
e_{\lambda}(x)=e^{i2\pi\lambda\cdot x}
as in the previous paragraph. Notice that
e_{\lambda}
is periodic with respect to some lattice, which can be obtained by rotating and dilating
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
. Thus, as in the previous section, since
e_{\lambda}
has mean zero,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\frac{1}{R}\|e_{\lambda}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{R}(x))}=0.

Next, since
\theta\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, we have
\theta(x)=\Theta(\tau_{x}\omega)
for some
\Theta\in C(\Omega_{\text{Bohr}})
and
\bar{\theta}=\mathbb{E}_{\text{Bohr}}[\Theta]
by definition.

Fix
\epsilon>0
. Since
\Theta-\bar{\theta}
has mean zero, there are points
\lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus\{0\}
and numbers
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{N}\in\mathbb{C}
such that

\displaystyle\sup_{\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}}\Big|\Theta(\omega)-\bar{\theta}-\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha_{k}E_{\lambda_{k}}(\omega)\Big|<\epsilon,

see
[
16
, Theorem 4.5]
. In particular,
\left|\theta(x)-\bar{\theta}-\sum_{k=1}^{N}\alpha_{k}e_{\lambda_{k}}(x)\right|<\epsilon
for every
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Taken together with our choice of normalization for the
H^{-1}
norm, see (
17
), and the Poincaré inequality (which implies
R^{-1}\|\mathbf{1}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{R})}\lesssim 1
), this implies

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R}\|\theta-\bar{\theta}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{R}(x))}\lesssim\epsilon+\sum_{k=1}^{N}|\alpha_{k}|\frac{1}{R}\|e_{\lambda_{k}}\|_{H^{-1}(Q_{R}(x))}.

We conclude upon sending first
R\to\infty
and then
\epsilon\to 0
.
∎

It remains to analyze the case when
a\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\text{Sym}(d))
, i.e., each entry of the matrix is in
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. Here is where we use the generality of the assumptions in Section
3.1
. Specifically, we work with finite-volume correctors rather than the infinite-volume ones we used in the random and periodic cases. Technically, the reason we treat the almost periodic case differently is simple: it is not known whether or not the quantity
{\rm Sub}_{x}(R)
measuring the sublinearity of the infinite-volume corrector (i.e., as defined in (
7
)) vanishes uniformly with respect to the center point
x
. As discussed further in Remark
9
below, this is a question of its own interest, related to open questions in the large-scale regularity theory for elliptic equations with almost periodic coefficients.

More specifically, in this setting, for any
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any bounded, Lipschitz open set
U
, we let
\phi_{U,\xi}
be the solution of the Dirichlet problem

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot a(x)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{U,\xi})=0\quad\text{in}\,\,U,\quad\phi_{U,\xi}=0\quad\text{on}\,\,\partial U.

By uniqueness, the map
\xi\mapsto\phi_{U,\xi}
is linear, hence as before we define the vector-valued corrector
\phi_{U}=(\phi_{U,e_{1}},\dots,\phi_{U,e_{d}})
.
Below we will construct, for any cube
Q
, a field
\sigma_{Q}=(\sigma_{Q,e_{1}},\dots,\sigma_{Q,e_{d}})
and a matrix
\bar{a}(Q)
in such a way that

\displaystyle a(x)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{Q,\xi})

\displaystyle=\bar{a}(Q)\xi+\nabla\cdot\sigma_{Q,\xi}

and

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\Big({\rm Sub}(Q_{R}(x))+|\bar{a}(Q_{R}(x))-\bar{a}|\Big)=0.

Here
\bar{a}
is the homogenized matrix associated with the operator
\nabla\cdot a(x)\nabla
, which we will access using the variational approach.

First, we construct
\sigma_{Q,\xi}
using the following lemma. The difficulty lies in obtaining a suitable estimate for
\sigma_{Q,\xi}
, which is essentially folklore but is complicated by the fact that we work in a bounded domain. Since we are not aware of a reference where this is done in detail, a proof is given at the end of this section (Section
5.4
). In the statement, we denote by
\mathcal{C}
the space of skew-symmetric matrix fields
\sigma^{\prime}\in L^{2}(Q;\text{Skew}(d))
such that
\nabla\cdot\sigma^{\prime}=0
weakly in
Q
.

Lemma 9
.

Given a cube
Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a divergence-free vector field
b\in L^{2}(Q;\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there is a unique skew-symmetric matrix field
\sigma\in L^{2}(Q;\text{Skew}(d))
such that

(117)

\displaystyle\nabla\cdot\sigma=b\quad\text{in}\,\,Q\quad\text{and}\quad\int_{Q}\text{tr}(\sigma(x)^{T}\sigma^{\prime}(x))\,dx=0\quad\text{for each}\,\,\sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}.

Further, there is a constant
C(d)>0
such that

\displaystyle\fint_{Q}\text{tr}(\sigma(x)^{T}\sigma(x))\,dx\leq C(d)\|b\|_{H^{1}(Q)^{\prime}}^{2},

where
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(Q)^{\prime}}
is the dual norm

\displaystyle\|b\|_{H^{1}(Q)^{\prime}}=\sup\left\{\fint_{Q}b\cdot V\,\mid\,\int_{Q}|\nabla V|^{2}\,dx+|Q|^{-2/d}\int_{Q}|V|^{2}\,dx\leq 1\right\}.

We apply the lemma to obtain, for each cube
Q
and each
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, a unique skew-symmetric matrix field
\sigma_{Q,\xi}
such that
\nabla\cdot\sigma_{Q,\xi}=a(x)(\xi+\nabla\phi_{Q,\xi})-\bar{a}(Q)\xi
in
Q
and
\sigma_{Q,\xi}
is orthogonal to divergence-free, skew-symmetric fields. By uniqueness, the map
\xi\mapsto\sigma_{Q,\xi}
is linear.

Finally, we establish uniform control over the homogenization error using variational techniques. Given a bounded open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and a vector
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
\bar{a}(U)
be the symmetric matrix such that

(118)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bar{a}(U)\xi\cdot\xi

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2}\fint_{U}a(x)\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx\,\mid\,u-\xi\cdot x\in H^{1}_{0}(U)\right\}.

For the proof that the right-hand sides of the displays above define quadratic forms in the variable
\xi
, and thus
\bar{a}(U)
and
\bar{a}_{*}(U)
are well-defined, see
[
4
, Section 1.2]
or
[
3
]
.

Using the topology of
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
in conjunction with results from the theory of
\Gamma
-convergence, we will prove that the quantities
|\bar{a}(Q_{R}(x))-\bar{a}|
and
{\rm Sub}(Q_{R}(x))
vanish as
R\to\infty
uniformly with respect to the center point
x
.

Proposition 10
.

If
a\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\text{Sym}(d))
and there are constants
0<\lambda\leq\Lambda
such that
\lambda\text{Id}\leq a\leq\Lambda\text{Id}
pointwise, then

(119)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\bar{a}(Q_{R}(x))-\bar{a}|=0,\quad\text{and}

(120)

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm Sub}(Q_{R}(x))=0.

Proof.

We first prove uniform convergence of
\bar{a}(Q)
, then we recall some facts from
\Gamma
-convergence and homogenization, and, finally, we prove the uniform convergence of
{\rm Sub}(Q)
. All the properties of
\Gamma
-convergence used in this proof are standard and can be found in the textbook
[
23
, Chapter 22]
.

Step 1: Uniform Convergence of Variational Quantities.
A change of perspective is useful. Since
a\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\text{Sym}(d))
, there is a continuous function
A:\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}\to\text{Sym}(d)
such that
a(x)=A(\tau_{x}\mathbf{1})
for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. It is convenient to define a symmetric matrix-valued set function
U\mapsto\bar{A}(U;\omega)
, proceeding by analogy with
\bar{a}(U)
, via the formula

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\bar{A}(U;\omega)\xi\cdot\xi

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\frac{1}{2}\fint_{U}A(\tau_{x}\omega)\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx\,\mid\,u-\xi\cdot x\in H^{1}_{0}(U)\right\}.

As in
[
3
, Section 1.2]
,
\lambda\text{Id}\leq\bar{A}(U;\omega)\leq\Lambda\text{Id}
(as symmetric matrices) for any
U
and
\omega
.
Notice that
\bar{a}(U)=\bar{A}(U;\mathbf{1})
for any
U
. In this step of the proof, we will show that

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}|\bar{A}(Q_{R}(x);\mathbf{1})-\bar{a}|=0.

Since
A(x+U;\omega)=A(U;\tau_{x}\omega)
for any
U
and
x
, it suffices to prove that

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}}|\bar{A}(Q_{R};\omega)-\bar{a}|=0.

As in
[
22
]
,
\bar{A}(\cdot;\omega)
is subadditive in the sense that if
U_{1},\dots,U_{N}
are disjoint bounded open sets and
U=U_{1}\cup\dots\cup U_{N}
, then, for any
\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
,

\displaystyle A(U;\omega)\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}A(U_{i};\omega),\quad A_{*}(U;\omega)^{-1}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{N}A_{*}(U_{i};\omega)^{-1},

where again the inequalities hold at the level of quadratic forms.
Therefore, the subadditive ergodic theorem implies that there is a (constant) symmetric matrix
\bar{A}
such that

\displaystyle\bar{A}(Q_{R};\omega)\to\bar{A}\quad\text{as}\,\,R\to\infty\quad\text{for}\,\,\mathbb{P}_{\text{Bohr}}\text{-almost every}\,\,\omega\in\Omega.

The classical result of
[
22
]
implies that
\bar{A}=\bar{a}
, the homogenized matrix. It only remains to prove that the above convergence actually holds uniformly with respect to
\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
.

Here we invoke the fact that
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
is a compact Abelian topological group, and, therefore, the continuity of
A
implies

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega^{\prime}\to\mathbf{1}}\sup\left\{|A(\omega^{\prime}\omega)-A(\omega)|\,\mid\,\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}\right\}=0,

see, e.g.,
[
33
, Proposition 2.6]
. At the same time, by using the uniform estimate
\bar{A}(U;\omega)\leq\Lambda\text{Id}
, we see that, for any
\omega^{\prime},\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
and any bounded open set
U
,

\displaystyle|\bar{A}(U;\omega^{\prime}\omega)-\bar{A}(U;\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\Lambda\lambda^{-1}\sup\left\{|A(\tau_{x}\omega^{\prime}\omega)-A(\tau_{x}\omega)|\,\mid\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\,\,\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\Lambda\lambda^{-1}\sup\left\{|A(\omega^{\prime}\omega^{\prime\prime})-A(\omega^{\prime\prime})|\,\mid\,\omega^{\prime\prime}\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}\right\}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\lim_{\omega^{\prime}\to\mathbf{1}}\sup_{R>0}\sup_{\omega\in\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}}|A(Q_{R};\omega^{\prime}\omega)-A(Q_{R};\omega)|=0,

Since
\Omega_{\text{Bohr}}
is compact, this implies
\bar{A}(Q_{R};\omega)\to\bar{a}
uniformly with respect to
\omega
as
R\to\infty
.

Step 2:
\Gamma
-convergence and Homogenization.
For any
\epsilon>0
, let
\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}
be the energy functional defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(u;U)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}a(\epsilon^{-1}x)\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx.

Since
a\in B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\text{Sym}(d))
, the theory of homogenization implies there is a symmetric matrix
\bar{a}
such that if
\bar{\mathcal{E}}
is the energy given by

\displaystyle\bar{\mathcal{E}}(u;U)=\int_{U}\bar{a}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx,

then
\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon}(\cdot;U)

\Gamma
-converges in
L^{2}(U)
to
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\cdot;U)
as
\epsilon\to 0
for any bounded open set
U
.

The convergence above can be upgraded: For any sequence of scales
(\epsilon_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and points
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, if
\mathcal{E}_{n}
is the shifted functional (abusing notation)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{n}(u;U)=\mathcal{E}_{\epsilon_{n}}(u(\cdot-x_{n});x_{n}+U),

then compactenss and integral representation results from the theory of
\Gamma
-convergence (see
[
23
, Theorem 22.3]
) imply that there is a subsequence
(\mathcal{E}_{n_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
and an energy functional
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}
such that
\mathcal{E}_{n_{j}}(\cdot;U)

\Gamma
-converges to
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(\cdot;U)
as
j\to\infty
for any bounded open set
U
and
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(u;U)=\frac{1}{2}\int_{U}\tilde{a}(x)\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx
for some bounded, positive definite matrix field
\tilde{a}
. Since
\Gamma
-convergence implies convergence of solutions of the Dirichlet problem in this setting (see
[
23
, Theorem 22.9]
), this implies that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
r>0
, and
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, if we let
y_{n}=\epsilon_{n}(x+x_{n})
, then

\displaystyle\min\left\{\tilde{\mathcal{E}}(u;Q_{r}(x))\,\mid\,u(y)=\xi\cdot y\,\,\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{r}(x)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{j\to\infty}\min\left\{\mathcal{E}_{n_{j}}(u;Q_{r}(x))\,\mid\,u(y)=\xi\cdot y\,\,\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{r}(x)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\bar{A}(Q_{\epsilon_{n_{j}}^{-1}r}(y_{n_{j}});\mathbf{1})\xi\cdot\xi

\displaystyle\qquad=\lim_{j\to\infty}\frac{1}{2}\bar{A}(Q_{\epsilon_{n_{j}}^{-1}r}(0);\tau_{y_{n}}\mathbf{1})\xi\cdot\xi=\frac{1}{2}\bar{a}\xi\cdot\xi.

In view of the fact that this is true independent of the choice of the triple
(x,r,\xi)
, it follows that
\tilde{a}(y)=\bar{a}
for almost every
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. This proves
\tilde{\mathcal{E}}=\bar{\mathcal{E}}
.

Step 3: Uniform Convergence of
{\rm Sub}(Q_{R}(x))
.
Fix a sequence
(R_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of scales and a sequence of points
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\limsup_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}{\rm Sub}(Q_{R}(x))=\lim_{n\to\infty}{\rm Sub}(Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n})).

We will show that the right-hand side is zero using what was proved in Step 2.

Let
\epsilon_{n}=R_{n}^{-1}
for each
n
. Associated to the sequences
(\epsilon_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
and
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, define the sequence
(\mathcal{E}_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
as in Step 2. As was shown in that step,
\mathcal{E}_{n}(\cdot;U)

\Gamma
-converges to
\bar{\mathcal{E}}(\cdot;U)
as
n\to\infty
for any fixed
U
.

Given
\xi\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, let
u_{n}
be the solution of the PDE

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot a(\epsilon_{n}^{-1}y+x_{n})\nabla u_{n}=0\quad\text{in}\,\,Q_{1},\quad u_{n}(y)=\xi\cdot y\quad\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{1}.

By construction,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{R_{n}^{2}}\fint_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n})}|\phi_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),\xi}-\fint_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n})}\phi_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),\xi}|^{2}=\fint_{Q_{1}}|u_{n}-\xi\cdot y-\fint_{Q_{1}}u_{n}|^{2}.

At the same time, as a consequence of
\Gamma
-convergence, the sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
converges in
L^{2}(Q_{1})
to the solution
\bar{u}
of

\displaystyle-\nabla\cdot\bar{a}\nabla\bar{u}=0\quad\text{in}\,\,Q_{1},\quad\bar{u}(y)=\xi\cdot y\quad\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{1}.

Since
\bar{a}
is constant, the solution is precisely
\bar{u}(y)=\xi\cdot y
for all
y
, hence

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{R_{n}^{2}}\fint_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n})}|\phi_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),\xi}-\fint_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n})}\phi_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),\xi}|^{2}

\displaystyle\qquad=\fint_{Q_{1}}|\bar{u}-\xi\cdot y-\fint_{Q_{1}}\bar{u}|^{2}=0.

Similarly, at the level of
\sigma_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n})}
, it is well-known that homogenization implies the weak convergence of fluxes:

\displaystyle a(\epsilon_{n}^{-1}y+x_{n})\nabla u_{n}\rightharpoonup\bar{a}\xi\quad\text{in}\,\,L^{2}(Q_{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{as}\,\,n\to\infty.

Since weak convergence in
L^{2}(Q_{1};\mathbb{R}^{d})
implies strong convergence with respect to the norm
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(Q_{1})^{\prime}}
defined in the statement of Lemma
9
, this combines with the estimate in said lemma and (
119
) to yield

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{R_{n}^{2}}\fint_{Q_{R_{n}}(x)}|\sigma_{Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),\xi}|^{2}\,dy=0.

Since
\xi
was arbitrary, by linearity, we obtain
{\rm Sub}(Q_{R_{n}}(x_{n}))\to 0
as desired.
∎

Remark 9
.

In the previous proposition, we proved that
{\rm Sub}(Q)
, which measures the sublinearity of the finite-volume corrector
\phi_{Q}
, converges uniformly to zero as
|Q|\to\infty
. It is not clear that the same could be said if finite-volume correctors were replaced by the infinite-volume corrector. This is the reason for using correctors
(\phi_{Q},\sigma_{Q})
and matrices
\bar{a}(Q)
that depend on
Q
in this section.

If it were known that, in the uniformly almost periodic setting, the quantity
{\rm Sub}_{x}(R)
associated with the infinite volume corrector (see (
7
) above) vanished uniformly with respect to
x
as
R\to\infty
, then this fact could be combined with the approach of
[
38
]
to prove (uniform-in-space) large-scale Lipschitz estimates for solutions of the corresponding elliptic equation. To the best of the authors’ knowledge, whether or not such estimates hold for arbitrary uniformly almost periodic coefficients remains an open question. (This is highlighted as an open problem in
[
5
]
, where Lipschitz estimates are proved under slightly stronger assumptions on
a
.) Conversely, if true, such estimates could be used in conjunction with the techniques of
[
4
]
to prove the uniform convergence of
{\rm Sub}_{x}(R)
.

5.3.
Proof of Corollary
2

By combining the observations of the previous two subsections with the results of Section
3
, we readily obtain the proof of Corollary
2
, that is, the
\Gamma
-convergence of
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}
to
\bar{\mathscr{E}}
for any scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
satisfying
\delta(\epsilon)\ll\epsilon
.

Proof of Corollary
2
.

Assume that both
a
and
\theta
are either
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic or functions in
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, and let
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
be any scale such that
\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
. As in the stochastic setting, we need to show that, for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, any
\varrho>0
, and any
e\in S^{d-1}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;Q_{\varrho}(x))\,\mid\,u(y)=q(\epsilon^{-1}(y-x)\cdot e)\,\,\text{for}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{\varrho}(x)\right\}=\bar{\sigma}(e).

As before, we restrict attention to the case
e=e_{1}
. Otherwise, one can check that composition with a rotation preserves
B^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, while such a rotation would transform a
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic function into a function periodic with respect to a rotated lattice. (It is a coincidence that
e_{1}
is a lattice direction. Recall from Section
5.1
that the correctors are defined for arbitrary directions
\xi
.) In either case, the relevant facts from Sections
5.1
and
5.2
are preserved.

The proof of the upper bound (Theorem
2
) carries over to the present setting, as is discussed briefly in Remark
10
in Appendix
A
below. In particular, this means that

\displaystyle\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;Q_{\varrho}(x))\,\mid\,u(y)=q(\epsilon^{-1}(y-x)\cdot e)\,\,\text{for}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{\varrho}(x)\right\}\leq\bar{\sigma}(e).

It only remains to check the lower bound. Toward this end, as in Section
3
, we rescale space by a factor
R\coloneqq\epsilon^{-1}
and introduce the parameter
\gamma(R)\coloneqq\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)
, so that now the goal is to prove

\displaystyle\liminf_{R\to\infty}\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)}(u;Q_{\varrho R}(Rx))\,\mid\,u(y)=q((y-Rx)\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{for}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{\varrho R}(Rx)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\geq\bar{\sigma}(e_{1}).

As in the proof of Theorem
3
, there is no loss of generality assuming that
\varrho R=(2K+1)r_{c}
for some
K\in\mathbb{N}
. Fix a minimizer
u
of the variational problem above. According to Theorem
5
, there is an
R
-independent modulus of continuity
\omega
and a function
\tilde{u}\in H^{1}(Q_{\varrho R}(Rx))
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\overline{\mathscr{F}}(\tilde{u};Q_{\varrho R}(Rx))}{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)}(u;Q_{\varrho R}(Rx))}-1

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\leq\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega\Big({\rm Sub}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1}}(x))+{\rm Osc}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1}}(x))+|\bar{a}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1}}(x)-\bar{a}|\Big).

In view of the discussion of Sections
5.1
and
5.2
above, our construction of the fields
(\phi_{Q},\sigma_{Q})
and the matrices
\bar{a}(Q)
for cubes
Q
yields that

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}\omega\Big({\rm Sub}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1}}(x))+{\rm Osc}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1}}(x))+|\bar{a}(Q_{r_{c}\gamma(R)^{-1}}(x)-\bar{a}|\Big)=0,

hence, upon invoking
\Gamma
-convergence results for the spatially homogeneous functional
\overline{\mathscr{F}}
(see
[
2
, Theorem 3.7]
), we find

\displaystyle\liminf_{R\to\infty}\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma(R)}(u;Q_{\varrho R}(Rx))\,\mid\,u(y)=q((y-Rx)\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{for}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{\varrho R}(Rx)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad\geq\lim_{R\to\infty}\min\left\{\overline{\mathscr{F}}(u;Q_{\varrho R}(Rx))\,\mid\,u(y)=q((y-Rx)\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{for}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{\varrho R}(Rx)\right\}

\displaystyle\qquad=\bar{\sigma}(e_{1}).

∎

5.4.
Proof of Lemma
9

Finally, we address the construction of
\sigma_{Q}
from Section
5.2
. We treat the equation
\nabla\cdot\sigma=b
as an equation for differential forms and then quantify the dependence of
\sigma
on
b
following
[
24
]
.

Proof of Lemma
9
.

By scaling, it suffices to prove the result when
|Q|=1
, which we assume henceforth for simplicity.

After suitably reformulating the problem, it becomes a question about differential forms. To reformulate, we pass from vector fields and skew-symmetric matrix fields (interpreted as
1
- and
2
-forms, respectively) to
(d-1)
- and
(d-2)
-forms using the Hodge star operator
\star
. The definition of the Hodge star operator in full generality can be found in
[
24
]
, but does not concern us here. For our purposes, it suffices to know that if
b
is a vector field in
Q
, and if
B=\star b
is the corresponding
(d-1)
-form, then

\displaystyle B=\sum_{j=1}^{d}(-1)^{j+1}b_{j}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{j-1}\wedge dx_{j+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{d},

and, thus,

\displaystyle dB=0\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\nabla\cdot b=0.

Similarly, if
\sigma
is a skew-symmetric matrix field, and if
\Sigma=\star\sigma
is the corresponding
(d-2)
-form, then

\displaystyle\Sigma=\sum_{i<j}(-1)^{i+j+1}\sigma_{ij}dx_{1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{i-1}\wedge dx_{i+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{j-1}\wedge dx_{j+1}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{d},

from which a straightforward computation shows that

\displaystyle d\Sigma=-B\quad\text{if and only if}\quad\nabla\cdot\sigma=b.

Finally, we will use the fact that, provided one treats the exterior power
\wedge^{k}\mathbb{R}^{d}
for a given
k
as an inner product space with orthonormal basis
\{dx_{i_{1}}\wedge\cdots\wedge dx_{i_{k}}\,\mid\,1\leq i_{1}<\cdots<i_{k}\leq d\}
, then the Hodge star operator is an isometry between
\wedge^{k}\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\wedge^{d-k}\mathbb{R}^{d}
. In particular, a skew-symmetric matrix field
\sigma
is perpendicular to
\mathcal{C}
as in the second half of (
117
) if and only if the corresponding
(d-2)
-form
\Sigma=\star\sigma
is perpendicular to the space of weakly closed, square-integrable
(d-2)
-forms, that is,

\displaystyle\langle\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(Q)}=0\quad\text{for each}\,\,\Sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\star},

\displaystyle\text{where}\quad\mathcal{C}_{\star}=\{\Sigma^{\prime}\in L^{2}(Q;\wedge^{d-2}\mathbb{R}^{d})\,\mid\,d\Sigma^{\prime}=0\}.

In view of these correspondences, we seek to prove that for a given
(d-1)
-form
B\in L^{2}(Q;\wedge^{d-1}\mathbb{R}^{d})
satisfying
dB=0
, there is a unique
\Sigma\in L^{2}(Q;\wedge^{d-2}\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that

(121)

\displaystyle d\Sigma=B\quad\text{in}\,\,Q\quad\text{and}\quad\langle\Sigma,\Sigma^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(Q)}=0\quad\text{for each}\,\,\Sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\star}

and for which the following estimate holds

(122)

\displaystyle\|\Sigma\|_{L^{2}(Q)}\leq C(d)\|B\|_{H^{-1}(Q)},

where
\|\cdot\|_{H^{1}(Q)^{\prime}}
is the norm

\displaystyle\|V\|_{H^{1}(Q)^{\prime}}

\displaystyle=\sup\left\{\langle V,V^{\prime}\rangle_{L^{2}(Q)}\,\mid\,V^{\prime}\in H^{1}(Q;\Lambda^{d-1}\mathbb{R}^{d})\quad\text{such that}\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.\|\nabla V^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Q)}+\|V^{\prime}\|^{2}_{L^{2}(Q)}\leq 1\right\}.

Concerning existence, since
Q
is convex, it is well-known that if
B
is smooth and satisfies
dB=0
, then there is a smooth
\Sigma
such that
d\Sigma=B
in
Q
. If
B
is merely square integrable with
dB=0
, then
[
24
, Proposition 2.3]
implies there is a
\Sigma\in H^{1}(Q;\wedge^{d-2}\mathbb{R}^{d})
such that
d\Sigma=B
. Further, we can invoke a version of the Poincaré inequality, namely,
[
24
, Proposition 3.6]
to find that

\displaystyle\inf\left\{\|\Sigma-\Sigma^{\prime}\|_{L^{2}(Q)}\,\mid\,\Sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\star}\right\}\leq C(d)\|B\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}.

Notice that if
\Sigma^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}_{\star}
, then
d(\Sigma-\Sigma^{\prime})=B
. Therefore, the orthogonal projection
\Sigma^{\perp}
of
\Sigma
perpendicular to
\mathcal{C}_{\star}
satisfies
d\Sigma^{\perp}=B
and
\|\Sigma^{\perp}\|_{L^{2}(Q)}\leq C(d)\|B\|_{H^{-1}(Q)}
. In particular, replacing
\Sigma
by
\Sigma^{\perp}
, we obtain a solution of (
121
) and (
122
) holds.

Finally, if
\Sigma_{1}
and
\Sigma_{2}
are two solutions of (
121
) with the same right-hand side
B
, then
d(\Sigma_{2}-\Sigma_{1})=0
so
\langle\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2},\Sigma_{1}-\Sigma_{2}\rangle_{L^{2}(Q)}=0
. Thus, the solution is unique.
∎

Appendix A
Homogenization Upper Bound

In this appendix, we prove the homogenization upper bound, Theorem
2
, which shows that the homogenized energy is always an upper bound in the regime where
\delta(\epsilon)\ll\epsilon
. It is important to note that in this appendix, while we continue to assume that
(a,\theta)
satisfy the assumptions of Section
1.1
, we only assume that
W:\mathbb{R}\to[0,\infty)
is continuous and

\displaystyle\{u\in\mathbb{R}\,\mid\,W(u)=0\}=\{-1,1\}.

The proof will be presented in two steps. First, we consider the special case when
x_{0}=0
, in which case we can readily apply an ergodic theorem to obtain the desired result. Next, when
x_{0}\neq 0
, we once again appeal to the ergodic theorem to argue that the behavior near
x_{0}
looks sufficiently similar to that at the origin.

In the first step, we will use the following variant of the standard ergodic theorem. As in
[
49
]
, we are motivated to consider averages over a
(d-1)
-dimensional group due to the fact that the energy scales like
R^{d-1}
.

Proposition 11
.

Assume that
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is a probability space supporting a measurable group action
\{\tau_{x}\}_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}}
and satisfying the assumptions of Section
1.1
. Let
(X_{\gamma})_{\gamma>0}
be a process (one-parameter family of
\mathcal{F}
-measurable random variables) such that the function
\gamma\mapsto X_{\gamma}
is continuous on an event of probability one and for which there is a constant
C_{X}>0
such that, with probability one,

|X_{\gamma}|\leq C_{X}\quad\text{for each}\,\,\gamma>0.

If there is a constant
\bar{X}\in\mathbb{R}
such that

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}X_{\gamma}=\bar{X}\quad\text{almost surely,}

then, for any
L\in\mathbb{N}
,

\lim_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to(0,0)}\left(\frac{L}{R}\right)^{d-1}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}X_{\gamma}\circ\tau_{k}=\bar{X}\quad\text{almost surely.}

In the above proposition, we impose a continuity assumption in the variable
\gamma
only because it is real-valued. This allows us to sidestep measurability issues. Such an assumption would not be necessary if we worked with sequences
(\delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
rather than functions
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
.

The proof of Proposition
11
is deferred to the end of this appendix. For now, we use the proposition to prove Theorem
2
in the special case when
x_{0}=0
.

Proof of Theorem
2
(case:
x_{0}=0
).

Fix an arbitrary (deterministic) scaling function
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
such that
\epsilon^{-1}\delta(\epsilon)\to 0
as
\epsilon\to 0
. As in Section
4.3
and (
18
), we will use the following shorthand notation:

m(\mathscr{F},U,q)=\inf\left\{\mathscr{F}(u;U)\,\mid\,u\in H^{1}(U),\,\,u(y)=q(y\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial U\right\}.

Recall that we aim to prove that if
q
satisfies the assumptions (
19
) and (
20
) of Section
2
, then

\limsup_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to(0,0)}R^{1-d}m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},Q_{\varrho R},q)\leq\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})\varrho^{d-1}\quad\text{almost surely,}

where
\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})
is the positive number with
\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})^{2}=\sigma_{W}^{2}\bar{\theta}e_{1}\cdot\bar{a}e_{1}
and
\sigma_{W}
is given by (
21
).

Up to replacing the medium
(a,\theta)
by the rescaled one
(a(\varrho\cdot),\theta(\varrho\cdot))
, we can and will assume without loss of generality that
\varrho=1
.

In the proof that follows, we will work with the cylinder
\mathcal{C}(R,h)\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
defined for
R,h>0
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(R,h)=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,|y\cdot e_{1}|<\frac{h}{2},\,\,|y\cdot e_{2}|,\dots,|y\cdot e_{d}|<\frac{R}{2}\right\}.

We first observe that, as in
[
49
, Proposition 5]
, there is a modulus of continuity
\omega:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
depending only on
q
,
\Lambda
, and
\theta^{*}
such that

(123)

\displaystyle m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},Q_{R},q)\leq m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},\mathcal{C}(R,h),q)+R^{1-d}\omega(h^{-1}).

In what follows, we take advantage of the fact that
m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},\mathcal{C}(R,h),q)
has a natural subadditive structure in the transversal variables
(y\cdot e_{2},\dots,y\cdot e_{d})
.

Specifically, we fix a discretization parameter
L\in\mathbb{N}
and discretize the cylinder
\mathcal{C}(R,h)
using the subcylinders
\{k+\mathcal{C}(L,h)\,\mid\,k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}\}
. It is convenient to define the discretization
\mathcal{C}_{L}(R,h)
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{L}(R,h)=\bigcup_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}(k+\mathcal{C}(L,h)).

By subadditivity (see
[
49
, Proposition 2]
), we can write

\displaystyle m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},\mathcal{C}(R,h),q)

\displaystyle\leq m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},\mathcal{C}(R,h)\setminus\mathcal{C}_{L}(R,h),q)

\displaystyle\qquad+\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},k+\mathcal{C}(L,h),q).

Observe that

\displaystyle m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},\mathcal{C}(R,h)\setminus\mathcal{C}_{L}(R,h),q)

\displaystyle\leq\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(q(y\cdot e_{1});\mathcal{C}(R,h)\setminus\mathcal{C}_{L}(R,h))

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathcal{C}(R,h)\setminus\mathcal{C}_{L}(R,h)}\left(\frac{\Lambda}{2}q^{\prime}(y\cdot e_{1})^{2}+\theta^{*}W(q(y\cdot e_{1}))\right)\,dy.

From this, we deduce that, with probability one, for any
L\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\sup_{\gamma}\frac{m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},\mathcal{C}(R,h)\setminus\mathcal{C}_{L}(R,h),q)}{R^{d-1}}=0.

It only remains to analyze the terms in the sum. This is where we apply Proposition
11
.

Define the process
(M_{\gamma})_{\gamma>0}
by setting

M_{\gamma}=m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},\mathcal{C}(L,h),q)

Recall that the pointwise bounds
\lambda\text{Id}\leq a\leq\Lambda\text{Id}
and
\theta_{*}\leq\theta\leq\theta^{*}
hold Lebesgue almost everywhere in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
with probability one. Thus, it is straightforward to check that, by the direct method of the calculus of variations, the function
\gamma\mapsto M_{\gamma}
is continuous in
(0,\infty)
almost surely.

At the same time, recall that, with probability one, the gradient functional

\int_{\mathcal{C}(L,h)}a(\gamma^{-1}x)\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx

\Gamma
-converges with respect to the weak topology of
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(L,h))
) as
\gamma\to 0
to the homogenized functional

\int_{\mathcal{C}(L,h)}\bar{a}\nabla u\cdot\nabla u\,dx.

By
[
23
, Theorem 21.1]
, this statement remains true if
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(L,h))
is replaced by
q+H^{1}_{0}(\mathcal{C}(L,h))
, i.e., the set of functions
u
with
u(y)=q(y\cdot e_{1})
on
\partial\mathcal{C}(L,h)
. Further, the potential well functional

\int_{\mathcal{C}(L,h)}\theta(\gamma^{-1}x)\tilde{W}(u)\,dx,\quad\text{with}\,\,\tilde{W}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}W(u),&\text{if}\,\,|u|\leq 1,\\
|u|^{2}-1,&\text{if}\,\,|u|>1,\end{array}\right.

is uniformly continuous on bounded subsets of
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(L,h))
(with a deterministic modulus of continuity, by the boundedness of
\theta
) and, thus, by the ergodic theorem, with probability one, it converges uniformly on bounded subsets of
H^{1}(\mathcal{C}(L,h))
as
\gamma\to 0
to the averaged functional

\bar{\theta}\int_{\mathcal{C}(L,h)}\tilde{W}(u)\,dx.

Since by Lemma
1
and its proof, the value of
M_{\gamma}
is unchanged if we replace
W
by
\tilde{W}
in the definition of
\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}
, we can invoke the standard perturbation result in the theory of
\Gamma
-convergence (see
[
23
, Proposition 6.20]
) to find

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}M_{\gamma}=\bar{M}\quad\text{almost surely,}

where

\displaystyle\bar{M}

\displaystyle=\min\left\{\overline{\mathscr{F}}(u,\mathcal{C}(L,h))\,\mid\,u(y)=q(y\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{if}\,\,y\in\partial\mathcal{C}(1,h)\right\}=m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\mathcal{C}(L,h),q).

Thus, by Proposition
11
, with probability one,

\displaystyle\lim_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to(0,0)}\frac{1}{R^{d-1}}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},\mathcal{C}(L,h),q)=L^{1-d}m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\mathcal{C}(L,h),q).

Combining the last limit with (
123
), we conclude that, with probability one,

\displaystyle\limsup_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to(0,0)}R^{1-d}m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},Q_{R},q)\leq L^{1-d}m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\mathcal{C}(L,h),q)+\omega(h^{-1}).

Setting
h=L
and sending
L\to\infty
, well-known results on the spatially homogeneous Allen-Cahn functional (see
[
2
, Theorem 3.7]
) imply that

\displaystyle\limsup_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to(0,0)}R^{1-d}m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},Q_{R},q)\leq\lim_{L\to\infty}L^{1-d}m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\mathcal{C}(L,L),q)=\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})\quad\text{almost surely.}

∎

Next, arguing as in
[
49
]
, we extend to the case where the center
x_{0}
is nonzero.

In this step of the proof, we use the following continuous variant of Egorov’s Theorem: If
(X_{y})_{y\in Y}
is a random process indexed by some open set
Y\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
such that
y\mapsto X_{y}
is continuous and
X_{y}\to 0
as
|y|\to 0
with probability one, then, for any
\delta>0
, there is an event
E_{\delta}
and a modulus of continuity
\omega_{\delta}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\mathbb{P}(E_{\delta})>1-\delta
and

\displaystyle\sup_{E_{\delta}}|X_{y}|\leq\omega_{\delta}(|y|).

This follows from an application of the standard version of Egorov’s Theorem to the discrete sequence
(X^{*}_{K})_{K\in\mathbb{N}}
given by
X^{*}_{K}=\sup_{|y|\leq K^{-1}}|X_{y}|
. (The continuity assumption is only imposed to ensure the
X^{*}_{K}
is measurable for each
K
.)

Proof of Theorem
2
(case:
x_{0}\neq 0
).

As in the
x_{0}=0
case, we assume without loss of generality that
\varrho=1
.

We begin by invoking the ergodic theorem. Notice that the two-parameter random process

\displaystyle(R,\gamma)\mapsto\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})\,\mid\,u(y)=q(y\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{R}\right\}

is continuous, as follows once again from an application of the direct method of the calculus of variations. Thus, by the
x_{0}=0
case of the theorem and the continuous variant of Egorov’s Theorem mentioned just before the start of this proof, there is an event
E\in\mathcal{F}
and a modulus of continuity
\eta:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\mathbb{P}(E)\geq\frac{1}{2}
and

\displaystyle\sup_{E}\left(R^{1-d}\min\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{R})\,\mid\,u(y)=q(y\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{on}\,\,\partial Q_{R}\right\}-\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})\right)\leq\eta(\gamma+R^{-1})

for any
R,\gamma>0
. Let
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
be a small parameter. By the ergodic theorem, with probability one,

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}\frac{|\{x\in Q_{2\alpha R}(Rx_{0})\,\mid\,\tau_{x}\omega\in E\}|}{R^{d}}=(2\alpha)^{d}\mathbb{P}(E)>0.

Thus, we can fix random points
(X_{R})_{R>0}
such that
X_{R}\in Q_{2\alpha R}(Rx_{0})
for any
R>0
and, for large enough
R
,
\tau_{X_{R}}\omega\in E
.

The remainder of the argument is deterministic. By the definition of
E
, for any
R,\gamma>0
, there is a function
u:Q_{(1-\alpha)R}(X_{R})\to[-1,1]
such that

\displaystyle(1-\alpha)^{1-d}R^{1-d}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{(1-\alpha)R}(X_{R}))\leq\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})+\eta(\gamma+R^{-1}),

\displaystyle u(y)=q((y-X_{R})\cdot e_{1})\,\,\text{for each}\,\,y\in\partial Q_{(1-\alpha)R}(X_{R}).

(We suppress the dependence on
R
,
\gamma
, and
\alpha
for notational ease.) This is nearly what we want, except that
u
has the wrong boundary condition: the boundary condition has been translated slightly (but no more than a distance
\alpha R
, which is macroscopically small). As we explain next, it is well-known that the error induced by this translation becomes negligible in the limit
\alpha\to 0
.

Arguing as in
[
49
, Proposition 9]
, we can define cut-off functions
\psi_{R,\alpha}
such that if
v:Q_{R}(Rx_{0})\to[-1,1]
is the function

v(y)=(1-\psi_{R,\alpha}(y))u(y)+\psi_{R,\alpha}(y)q((y-Rx_{0})\cdot e_{1}),

then
v(y)=q((y-Rx_{0})\cdot e_{1})
for each
y\in\partial Q_{R}(Rx_{0})
and

\displaystyle\limsup_{\alpha\to 0}\limsup_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to 0}R^{1-d}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(v;Q_{R}(Rx_{0}))

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{\alpha\to 0}\limsup_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to 0}R^{1-d}\mathscr{F}_{\gamma}(u;Q_{(1-\alpha)R}(X_{R}))

\displaystyle\leq\bar{\sigma}(e_{1}).

For more details, the reader is again referred to
[
49
, Proposition 9]
. Of course,
v
has the correct boundary condition, and its energy is asymptoptically no larger than
\bar{\sigma}(e_{1})
plus an error that vanishes as
\alpha\to 0
, so we are done.
∎

To conclude this appendix, it only remains to prove Proposition
11
.

Proof of Proposition
11
.

First, for any
\gamma>0
, define
M^{*}_{\gamma}
by

M^{*}_{\gamma}=\sup\left\{|X_{\mu}-\bar{X}|\,\mid\,\mu\leq\gamma\right\}.

This is measurable since
\gamma\mapsto X_{\gamma}
is continuous on an event of probability one.
4
4

4

Technically, since we have not specified that
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is complete, we should define
M^{*}_{\gamma}
more precisely by setting
M_{\gamma}^{*}=\sup\left\{|X_{\mu}-\bar{X}|\,\mid\,\mu\in\mathbb{Q}\cap(0,\gamma]\right\}
, let
E\in\mathcal{F}
be an event of probability one on which
\gamma\mapsto X_{\gamma}
is continuous, and then restrict attention in the rest of the proof to the almost-sure event
\cap_{k\in\mathbb{Z}^{d}}\tau_{k}^{-1}(E)
.
By assumption,
M^{*}_{\gamma}\to 0
almost surely as
\gamma\to 0
. Thus, by Egorov’s Theorem applied to the sequence
\{M_{n^{-1}}^{*}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
, for any
\delta>0
, there is an event
E_{\delta}\in\mathcal{F}
and a modulus of continuity
\omega_{\delta}:[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
such that
\mathbb{P}(E_{\delta})\geq 1-\delta
and

\sup_{E_{\delta}}M^{*}_{\gamma}\leq\omega_{\delta}(\gamma).

Observe that we can write

\displaystyle\left(L^{-1}R\right)^{1-d}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}

\displaystyle|X_{\gamma}\circ\tau_{k}-\bar{X}|\leq\left(L^{-1}R\right)^{1-d}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}M_{\gamma}^{*}\circ\tau_{k}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\omega_{\delta}(\gamma)\left(L^{-1}R\right)^{1-d}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}\mathbf{1}_{E_{\delta}}\circ\tau_{k}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+2C_{X}\left(L^{-1}R\right)^{1-d}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega\setminus E_{\delta}}\circ\tau_{k}.

At this stage, we apply the ergodic theorem, although we apply it to the (not necessarily ergodic) group action
\{\tau_{k}\}_{k\in\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}}
. Let
\mathscr{I}_{L}
denote the
\sigma
-algebra of sets invariant under this action:

\mathscr{I}_{L}=\{E\in\mathcal{F}\,\mid\,\tau_{k}^{-1}(E)=E\,\,\text{for each}\,\,k\in\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1}\}.

By the ergodic theorem (see, e.g.,
[
4
, Section 1.2]
or
[
49
, Appendix B]
),

\displaystyle\lim_{R\to\infty}(L^{-1}R)^{1-d}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega\setminus E_{\delta}}\circ\tau_{k}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\Omega\setminus E_{\delta}}\mid\mathscr{I}_{L}]\quad\text{almost surely.}

Since
\omega_{\delta}
is a modulus of continuity, we deduce that, for any
\delta>0
, with probability one,

(124)

\displaystyle\limsup_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to(0,0)}(L^{-1}R)^{1-d}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}|X_{\gamma}\circ\tau_{k}-\bar{X}|

\displaystyle\leq 2C_{X}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\Omega\setminus E_{\delta}}\mid\mathscr{I}_{L}].

At the same time, since
\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\Omega\setminus E_{\delta}}\mid\mathscr{I}_{L}]]=\mathbb{P}(\Omega\setminus E_{\delta})\leq\delta
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{\delta\to 0}\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\Omega\setminus E_{\delta}}\mid\mathscr{I}_{L}]=0\quad\text{in probability.}

Thus, since the left-hand side of (
124
) is independent of
\delta
, we conclude

\displaystyle\limsup_{(R^{-1},\gamma)\to(0,0)}(L^{-1}R)^{1-d}\sum_{k\in(\{0\}\times L\mathbb{Z}^{d-1})\cap Q_{R}}|X_{\gamma}\circ\tau_{k}-\bar{X}|=0\quad\text{almost surely.}

∎

Finally, we comment on the case when
a
and
\theta
are both either
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic or
B^{\infty}
functions.

Remark 10
.

The above proof generalizes without fanfare to the case when
a
and
\theta
are both either
\mathbb{Z}^{d}
-periodic or
B^{\infty}
functions, as in Corollary
2
. In that case, it is not necessary to treat the
x_{0}=0
and
x_{0}\neq 0
cases separately. In the
x_{0}=0
stage of the proof, one should simply work with the cylinders
x_{0}+\mathcal{C}(R,h)
and
x_{0}+\mathcal{C}_{L}(R,h)
directly.

In lieu of the ergodic theorem, one needs to show that, for any
L\in\mathbb{N}
, there is a uniform limit in this context:

\lim_{\gamma\to 0}\sup\left\{|m(\mathscr{F}_{\gamma},x+\mathcal{C}(L,h),q)-m(\overline{\mathscr{F}},\mathcal{C}(L,h),q)|\,\mid\,x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\right\}=0.

This can be proved by mimicking the argument in the proof of Proposition
10
above. The details are left to the reader.

Appendix B
\Gamma
-Convergence in Probability

In this section, we complete the proof of Theorem
1
by showing that our analysis of the planar cell problem (
22
) implies
\Gamma
-convergence. This will build on the approach in
[
2
,
49
,
45
]
, the only novelty being we will consider convergence in probability in addition to almost-sure convergence. Where possible, we follow
[
2
]
and
[
49
]
.

Before continuing, the reader may want to recall the abbreviation (
18
) that we use to paraphrase the energy in (
23
) from now on.

B.1.
Assumptions in this Appendix

Throughout this appendix, we assume that the medium
(a,\theta)
satisfies the assumptions of Section
1.1
, but concerning the potential well
W
, we only assume that it is a nonnegative continuous function, that
\{u\in\mathbb{R}\,\mid\,W(u)=0\}=\{-1,1\}
, and that the (super)quadratic growth assumption (
4
) holds.

B.2.
Subsequential Limits of the Cell Problems

To begin the section, we observe that, after possibly passing to a subsequence of scales
(\epsilon_{j})
, it is always possible to get almost-sure convergence in the cell problems studied in Section
3
, independently of the direction. This is the main probabilistic ingredient used in the proof, which is tantamount to the well-known fact that convergence in probability is equivalent to subsequential almost-sure convergence. The precise assertion is made in the next proposition.

First, we need a bit of notation. Recall that
\{e_{1},\dots,e_{d}\}
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
denotes the standard orthonormal basis in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Rather than varying the basis as in
[
49
]
, it is convenient to instead work with orthogonal transformations. Toward that end, let
O(d)
denote the family of all orthogonal
d\times d
-matrices. Given
\mathcal{O}\in O(d)
,
R>0
, and
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we define the (tilted) cube
Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x)
via the following procedure: first, define an orthogonal coordinate system on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
via the rule

y=\sum_{i=1}^{d}y_{i}^{\mathcal{O}}\mathcal{O}(e_{i}),\quad y_{i}^{\mathcal{O}}=y\cdot\mathcal{O}(e_{i}),

then this is the cube determined by

Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x)=\left\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,\mid\,|y_{i}^{\mathcal{O}}-x_{i}^{\mathcal{O}}|<\frac{R}{2}\right\}.

Equivalently, if
Q_{R}(x)
is defined as in (
100
), then

Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x)=\mathcal{O}(Q_{R}(\mathcal{O}^{-1}x)).

Furthermore, we will write

\displaystyle q^{\mathcal{O}}(x)\coloneqq q(\mathcal{O}(e_{1})\cdot x).

Here is the result concerning subsequential limits of the cell problems.

Proposition 12
.

Fix a positively one-homogeneous convex function
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
. Suppose that the scale
\epsilon\mapsto\delta(\epsilon)
has been chosen in such a way that, for any
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)\times O(d)
and any
\nu>0
,

(125)

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}\{|m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon^{-1}(\cdot-x)))-\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))R^{d-1}|>\nu\}=0.

Then, given any sequence
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,\infty)
such that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
, there is a subsequence
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that
j_{k}\to\infty
as
k\to\infty
and, with probability one, for any
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)\times O(d)
,

(126)

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x)))=\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))R^{d-1}.

Since the set
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)\times O(d)
appearing above is uncountable, it will be convenient to employ a countable, dense approximation.

Proof of Proposition
12
.

We split the proof in two steps. First, we we establish that (
126
) holds for all
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathscr{E}
in a countable dense subset
\mathscr{E}\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)\times O(d)
. Afterwards we use the continuity of
m
and
\sigma
to conclude almost-sure convergence.

Step 1 (Almost-sure convergence on a dense subset).
Since
\mathscr{E}
is countable, let us enumerate it in the form of a sequence:

\mathscr{E}=\{(x_{N},R_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}.

By (
125
), for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, we can fix a
j_{k}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
j_{k}\geq k
and

\displaystyle\sum_{n=1}^{k}\mathbb{P}\left\{\left|m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}_{n}}_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),q^{\mathcal{O}_{n}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x_{n})))-\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))R_{n}^{d-1}\right|>2^{-k}\right\}\leq 2^{-k}.

In particular, by the union bound,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\left(\bigcup_{n=1}^{k}\left\{\left|m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}_{n}}_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),q^{\mathcal{O}_{n}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x_{n})))-\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))R_{n}^{d-1}\right|>2^{-k}\right\}\right)\leq 2^{-k}.

Thus, the Borel-Cantelli Lemma implies there is a random variable
K\in\mathbb{N}
such that
\mathbb{P}\{K<\infty\}=1
and, with probability one, for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and any
k\geq\max\{N,K\}
, we have

\left|m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}_{N}}_{R_{N}}(x_{N}),q^{\mathcal{O}_{N}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x_{N})))-\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))R_{N}^{d-1}\right|\leq 2^{-k}.

Of course, since
\mathscr{E}=\{(x_{N},R_{N},\mathcal{O}_{N})\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
, this proves (
126
) for
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathscr{E}
.

Step 2 (Upgrade to almost-sure convergence).
By Step 1, there is an event of probability one on which, for any
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathscr{E}
, the pointwise limit (
126
) holds. It only remains to extend this to arbitrary parameters
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathscr{E}
.

Fix
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathscr{E}
. Choose a sequence
\{(x_{n},R_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{D}}
such that

(i)

R_{n}<R
for each
n
,

(ii)

Q^{\mathcal{O}_{n}}_{R_{n}}(x_{n})\subset\subset Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x)
for each
n
, and

(iii)

(x_{n},R_{n},\mathcal{O}_{n})\to(x,R,\mathcal{O})
as
n\to\infty
.

Arguing using the fundamental estimate as in
[
49
, Proof of Proposition 10]
, we deduce that

\displaystyle\liminf_{n\to\infty}\liminf_{k\to\infty}\Big(

\displaystyle m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}_{n}}_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),q^{\mathcal{O}_{n}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x_{n})))

\displaystyle\quad-m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x)))\Big)\geq 0.

Thus, by Step 1,

\limsup_{k\to\infty}m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x)))\leq\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))R^{d-1}.

To conclude, choose a sequence
\{(y_{n},S_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathscr{E}_{\mathcal{D}}
such that

(i)

S_{n}>R
for each
n
,

(ii)

Q^{\mathcal{U}_{n}}_{S_{n}}(y_{n})\supset\supset Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x)
for each
n
, and

(ii)

(y_{n},S_{n},\mathcal{U}_{n})\to(x,R,\mathcal{O})
as
n\to\infty

Once again, we argue as in
[
49
]
. First, fix
n
. For any
k
, choose
u_{k}
such that

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u_{k};Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x))=m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x))),

\displaystyle u_{k}-q((\cdot-x)\cdot\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))\in H^{1}_{0}(Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x)).

It is convenient to extend
u_{k}
so that
u_{k}(y)=q((y-x)\cdot\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))
for each
y\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x)
. Applying the fundamental estimate in a manner similar to
[
49
, Proof of Proposition 10]
, we obtain a sequence
\{E_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\liminf_{k\to\infty}m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{U}_{n}}_{R_{n}}(x_{n}),q^{\mathcal{U}_{n}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x)))\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u_{k};Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x))+E_{n},

and
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}E_{n}=0
.
In particular, after sending
n\to\infty
, we deduce that, on the event
\hat{\Omega}
, we have

\displaystyle\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))R^{d-1}

\displaystyle\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u_{k};Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x))

\displaystyle=\liminf_{k\to\infty}m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x))).

∎

B.3.
Subsequential
\Gamma
-Convergence

Next, we show that subsequential convergence of the energy in the planar cell problems implies subsequential
\Gamma
-convergence. This part of the argument is completely deterministic, hence we emphasize that the results are stated for a fixed realization of the medium
(a,\theta)
.

Proposition 13
.

Let
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
be a positively one-homogeneous convex function and fix a realization of the medium
(a,\theta)
. Suppose that
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}},(\delta_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,\infty)
are sequences such that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
and, for any
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)\times O(d)

(127)

\lim_{j\to\infty}m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta(\epsilon_{j})},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon_{j}^{-1}(\cdot-x)))=\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))R^{d-1}.

Then, for any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
u\in L^{1}(U)
,

\Gamma\text{-}\lim_{j\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta_{j}}(u;U)=\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}(u;U),

where
\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}
is the functional

(128)

\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}(u;U)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\int_{U\cap\partial^{*}\{u=1\}}\sigma(\nu_{\{u=1\}}(\xi))\,\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(d\xi),&\text{if}\,\,u\in BV(U;\{-1,1\}),\\
+\infty,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

This proposition, which is implicit already in
[
49
]
, improves the corresponding result in
[
2
]
. In particular, in
[
2
, Theorem 3.7]
, it is necessary to first check a translation-invariance condition, whereas here we bypass this extra step.

We expect that the proposition could also be proved following the arguments in
[
45
]
.

The proof of Proposition
13
will use the following compactness result from
[
2
]
.

Theorem 6

(Theorem 3.3 and 3.5 in
[
2
]
)
.

Fix a realization of the medium
(a,\theta)
. Given any sequence
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
, there is a subsequence
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
and a bounded Borel function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
, both of which may depend on
(a,\theta)
, such that, for any
u\in L^{1}(U)
and any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\Gamma\text{-}\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u;U)=\mathscr{E}_{\varphi}(u;U),

where
\mathscr{E}_{\varphi}
is the functional given by

\mathscr{E}_{\varphi}(u;U)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\int_{U\cap\partial^{*}\{u=1\}}\varphi(\xi,\nu_{\{u=1\}}(\xi))\,\mathcal{H}^{d-1}(d\xi),&\text{if}\,\,u\in BV(U;\{-1,1\}),\\
\infty,&\text{otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

For any
(x,\mathcal{O})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times O(d)
, the integrand
\varphi
is determined by the formula

(129)

\displaystyle\varphi(x,\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))=\limsup_{\varrho\to 0}\inf\left\{\frac{\mathscr{E}_{\varphi}(u;Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x))}{\varrho^{d-1}}\,\mid\,u=\chi_{\mathcal{O}(e_{1})}(\cdot-x)\,\,\text{in}\,\,Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x)^{c}\right\},

where
\chi_{\nu}
denotes the function equal to
1
on
\{x\cdot\nu\geq 0\}
and
-1
on
\{x\cdot\nu<0\}
.

Finally, here is the proof of Proposition
13
. Note that the proof is simpler than that of
[
49
, Theorem 1]
; in particular, Proposition 12 in that reference is much more than is needed here.

Proof of Proposition
13
.

To establish that
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta(\epsilon_{j})}\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}
as
j\to\infty
, it suffices to show that, for any subsequence
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
, we have that
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}
.

Fix a subsequence
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
. By Theorem
6
, there is a further subsequence along which
\Gamma
-convergence holds. Thus, up to relabelling, we can assume that there is a bounded Borel function
\varphi:\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\to[0,\infty)
such that, for any
u\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
and any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
,

\Gamma\text{-}\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u;U)=\mathscr{E}_{\varphi}(u;U)

We claim that (
127
) implies that
\varphi(x,e)=\sigma(e)
for each
(x,e)\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times S^{d-1}
, which is enough to complete the proof since then
\mathscr{E}_{\varphi}\equiv\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}
. We proceed by showing first that
\varphi(x,e)\leq\sigma(e)
and then by showing that
\varphi(x,e)\geq\sigma(e)
.

Fix
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
e\in S^{d-1}
. Let
\mathcal{O}\in O(d)
be such that
e=\mathcal{O}(e_{1})
. By (
129
), to establish that
\varphi(x,e)\leq\sigma(e)
, it suffices to show that, for any
\varrho>0

(130)

\inf\left\{\mathscr{E}_{\varphi}(u;Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x))\,\mid\,u=\chi_{\mathcal{O}(e_{1})}(\cdot-x)\,\,\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x)\right\}\leq\sigma(\mathcal{O}(e_{1}))\varrho^{d-1}.

Toward that end, for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
, fix a
u_{k}\in H^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};[-1,1])
such that

(131)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u_{k};Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x))=m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x))),

\displaystyle u_{k}=q(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x))\quad\text{in}\,\,\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x).

Observe that, for any bounded open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
, there holds

\displaystyle\sup\left\{\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u_{k};U)\,\mid\,k\in\mathbb{N}\right\}<\infty.

Thus, up to passing to yet another subsequence, we can assume that there is a
u\in BV_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d};\{-1,1\})
such that

\displaystyle u=\lim_{k\to\infty}u_{k}\quad{\rm in}~L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

By
\Gamma
-convergence, this implies

\displaystyle\mathscr{E}_{\varphi}(u;Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x))\leq\liminf_{k\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u_{k};Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x)).

Due to the boundary conditions imposed on
u_{k}
outside of
Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x)
, we have that
u=\chi_{\mathcal{O}(e_{1})}(\cdot-x)
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}\setminus Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{\varrho}(x)
. Thus, in view of (
131
) and (
127
), the bound (
130
) follows.

It only remains to establish the lower bound
\varphi(x,e)\geq\sigma(e)
. This half of the proof follows by arguing exactly as in
[
49
, Proof of Theorem 1]
