(which, in particular, does not use Proposition 12 of that reference), hence the details are omitted. ∎

B.4.
The Metric
d_{\Gamma}

Now that the main subsequential convergence results are proved, we turn to the task of providing a suitable definition for
\Gamma
-convergence in probability. In what follows, we fix a
c_{*}>0
such that
W(u)\geq c_{*}^{2}|u|^{p}
if
|u|\geq 2
, which is possible by (
4
), and let
\Phi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
be the function determined by

(132)

\Phi(0)=0,\quad\Phi^{\prime}(u)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{r l}\sqrt{W(u)},&\text{if}\,\,|u|\leq 2,\\
c_{*}|u|^{\frac{p}{2}},&\text{if}\,\,|u|>2.\\
\end{array}\right.

Notice that
\Phi
is increasing and surjective and
\Phi^{\prime}(u)\leq\sqrt{W(u)}
for each
u\in\mathbb{R}
. We argue that there exists a metric
d_{\Gamma}
on the space of functionals

(133)

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}=\left\{\mathscr{F}:L^{1}(U)\rightarrow[0,\infty]~{\rm l.s.c.}~\middle|~\frac{1}{c}\mathscr{F}(u)\geq\int_{U}|\nabla\Phi(u)|+\int_{U}|u|^{p}-2^{p}|U|\right\},

so that convergence in
(\mathcal{M},d_{\Gamma})
is equivalent to
\Gamma
-convergence (induced by the strong
L^{1}(U)
topology). In (
133
),
c>0
denotes some constant. Recall the following “BV trick” (cf.
[
47
, Proof of Proposition 3]
), which follows from the lower bound (
4
) on
W
and Young’s inequality:

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}(u,U)

\displaystyle\gtrsim_{\lambda,\theta_{*},W}\int_{U}\sqrt{W(u)}|\nabla u|\geq\int_{U}|\nabla\Phi(u)|.

In addition, for any
\epsilon\leq 1
, the assumption (
4
) implies
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}(u,U)\geq\int_{U}W(u)\gtrsim_{W}\int_{U}|u|^{p}-2^{p}|U|
. Combining these two lower bounds, we deduce that

(134)

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}(u,U)\gtrsim_{\lambda,\theta_{*},W}\int_{U}|\nabla\Phi(u)|+\int_{U}|u|^{p}-2^{p}|U|

so that in fact
\mathbb{P}\{\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}\in\mathcal{M}\,\,\text{for each}\,\,\epsilon\leq 1\,\,\text{and}\,\,\delta>0\}=1
for an appropriate choice of
c
.

In the next lemma, we note that the functional in the lower bound (
133
) has compact sublevel sets. Hence this functional is equicoercive in the sense of
[
23
, Definition 7.6]
.

Lemma 10
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, the set
F(t)\subseteq L^{1}(U)
given by

\displaystyle F(t)=\left\{u\in L^{1}(U)~\middle|~\int_{U}|\nabla\Phi(u)|+\int_{U}|u|^{p}\leq t+2^{p}|U|\right\}

is compact in
L^{1}(U)
.

Proof.

We first observe that by the definition (
132
),
\Phi
is strictly increasing and surjective, hence the inverse
\Phi^{-1}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
exists and is continuous. Further, the definition of
\Phi
implies that
|\Phi(u)|\lesssim|u|^{\frac{p}{2}+1}
, that is,

(135)

\displaystyle\limsup_{|u|\to\infty}|u|^{-(\frac{p}{2}+1)}|\Phi(u)|<\infty.

We conclude by showing that if
(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq F(t)
, then it has a subsequence that converges in
L^{1}(U)
and pointwise almost everywhere. From the definition of
F(t)
, we directly see that
(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
L^{p}(U)
, so that
(|u_{k}|^{\frac{p}{2}+1})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
L^{1}(U)
by our assumption that
p\geq 2
. In view of (
135
), this implies that
(\Phi(u_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is bounded in
L^{1}(U)
. Combined with
\int_{U}|\nabla\Phi(u_{k})|\lesssim 1
and the compactness of the embedding
BV(U)\subseteq L^{1}(U)
, we learn from this that
(\Phi(u_{k}))_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact in
L^{1}(U)
. Therefore, it has a subsequence
(\Phi(u_{k_{j}}))_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
that converges to some function
w\in L^{1}(U)
. By the continuity of
\Phi^{-1}
, the boundedness of
(u_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
L^{p}(U)
, and Vitali’s Theorem, this implies that
(u_{k_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
converges to
\Phi^{-1}(w)
in
L^{1}(U)
. Finally, by lower semicontinuity,
\Phi^{-1}(w)\in F(t)
. ∎

Due to the above equicoercivity, we can invoke
[
23
, Theorem 10.22]
: To this end, let us recall the Yosida-Moreau transform of some functional
\mathscr{F}\in\mathcal{M}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}^{\alpha,\lambda}(u)\coloneqq\inf\left\{\mathscr{F}(v)+\lambda\|u-v\|_{L^{1}(U)}^{\alpha}~\middle|~v\in L^{1}(U)\right\}

for
u\in L^{1}(U)
, cf.
[
23
, Definition 9.8]
. Using this, we define the metric

(136)

\displaystyle d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F},\mathscr{G})\coloneqq\sum_{i,j\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{2^{i+j}}|\arctan(\mathscr{F}^{1,j}(u_{i}))-\arctan(\mathscr{G}^{1,j}(u_{i}))|

for a dense subset
(u_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subset L^{1}(U)
.

Proposition 14

(Theorem 10.22 in
[
23
]
)
.

Definition (
136
) yields a metric
d_{\Gamma}
on
\mathcal{M}
such that
(\mathcal{M},d_{\Gamma})
is a compact metric space and for any sequence
(\mathscr{F}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\mathscr{F}_{k}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Gamma}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathscr{F}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{k},\mathscr{F})\rightarrow 0.

as
k\rightarrow\infty
.

We will rely on the fact that
\Gamma
-convergence holds if and only if it holds along any subsequence. Toward that end, of course, it helps to be precise about the definition of
\Gamma
-convergence from the very beginning. Since standard texts such as
[
14
,
23
]
define
\Gamma
-convergence in terms of sequences, for the sake of completeness, let us give a precise definition of
\Gamma
-convergence of the one-parameter family
(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)})_{\epsilon>0}
.

Definition 1
.

The functionals
\Gamma\text{-}\liminf\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}
and
\Gamma\text{-}\limsup\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}
are defined, for any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any
u\in L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})

\displaystyle\Gamma\text{-}\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;U)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\inf_{v\in L^{1}(U)\,:\,\|v-u\|_{L^{1}(U)}\leq r}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}(v;U),

\displaystyle\Gamma\text{-}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(u;U)

\displaystyle=\lim_{r\to 0}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\inf_{v\in L^{1}(U)\,:\,\|v-u\|_{L^{1}(U)}\leq r}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta}(v;U).

In particular, we say that
(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(\cdot\,;U))_{\epsilon>0}

\Gamma
-converges to a functional
\mathscr{G}
as
\epsilon\to 0
, written
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(\cdot\,;U)\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\mathcal{G}
, if the following equality holds:

\mathscr{G}=\Gamma\text{-}\liminf_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(\cdot\,;U)=\Gamma\text{-}\limsup_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(\cdot\,;U)\quad\text{in}\,\,L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

The next lemma asserts that the above definition is equivalent to subsequential
\Gamma
-convergence along an arbitrary subsequence.

Lemma 11
.

Give any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any lower semi-continuous functional
\mathcal{G}
on
L^{1}(U)
, we have that

\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon,\delta(\epsilon)}(\cdot\,;U)\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\mathcal{G}\quad\text{as}\quad\epsilon\to 0

if and only if, for any sequence
(\epsilon_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(0,\infty)
for which
\epsilon_{j}\to 0
as
j\to\infty
, we have

\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j},\delta(\epsilon_{j})}(\cdot\,;U)\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\mathcal{G}.

Since the proof follows in a more-or-less routine way from Definition
1
, the details are left to the reader.

B.5.
Convergence in Probability

We showed above that the
\Gamma
-convergence problem of interest to us can be understood via convergence in some abstract metric space
(\mathcal{M},d_{\Gamma})
. Here we recall the corresponding notion of convergence in probability for
(\mathcal{M},d_{\Gamma})
-valued random variables.

Denote by
\mathcal{B}
the
\sigma
-algebra generated by the metric topology on
(\mathcal{M},d_{\Gamma})
. Furthermore, let
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
be a probability space. A map
X:\Omega\to\mathcal{M}
is called an
(\mathcal{M},d_{\Gamma})
-valued random variable if, for any
A\in\mathcal{B}
, we have that
X^{-1}(A)\in\mathcal{F}
.

Definition 2
.

Given
(\mathcal{M},d_{\Gamma})
-valued random variables
(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
and
\mathscr{F}
(on some common probability space), we say that
(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
converges in probability to
\mathscr{F}
as
\epsilon\to 0
provided that, for any
\nu>0
,

\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\mathbb{P}\{d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon},\mathscr{F})>\nu\}=0.

We will take advantage of the fact that, in metric spaces, convergence is determined by subsequences.

Proposition 15
.

Let
(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
and
\mathscr{F}
be
(\mathcal{M},d_{\Gamma})
-valued random variables. The following are equivalent:

(1)

It holds
d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon},\mathscr{F})\to 0
in probability as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

(2)

Every subsequence
(\mathscr{F}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
has a further subsequence
(\mathscr{F}_{k_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
d_{\Gamma}(\mathscr{F}_{k_{j}},\mathscr{F})\to 0
almost surely as
j\rightarrow\infty
.

(3)

Every subsequence
(\mathscr{F}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
has a further subsequence
(\mathscr{F}_{k_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathscr{F}_{k_{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Gamma}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathscr{F}
almost surely as
j\rightarrow\infty
.

In particular, if along every subsequence
(\mathscr{F}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
there is a further subsequence
(\mathscr{F}_{k_{j}})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\mathscr{F}_{k_{j}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Gamma}}{{\rightarrow}}\mathscr{F}
almost surely as
j\to\infty
, then
(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
converges to
\mathscr{F}
in probability (w.r.t.
d_{\Gamma}
) as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
.

Proof.

Note that by the very definition, (1) is equivalent to the fact that every subsequence
(\mathscr{F}_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon})_{\epsilon>0}
converges in probability (w.r.t.
d_{\Gamma}
) to
\mathscr{F}
. The equivalence to (2) and (3) follows from Lemma 5.2 in
[
42
]
and Proposition
14
.
∎

B.6.
\Gamma
-Convergence in Probability: Proof of Theorem
4

Finally, to complete the proof of Theorem
1
, we establish that if convergence in probability holds at the level of the planar cell problems, then
\Gamma
-convergence holds in probability.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

The proof amounts to a concatenation of what has been proved so far in this section. The details are provided for the reader’s convenience.

In view of Proposition
15
, we only need to show that, given any bounded Lipschitz open set
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
and any sequence
(\epsilon_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
\epsilon_{k}\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, there is a subsequence
(k_{j})_{j\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that the limit
\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{k_{j}},\delta(\epsilon_{k_{j}})}(\cdot\,;U)\overset{\Gamma}{\to}\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}(\cdot\,;U)
holds with probability one as
j\to\infty
.

Toward that end, notice that the assumption (
22
) implies that the hypotheses of Proposition
12
hold. Therefore, that proposition implies that there is a subsequence
(j_{k})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}
such that, with probability one, for any
(x,R,\mathcal{O})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times(0,\infty)\times O(d)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}m(\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})},Q^{\mathcal{O}}_{R}(x),q^{\mathcal{O}}(\epsilon_{j_{k}}^{-1}(\cdot-x)))=\sigma(e)R^{d-1}.

This in turn implies, by Proposition
13
, that, with probability one,

\Gamma\text{-}\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathscr{F}_{\epsilon_{j_{k}},\delta(\epsilon_{j_{k}})}(u;U)=\mathscr{E}_{\sigma}(u;U)

for any
u\in L^{1}(U)
. ∎
